rayadas y sus quejas. Son muchas, muchas personas por las que, sin duda, he
llegado hasta aquí. Y espero que en estas páginas pueda expresar aunque sea una

que les agradezco todo lo que han compartido conmigo, lo mucho que me han
ayudado, lo mucho que me han apoyado, lo mucho que me han querido. Y también

   Resum
    La difusió en grafs és una eina fonamental per modelitzar sistemes complexos,
des del transport i l’ecologia fins a la sociologia i la neurociència. En la formulació
clàssica, la difusió en grafs ve donada pel Laplacià combinatori i pressuposa inter-
accions amb veïns directes i conservació de la massa. Aquesta tesi amplia aquest
marc mitjançant el desenvolupament i l’anàlisi de famílies d’operadors basats en
el Laplacià clàssic que incorporen tres característiques habituals en aplicacions
reals: biaixos dependents de la topologia, influències de llarg abast i guany o pèr-
dua de massa. L’objectiu és mantenir models interpretables i matemàticament
analitzables, alhora que s’amplia el ventall de comportaments dinàmics que es
poden descriure.
    En primer lloc, introduïm Laplacians amb biaix cap a o contra nodes molt
connectats i la seva generalització a Laplacians amb biaix per centralitat, que
modifiquen les probabilitats de transició en funció del grau del node o d’altres
mesures de centralitat. Aquests operadors permeten, de manera sistemàtica, afa-
vorir o penalitzar nodes d’alt grau i controlar la velocitat de convergència a través
de propietats espectrals. A més, els seus operadors adjunts esdevenen dinàmiques
conservatives advectives en xarxes no dirigides i condueixen a estats estacionaris
heterogenis, estretament lligats a l’estructura de la xarxa. En segon lloc, estudiem
la difusió no local mediant Laplacians d-path i les seves transformades, tot con-
nectant els salts de llarg abast en grafs amb idees pròpies de la difusió fraccionària.
Les transformades de Mellin d’aquests operadors permeten obtenir règims su-
perdifusius en grafs. En tercer lloc, analitzem la difusió no conservativa basada
en els operadors de Lerman-Ghosh i proposem models logístics de reacció-difusió
que eviten tant el creixement explosiu com l’extinció, i que donen lloc a equilibris
acotats dependents de la xarxa i a estats de consens heterogenis.
    Aquests desenvolupaments teòrics s’acompanyen d’aplicacions: un model
d’advecció-difusió per al moviment del lèmur de cua anellada en un ecosistema
fragmentat, l’efecte del biaix per grau sobre la sincronització explosiva en xarxes
de Kuramoto i experiments controlats sobre la influència social indirecta en l’adopció
d’innovacions. Així mateix, apliquem el marc no conservatiu a sistemes multia-
gent i a models d’interacció ecològica. Finalment, introduïm models de metaplex
que combinen difusió contínua dins de cada node amb transport entre nodes a
través de la xarxa, i els apliquem a escenaris inspirats en el cervell amb difusió
en metaplexes simplicials, tot destacant les diferències amb Laplacians d’ordre
superior i el paper de la geometria interna.
    Resumen
    La difusión en grafos es una herramienta fundamental para modelizar sis-
temas complejos, desde el transporte y la ecología hasta la sociología y la neuro-
ciencia. En su formulación clásica, la difusión en grafos viene dada por el Lapla-
ciano combinatorio y supone interacciones con vecinos directos y conservación
de la masa. Esta tesis amplía ese marco mediante el desarrollo y el análisis de
familias de operadores basados en el Laplaciano clásico que incorporan tres car-
acterísticas habituales en aplicaciones reales: sesgos dependientes de la topología,
influencias de largo alcance y ganancia o pérdida de masa. El objetivo es mantener
modelos interpretables y analizables matemáticamente, a la vez que se amplía el
abanico de comportamientos dinámicos que pueden describirse.
    En primer lugar, introducimos Laplacianos con sesgo hacia o contra nodos
muy conectados y su generalización a Laplacianos con sesgo por centralidad, que
modifican las probabilidades de transición en función del grado del nodo u otras
medidas de centralidad. Estos operadores permiten, de forma sistemática, favore-
cer o penalizar nodos de alto grado y controlar la velocidad de convergencia a
través de propiedades espectrales. Además, sus operadores adjuntos dan lugar a
dinámicas conservativas advectivas en redes no dirigidas y conducen a estados
estacionarios heterogéneos, estrechamente ligados a la estructura de la red. En
segundo lugar, estudiamos la difusión no local mediante Laplacianos d-path y sus
transformadas, conectando los saltos de largo alcance en grafos con ideas propias
de la difusión fraccionaria. Las transformadas de Mellin de estos operadores per-
miten obtener regímenes superdifusivos en grafos. En tercer lugar, analizamos
la difusión no conservativa basada en los operadores de Lerman-Ghosh y pro-
ponemos modelos logísticos de reacción-difusión que evitan tanto el crecimiento
explosivo como la extinción, dando lugar a equilibrios acotados que dependen de
la red y a estados de consenso heterogéneos.
    Estas teorías se acompañan de aplicaciones: un modelo de advección-difusión
para el movimiento del lémur de cola anillada en un ecosistema, el efecto del sesgo
sobre la sincronización explosiva en redes y experimentos controlados sobre la
influencia social indirecta en la adopción de innovaciones. También aplicamos
el marco no conservativo a sistemas multiagente e interacciones ecológicas. Por
último, introducimos los metaplexes que combinan difusión continua dentro de
cada nodo con transporte entre nodos, y los aplicamos a escenarios inspirados en
el cerebro con difusión en metaplexes simpliciales, destacando las diferencias con
Laplacianos de orden superior y el papel de la geometría interna.
    Abstract
    Diffusion on graphs is a central modeling tool for complex systems, from
transport and ecology to social dynamics and neuroscience. In its classical form,
graph diffusion is driven by the combinatorial Laplacian and assumes nearest-
neighbor interactions and mass conservation. This thesis extends that setting by
developing and analyzing families of Laplacian-based operators that incorporate
three features frequently required in applications: topology-dependent biases,
long-range couplings, and nonconservative gain and loss. The aim is to keep
models interpretable and mathematically analyzable, while enlarging the range
of dynamical behaviors that can be represented.
    First, we introduce hub-biased Laplacians and their generalization to centrality-
biased Laplacians, which modify transition rates according to node degree or
other centrality measures. These operators provide a principled way to favor
or penalize hubs and to tune convergence rates through spectral properties, while
their adjoint counterparts generate conservative advection-like dynamics on undi-
rected networks and yield heterogeneous steady states linked to the underlying
structure. Second, we study nonlocal diffusion through d-path Laplacians and
their transforms, connecting graph-based long-range hopping with ideas from
fractional diffusion. Mellin-transformed versions of these operators make it pos-
sible to achieve superdiffusive regimes on graphs, with heavy-tailed spreading.
Third, we analyse nonconservative diffusion based on the Lerman-Ghosh opera-
tors and propose logistic reaction-diffusion models that prevent blow-up and ex-
tinction, producing bounded, network-dependent equilibria and heterogeneous
consensus states.
    These theoretical developments are complemented with applications: advection-
diffusion modeling of ring-tailed lemur movement in a fragmented landscape,
degree-biased effects on explosive synchronization in Kuramoto networks, and
laboratory experiments on indirect social influence in innovation adoption. We
also apply the non-conservative framework to multi-agent systems and ecological
interaction models. Finally, we introduce metaplex models that couple continu-
ous intra-node diffusion with inter-node network transport, and we apply them to
brain-inspired simplicial-metaplex diffusion, highlighting differences with higher-
order Laplacians and the role of internal geometry.

2.13 Example of a simplicial complex representing a group contagion process from [147].
A susceptible individual can be affected by an infected one with probability β. If there is
a group interaction of three people, meaning that they form a simplex, then the proba-
bility of infection is not two independent events with probability β, but includes a third
independent event with probability β∆ . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 30

of the cube and the edges are the rotations of the faces (only allowed 180◦ turns). The
green lines represent the rotation of the frontal face, the blue ones are the upper face
rotation while the red lines are the rotations of the lateral one [15]. . . . . . . . . . . . 34

3.1 Example of the weights of the edges for the value of α = 1/2. This (hubs-repelling)
Laplacian favors the less connected nodes, as we can see by  √ the width of the arrows in
the picture. Thus, a particle in the purple node will have 2 times more probability of
hoping to the red node than to the green ones. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 77
3.2 Transient dynamics of the diffusion process under different hubs biased Laplacian
for a simple graph. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 84
3.3 Comparison of the second smallest eigenvalue of the hubs biased Laplacian with
the classical one. The values of α are 20 points equidistributed in the interval [−5, 5],
the number of nodes of the random graphs in the vertical axis are 20 values equidis-
tributed between 10 and 1000. For each n, we compute 100 random graphs with the
same number of nodes and then we compute the second smallest eigenvalue of the hubs
biased Laplacian for each value of α and compare it with the second smallest eigenvalue
of the classical Laplacian. The color represents the proportion of these graphs that show
a greater second smallest eigenvalue for the hubs biased Laplacian than for the classical
one. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 85

10.6 Schematic illustration of the transformations of a metaplex into a simplicial com-
plex. A metaplex can be transformed into a fatgraph by considering its edges as nodes as
well, giving them spatial structure. This fatgraph could be transformed into a simplicial
metaplex by adding a node for each triangle with the corresponding spatial structure. In
the same way, a metaplex can be transformed into a simplicial metaplex by doing both
steps at the same time. Finally, a simplicial metaplex can be transformed into a 2-simplex
by erasing the spatial structure of all the nodes. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 248



10.7 Illustration of different metaplexes formed by 3-dimensional objects which are
connected to 2-dimensional ones through their boundary. The dynamical operators are
different in each of the objects due to their dimensions. a) A simplicial metaplex formed
by two parts K1 and K2 which consist of a 1-simplex and 2-simplex, respectively. The
connection is represented by the map F1,2 , which, according to Definition 2.29 acts as
a sink in K1 and as a source in K2 . b) A cube complex, which is a polytopal complex
in which Q2 and Q3 are cubes in 2- and 3-dimensions, respectively. c) An example of
CW-complex in which sets of nodes are covered by 2- and 3-dimensional balls B 2 and B 3
are connected by their boundary spheres. d) An example of manifold complex formed
by two interconnected manifolds (2- and 3-dimensional ones). . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 249
10.8 Representation of a simplicial complex consisting of 5 vertices V = {a, b, c, d, e, f }
forming a 3-simplex with vertex set V1 = {a, b, c, d} , a 2-simplex with vertex set V2 =
{a, d, e} , and two 1-simplices with vertex sets V3 = {a, f } , and V4 = {e, f }. We illus-
trate a particle that departs from vertex a (a 0-simplex) and performs a 1-dimensional
diffusion along the 1-simplex {a, b} which is a face of the triangle {a, b, c} belonging to
the 3-simplex. Because only transitions from Rm to Rm±1 inside the same simplex are
allowed, the particle hops to the 2-simplex {a, b, c} where it performs a 2-dimensional
random walk. From there it hops to the interior of the 3-simplex and performs a 3-
dimensional diffusion, emerging again to one of the faces of the tetrahedra, i.e., the face
{a, c, d}. The transition from this face can only be either back to the interior of the
tetrahedra or to any of the edges of the triangle {a, c, d}. Here the particle hops to the
1-simplex {a, d} from where it can navigate towards the 2-simplex {a, d, e} due to the
fact that {a, d} is a simplex shared by both the tetrahedra {a, b, c, d} and the trian-
gle {a, d, e} . From the triangle, it can hop to any of its edges, such as {a, e}, hopping
through a vertex to the edge {e, f }and ending its long walk at the 0-simplex f . . . 256

1.1   The art of Science
      Science, in its original and primary sense, is the human effort to understand na-
      ture: to see how the world works, to explain why things happen, and, in an animal
      instinctive pulse for survival, to say something about what may happen next. It
      is an old human desire: to move from surprise to knowledge, without losing the
      sense of wonder. If these predictions are what grant science its public victories,
      as they are reliable and useful, they are also its private discipline, because you
      cannot predict in a serious way unless you have really understood the roots of
      the phenomena, what links cause and effect. In that sense, science is not just a
      collection of facts, it is a way of looking at reality with patience, method, and a
      certain humility.
         To achieve such authority and trust, scientists built a complete set of methods,
      known today by any citizen since elementary school. It starts with observation:
2                                                        Chapter 1. Introduction

looking carefully, measuring, taking notes, repeating. It continues with experi-
ments, where we change one thing at a time and see what effects may persist. It
relies on numbers because they allow comparisons and reduce confusion. It also
uses theory: explanations that can be tested, corrected, and, when needed, re-
placed. And in recent decades, science has added more instruments: statistics to
learn from noisy data, computers to explore “what if” questions, and simulations
to test ideas when direct experiments are difficult, expensive, or impossible. Fi-
nally, this loop of observation, experimentation, theorization, and testing is kept
going, refining itself, correcting mistakes, and improving our understanding.
     A requirement for applying these methods is mathematics, which plays a spe-
cial role in science. Its characterization of nature is, in Wigner’s words, “unrea-
sonably effective”, thus making it the perfect ally for science. The physicist Lord
Kelvin put the practical side of it very directly: “When you can measure what
you are speaking about, and express it in numbers, you know something about
it.” Behind that sentence there is a whole scientific attitude: measurement forces
us to leave the comfort of vague words and commit to something that can be
checked, compared, and improved. Numbers make disagreements visible. They
also make progress possible, because they let different people, in different places
and times, talk about the same phenomenon without relying only on intuition.
Because of this, mathematics is not only a toolbox, it is also a language. Feynman
described it outright: “mathematics is not just a language. . . it’s like a language
plus logic.” That “plus logic” matters in many ways: mathematics is a science in
itself, a landscape to explore, because, once you accept a set of assumptions, you
cannot simply jump to the conclusion you like. You must follow the chain. This
is the perfect symbiosis for science: it is crucial, because reality often punishes
the smallest hidden mistake. Mathematics gives a kind of intellectual honesty: if
something does not follow, it will not work, no matter how much you believe in
it.
    Despite all this, perhaps the most impressive aspect of mathematics is that it
does more than describe, it connects. Poincaré captured this idea with a line that
almost defines abstraction itself: “Mathematics is the art of giving the same name
to different things.” It points to a deep reason why mathematics is so useful in sci-
ence: it allows us to recognize the same structure wearing different disguises. The
same kind of equation can reappear in heat flow and in diffusion, in waves and in
probability, in population change and in markets. Not because those phenomena
are identical, but because they share a common skeleton of relations. In that way,
mathematics becomes a bridge: it turns observation into quantities, quantities
1.1 The art of Science                                                             3

into relations, and relations into concepts that can travel from one problem to
another without losing precision.
    Unfortunately, these wide and surprising connections are not enough to deal
with the complexity of reality. Faced with the enormous variety present in nature,
science historically adopted a very powerful strategy: reductionism. Reduction-
ism is the idea that to understand a system we should break it into parts, study
the parts in isolation, and then rebuild the explanation from the bottom up. This
approach has been extremely successful. It is how physics found its laws, how
chemistry understood reactions and bonds, and how biology advanced through
genes, cells, enzymes, and molecules. Reductionism is, in a way, the art of making
a problem smaller so that the mind can hold it. It gives us mechanisms: concrete
answers to the question “how,” not just general descriptions of “what,” about the
phenomena happening.
    Its success is not an accident. When the parts have stable properties and
the interactions are simple enough, studying pieces separately gives deep under-
standing. It provides clean experiments and clear explanations. It helps avoid
the temptation of vague stories about “the whole” when we have not yet under-
stood the basics. In many cases, reductionism is not just useful, it is necessary.
Without it, science would often lose the solid ground that makes it different from
speculation.
    But with time, science discovered something important: natural occurrences
are not typically made up of a list of parts acting by themselves, but also of how
they influence each other. It is not that the parts do not matter, but that their or-
ganization and relations have to be taken into account too. A pile of bricks is not
a house. A group of musicians is not automatically an orchestra. At this point,
a sentence often associated with Aristotle becomes a helpful guide: “the whole
is more than the sum of its parts.” Even if that line reaches us through many
retellings, the intuition behind it is correct: once things are organized and con-
nected, new properties can appear that are not visible in the isolated ingredients.
    This is clear in the relationship between chemistry and biology. Living beings
are made of molecules, and molecules obey chemical laws. But “life” is not a
molecule. Life is an organized process: a network of reactions that keeps itself
going, repairs itself, and adapts. If you know all the chemical properties of the
molecules in a cell, you still need to understand how they work together as a
system: regulation, coordination, feedback, and the way information is stored
and used. Biology does not break chemistry, it builds on it. But it adds something
new: a level of organized behavior that requires its own concepts.
4                                                        Chapter 1. Introduction

     When we move to sociology, the same idea becomes even stronger. Humans
are biological organisms, but social life is not just biology multiplied. Here, some
old philosophical lines suddenly sound like early hints of what we would now
call a “holistic” viewpoint. Aristotle wrote that we are “political animals” (often
translated as social animals). In modern terms, one could twist it like this: a per-
son is not only an isolated unit, but also a node shaped by relations. Cicero’s “non
nobis solum nati sumus” (“we are not born for ourselves alone”) was meant as a
moral lesson, but it can also be read as a scientific hint: if we want to understand
society, we must study not only individuals, but also the ties between them (co-
operation, conflict, imitation, trust, power, . . . ). And the poet John Donne said it
in a line that is almost perfect for this discussion: “No man is an island.” These are
not scientific laws, but they point to a very scientific idea: many human outcomes
are created between people, not only inside people.
     This brings us to a key concept: emergence. Emergence means that the sys-
tem as a whole can show behaviors that are not possible to predict by looking at
the parts separately. This is not magic. It is not a violation of physics. It is what
happens when many elements interact and influence each other. Temperature is
a classic example: no single molecule “has” temperature, temperature is a collec-
tive property. A traffic jam can appear even if nobody wants it and no accident
happens, it emerges from many drivers reacting locally. A sudden wave of opin-
ion, a market panic, or a cultural trend can emerge from many small decisions
that affect one another.
     For this reason, complexity studies often repeat a phrase from the physicist
Philip Anderson: “More is different.” The idea is simple: when you have enough
interacting parts, new patterns appear, and you may need new concepts to de-
scribe them properly. Here fits a phrase often attributed to Heraclitus, “every-
thing flows”, which, even if it comes to us in fragments, expresses a key reasoning:
in an ever-changing world, holding to a unique, fixed philosophy of science is not
enough. Sometimes we need to adapt our methods and ideas to the phenomena
we want to understand.
     So the newer perspective does not reject reductionism. It completes it. It says:
the parts still matter, but relations matter too, mechanisms matter, but organiza-
tion matters too. Reductionism tells us what each piece can do. The complemen-
tary view asks what happens when pieces interact, organize, and create collective
behavior. If we only study the parts, we may miss the behavior of the whole. If
we only study the whole, we may lose the mechanisms that keep us honest.
     In the end, science advances by moving in both directions at once: inward,
      1.2 Graphs, dynamics, complexity                                                     5

      toward the pieces and their rules, and outward, toward the patterns that appear
      when many pieces act together. The first movement gives precision, the second
      gives meaning. Between them there is a kind of dialogue (sometimes calm, some-
      times tense) where we learn which details can be ignored, which relations can-
      not, and which descriptions are strong enough to travel from one level to another
      without breaking.


1.2   Graphs, dynamics, complexity
      When entering the world of complex systems, the aforementioned ideas of emer-
      gence and interactions become central. Hence, there are tools, areas of mathe-
      matics, that are a perfect match for dealing with such concepts. Two of the most
      important ones are graph theory and dynamical systems.
          Graph theory begins with a radical abstraction. A graph is a set of nodes
      and a set of edges. The nodes may represent neurons, cities, proteins, computers,
      animals, or people, the edges may represent contact, exchange, similarity, depen-
      dency, or communication. Much is erased, and that erasure is not a defect but a
      decision: the model does not aim to reproduce the whole system, but to preserve
      the architecture of influence, its essential constraints. This has also led, in some
      works, to a distinction between networks and graphs, where networks are seen as
      the real-world systems, and graphs as their mathematical representations. Here
      we will abstract once more and use both terms interchangeably.
          Yet structure alone is a still photograph. The systems that motivated this
      viewpoint are not only connected, they are active. They evolve, respond, accu-
      mulate, relax, amplify. Here dynamical systems enter not as a separate topic but
      as the natural partner of graphs. A dynamical description does not ask only what
      is connected to what, but what changes with time. Many of the properties we
      care about in complex systems are in fact properties of time evolution: stability
      and instability, memory and forgetting, robustness and fragility, coordination and
      competition.
          If graphs provide the canvas, dynamics provide the color. When the two are
      combined, we can relate theory to the observation of a wider range of situations.
      Studying dynamics on networks allows us to track the influence of the local in-
      teractions mediated by the network structure on the global behavior of the sys-
      tem. In this setting, each node carries a value (activity, load, belief, concentration,
      resource), and edges mediate couplings (exchange, constraint, influence, inhibi-
      tion). A useful model must decide which of these options counts as “state” in the
      6                                                         Chapter 1. Introduction

      first place, because, in network terms, what often matters is not the substance
      of the nodes, but the organization of relations and the transformation of signals,
      resources, or constraints as they pass through the web.
          Nonetheless, all of this comes with a methodological warning. Abstraction
      buys clarity by paying with omission. One should keep in mind Box’s practical
      aphorism: “Essentially, all models are wrong, but some are useful.” Modeling
      through graphs is exactly this kind of delicate bargain: it can be astonishingly
      predictive when relations dominate behavior, yet misleading when the erased
      details are the true drivers. The artistic face of this science is then to choose the
      level of description at which the essential constraints still act, and at which the
      remaining dynamics are simple enough to be simulated.
           But, related to this, there is a fear that any scientist should have, and that we
      can express through Alfred Korzybski’s warning: “the map is not the territory.”
      Broader models may capture the large-scale picture, but they inevitably lose de-
      tail. The danger is not in abstraction itself, but in forgetting that it remains an
      abstraction: a way of seeing certain aspects of reality while hiding others, a map
      rather than reality.
          All of these modeling choices affect the system’s outcomes, and they usu-
      ally bring us to a new question: are these collective patterns a consequence of
      the rules, or are they dictated by the architecture? Can they appear without the
      combination of both?


1.3   Diffusing through the network
      This thesis is focused on deepening our understanding of a particular family of
      processes that take place on networks: diffusion processes. Diffusion is a natural
      phenomenon based on the spread of a quantity from nodes with high concentra-
      tion to nodes with low concentration. Hence, it can be observed in many differ-
      ent contexts, from the evolution of heat in a solid material to the dissemination
      of information in social networks. The study of diffusion processes on networks
      is crucial for understanding how information, diseases, and other phenomena
      spread through complex systems.
          As we will see, the classical diffusion process on graphs is inherently linked
      to the structure of the network itself. Although the mathematical model is a sim-
      ple linear equation, this relationship between structure and dynamics is central
      to classical diffusion and has developed into a rich field of study. It is still an ac-
      tive area, with applications in various domains, including physics, biology, social
1.3 Diffusing through the network                                                7

sciences, and computer science.
    Nonetheless, we can add new layers of information to the classical diffusion
process, keeping the core idea of spreading a quantity through the network, while
enhancing specific structural or dynamical properties of the network or of the
phenomenon under study. This thesis will explore some of these extensions, ex-
panding the two main pillars of the field: the theoretical framework and the ap-
plication to real-world problems. On the theoretical side, we will introduce new
mathematical models that modify the influence of central nodes in the diffusion
process, strengthen weak connections, or modify the amount of information the
system processes at each time. Then, we will see how these models can be used to
study real phenomena, such as animal movement patterns, neural activation, or
social interactions. By combining theoretical insights with practical applications,
this thesis aims to contribute to the understanding of a wider class of diffusion
processes on networks and their implications in various fields such as sociology,
ecology, and neurology.

    This Chapter intends to explain the basic concepts and definitions that will
be used throughout this thesis. It is not thought to be an exhaustive review of the
topics, but rather a summary of the most important concepts that will be used
later on or that are needed for undertanding the scope and ideas that will guide
the research presented here.
   For understanding the context of this thesis, we need to set up concepts from
Linear Algebra and Dynamical Systems, both in the context of Graph Theory.
We will study operators and the equations that govern different processes in net-
works, so we need to define these concepts properly.
    Although we will usually restrict ourselves to the most basic cases, we will
use some concepts from more general frameworks, which are extensions of basic
graphs. Thus, it is also necessary to define them properly here, as well as to
explain how diffusion occurs in these new structures.
      10                                              Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

          Another important relationship that must be addressed in this context is the
      relation between discrete and continuous systems. Many of the processes that
      we will study in this thesis have a continuous counterpart, which are the most
      commonly studied in university courses. Thus, we will explain how to relate
      these two types of systems, and how to use the results obtained in one of them
      to understand the other.
          Finally, we will present how to construct more general diffusion operators
      in graphs, stating the basis for the new operators that will be presented in the
      following chapters.

2.1   Concepts on Graph Theory
      Graph Theory is the mathematical field that studies the properties of graphs,
      which are structures made up to describe sets of objects focusing also in the rela-
      tionships between them. Graphs are sometimes called also networks, and many
      works use these two different words to remark if the object in question is an ab-
      stract entity (graph) or a physical one (network). Nonetheless, both words refer
      to the same mathematical object, and we will use them indistinctly throughout
      this thesis.
          These graphs can be used to model a wide variety of systems, from social
      networks to biological systems, and they are a powerful tool for understanding
      the complexities that emerge in many-body systems.
          Let’s give a formal definition of a graph:
        Definition 2.1 An (undirected) graph is a pair G = (V, E), where V is a set
        of objects, called vertices or nodes, and E ⊂ V × V is a symmetric set of
        connections between them, called edges, where × represents the Cartesian
        product between sets.


           R   Note that these objects could be only defined by their connections G =
               E ∈ P(V × V ), as it encloses all the information about the graph. How-
               ever, it is more intuitive to define them as a pair of sets, as it allows to
               clearly differentiate the components V , elements that may have been stud-
               ied individually, and E, that reflects which of them are interacting.
          Although the correct name for this object is undirected graph, due to the fact
      that it is a symmetric relation, we will refer to it simply as graph. The reason for
      this is that it is the most common type of graph, which serves as a base for the
      definition of other types of graphs, and will be the most used in this thesis.
          The most useful representation of graphs are through matrices, as it allows to
2.1 Concepts on Graph Theory                                                           11

use the powerful tools of linear algebra. The most common one is the adjacency
matrix, which is defined as:
  Definition 2.2 The adjacency matrix of a graph G = (V, E) is the matrix
  A ∈ R|V |×|V | , denoting | · | as the cardinality of a set, such that:
                                       (
                                        1 if (i, j) ∈ E
                               Aij =                                                (2.1)

    From this matrix, we can define the degree of a vertex i as the number of edges
that are incident to it, which generally counts the number of nodes connected to
the i, generally called its neighbors. It is given by the sum of the i-th row of the
                           P|V |
adjacency matrix: ⃗ki = j=1 Aij , and it is useful to define the diagonal matrix
of degrees as K = diag(⃗k).
     Nonetheless, there are other matrices that enclose the structure of the graph,
its topology. Another important matrix is the Incidence matrix, which is defined
as:
   Definition 2.3 The incidence matrix of a graph G = (V, E) is the matrix
   B ∈ R|V |×|E| such that:
                             

  where, for an edge e = (i, j), we say that j is the head (the node it is pointing
  to) and i is the tail (the node it is coming from).

   R    The sign convention is not unique, and there authors that define the tail
        as 1 and the head as −1.
    This matrix needs to define a direction for each of the connections in the
graph. The convention is usually to give a numerical label to each node (between
1 and |V |) and, for each edge (i, j), point the head of the edge towards the bigger
node.
    The importance of these matrices is that they directly relate the vertices to
the edges, showing directly which elements participate in each interaction, which
will be relevant for some future definitions.
    It is easy to see that we can divide graphs into smaller structures with the
same properties, called subgraphs:
        12                                              Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

          Definition 2.4 A subgraph of a graph G = (V, E) is a pair H = (VH , EH )
          such that VH ⊆ V and EH ⊆ E.

            For any graph, there are some important properties that can be defined, which
        are useful to understand the structure of the graph. The first of them is the con-
        cept of simple graphs, which we will use throughout this thesis except explicitly
        stated otherwise. For that, we need to define the concept of connectedness and
        paths:
          Definition 2.5 Let G = (V, E) be a graph. Then, the following definitions
          hold:
               • A walk in G is a sequence of nodes v1 , v2 , . . . , vn such that (vi , vi+1 ) ∈
                 E for all i = 1, . . . , n − 1.
               • A path in G is a walk in which all the nodes are distinct, i.e., vi ̸= vj for
                 all i ̸= j.
               • A closed walk in G is a walk in which the first and last nodes are the
                 same, i.e., v1 = vn .
               • A cycle in G is a closed walk that is also a path.
          Definition 2.6 A graph is connected if there is a path between any two nodes
          in the graph.

            If a graph is not connected, we can define its connected components as the
        maximal connected subgraphs, i.e., the largest subgraphs in which any two nodes
        are connected by a path.
          Definition 2.7 A graph is simple if and only if it is connected, undirected and
          has no loops (edges that connect a node to itself).


2.1.1   Special types of graphs
        In the study of graphs, and specially working with complex systems, there are
        kinds of graphs that are very common, either from the point of view of their
        properties or because they are easy to construct. Here we will define some of the
        most typical ones.
             The first one we are going to present are the trees.
          Definition 2.8 A tree is a connected graph with no cycles, i.e., a connected
          acyclic graph.
2.1 Concepts on Graph Theory                                                       13

 Theorem 2.1 A graph G = (V, E) is a tree if and only if it is connected and
 has |E| = |V | − 1.


Proof. For the necessary condition, assume that G is a tree. Then, it is connected
and has no cycles. Lets proceed by induction.
     Suppose that |V | = 1. Then, |E| = 0, and the condition holds trivially. The
same as if |V | = 2, as the conectedness condition implies that there is an edge
between the two nodes and there can not be other, so |E| = 1.
     Suppose that |V | = n and that for any tree with n − 1 nodes, it has exactly
n − 2 edges. Take a node of degree 1, v ∈ V . Such a node must exists, as if
all the nodes had degree at least 2, then any incoming path to the node could be
continued following at least one different adjacent edge, and thus, we would have
to end up in a repeated node as the graph is finite. If we remove the node v, then
the resulting graph G′ = (V ′ , E ′ ) with V ′ = V \ {v} and E ′ = E \ {(v, u)},
where u is the only neighbor of v, is still a tree. Finally, as G′ has n − 1 nodes, by
the induction hypothesis it has n − 2 edges. Thus, G has n − 1 edges.
     For the sufficient condition, suppose that G is connected and has |E| = |V | −
1. Suppose that G has exactly one cycle. Then, there exists at least one edge e ∈ E
that can be removed without disconnecting the graph. If we remove this edge,
then the resulting graph G′ = (V, E\{e}) is still connected and has |E ′ | = |V |−2
edges. Now it is enough to show that a graph with |E| < |V | − 1 can’t be
connected, and we will proceed again by induction.
     Suppose that |V | = 1. Then, |E| = 0, and the condition holds trivially. If
|V | = 2, then |E| ≤ |V | − 1 = 1, so the only possibility is to not have edges and
the graph is trivially disconnected and the minimum condition for connectedness
is |E| = 1 = |V | − 1.
     Finally, for any graph with |V | = n, it can be seen as a graph with n − 1
nodes plus one extra node. To connect this new node to the rest of the graph,
at least one edge is needed. Thus, the induction hypothesis states that the graph
with n − 1 nodes needs at least n − 2 edges to be connected, and adding the new
node requires at least one more edge, so the graph with n nodes needs at least
n − 1 edges to be connected.                                                    q.e.d.


     It is obvious that any graph has trees as subgraphs. This leads to a decompo-
sition in spanning trees, which are tree subgraphs that include all the nodes of
the graph, and are very useful in many applications.
14                                            Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

that everybody sees at school: the family tree. In this case, the nodes are the
members of the family, and the edges are the relationships between them. The
tree structure is given by the assumption that family members are not married
between them, which would close the graph. Unfortunately, this is not always
the case, as some family trees are as the Ptolemaic dynasty of Egypt, where the
family members were married between them to keep the bloodline pure. (Fig. 2.1)

relationships between species, called phylogenetic trees. In this case, the nodes
are the species and the edges are the evolutionary relationships between them.

species, but rather diverge from a common ancestor. (Fig. 2.2)                    ■


    Another important type of graph is the bipartite graph, which is a special case
of graph that has a very specific structure.
    Definition 2.9 Given a graph G = (V, E), we say that it is bipartite if there
    exists a partition of the set of nodes V into two disjoint sets V1 and V2 such
    that every edge in E connects a node in V1 to a node in V2 , i.e., E ⊆ V1 × V2 .

     R   A curious property of bipartite graphs is that all their cycles have an even

■   Example 2.3 A common example of bipartite graph is the one used to represent
the relationships between users and the items they like. In this case, the nodes
in V1 are the users and the nodes in V2 are the items, and the edges connect
users with the items they like. This structure is very common in recommendation
systems, where we want to recommend items to users based on their preferences.
(Fig. 2.3)
