    There are kinds of graphs which are defined by a variable that explains the
important property that defines the network. For example, the complete graph,
which is then only dependent on the number of nodes. This does not mean than
in trees or bipartite graphs the number of nodes is not important, but that the
structure feature important for those does not depend on the size of the system.
Let’s define then the complete graph:
    Definition 2.10 A complete graph is a graph Kn = (V, E) with |V | = n such
    that every pair of nodes is connected by an edge, i.e., E = {(i, j) : i, j ∈
    V, i ̸= j}. (Fig. 2.4a)

     It may seem that the complete graph is not a very interesting graph, as it is
very simple and kind of trivial. However, it is important for two reasons. The
first, purely combinatorial, as it is the maximum number of connections that can
be established between n nodes, and it is used as a reference for many other
properties of graphs. The second reason is that it is the link with the mean field
theory, which is a very important theory in statistical physics that allows to study
systems with a large number of components by approximating the interactions
between them. The complete graph is the simplest case of a mean field system, as
it assumes that all the nodes are connected to each other, and thus the interactions
between them are averaged out.

     R   When a complete graph is presented as a subgraph of a bigger one, it is
         called a clique.
2.1 Concepts on Graph Theory                                               17




Figure 2.3: Subgraph of the Netflix Price dataset [204], showing the rela-
tionships between 10 users and their top-3 movies. The nodes in V1 are the
users and the nodes in V2 are the movies, and the edges connect users with the
movies they like.
18                                            Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

    We can combine this two last definitions to give raise to the complete bipartite
graph, which now depends on two variables, the number of nodes in each set. It
is defined as:
   Definition 2.11 A complete bipartite graph is a bipartite graph Kn,m = (V, E)
   with |V1 | = n and |V2 | = m such that every node in V1 is connected to every
   node in V2 , i.e., E = {(i, j) : i ∈ V1 , j ∈ V2 }. (Fig. 2.4b)

    Now, we can focus on more sparse graphs but which retain an important
property of the complete graph, which is that every node has the same number
of connections, i.e., the degree of every node is the same. These graphs are called
regular graphs, and they are defined as:
    Definition 2.12 A k-regular graph is a graph G = (V, E) such that every node
    has the same degree k, i.e., for the degree vector ⃗k, we have ⃗k(i) = k for all
    i ∈ V . (Fig. 2.4c)

■ Example 2.4 The most known of these regular graphs is the cycle graph, which
is a 2-regular graph.
    Another very important one are the grid graphs, which can be infinite or
cyclic if finite. The most common ones are the square grid graphs, which are 4-
regular graphs and can be seen as a Z2 lattice if they are infinite. Nonetheless,
any cyclic teselation of the plane can be seen as a regular graph, and it is used
commonly the triangular or hexagonal grids, which are 3-regular graphs. These
graphs are very useful in many applications, such as image processing, as they
allow to represent the pixels of an image as nodes and the connections between
them as edges.                                                                  ■




     (a) Complete graph        (b) Complete bipartite          (c) 3-regular graph
                               graph

                   Figure 2.4: Three basic examples of graphs.
2.1 Concepts on Graph Theory                                                     19

   There are then some graphs that are defined by some variable, but that the
regularity presented is in other aspects rather than the degree. A first simple
example is the path graph:
  Definition 2.13 A path graph is a graph Pn = (V, E) with |V | = n such that
  exactly 2 nodes has degree 1 and the other n − 2 nodes have degree 2.

    This graph can be as a 2-regular infinite graph that has been truncated, with-
out making it cyclic. It can be directly linked with a representation of an interval
I ⊂ Z, and it is used in many applications of numerical analysis, such as finite
element methods. They are quite important in the study of diffusive one dimen-
sional systems, as they allow to take a central node to initialize the process and
study properties of the dynamics, such as the diffusion speed, numerically, at least
until the process reaches the boundaries of the path and mirroring effects start to
affect the pure advance of the diffusion barrier.
    Other graphs that are defined by a variable are the star graphs, which are
defined as:
  Definition 2.14 A star graph Sn is the complete bipartite graph K1,n−1 , i.e.,
  a graph with one central node connected to n − 1 nodes, called leafs, that are
  not connected between them.

    This graphs are typically used to study the effect of hubs, or highly connected
nodes, in the dynamics of a system. This is because star graphs are the sim-
plest networks with the minimum degree assortativity, i.e., connections appear
between nodes with different degrees, in this case case between the central node
with degree n − 1 and the leaf nodes with degree 1.
    Among these families of graphs, we have also to define one specific type that
we will use several times during this thesis because of its properties: the Cayley
irregular graphs.
  Definition 2.15 A Cayley irregular (tree) graph of order k is a graph con-
  structed by an starting root node for which we attach k children nodes. Then,
  each of these children nodes is attached to k − 2 children nodes, so they all
  have degree k − 1. The process is repeated until arriving to the leaf nodes,
  with degree 1.

    Finally, there is an interesting property of some graphs that, although it won’t
be used in this thesis, it is worth mentioning: planar graphs. These graphs are
defined as the ones that can be drawn in the plane without any edges crossing
each other. This property is very useful in many applications, such as circuit
          20                                               Chapter 2. Foundational concepts




                   (a) Path graph               (b) Star graph           (c) Cayley irregular tree
                                                                         graph of order k = 4.

                         Figure 2.5: Path, star, and Cayley tree graph examples.


          design, where it is important to avoid crossing wires. The most famous theorem
          about planar graphs is Kuratowski’s theorem, which states that a graph is planar
          if and only if it does not contain a subgraph that is a subdivision of the complete
          graph K5 or the complete bipartite graph K3,3 .

2.1.1.1   Generating graphs
          When trying to study the properties of some dynamical process in a graph, it is
          not enough to consider neither the real life networks we can find all over, nor
          the abstract graphs that we have defined. We need to create new graphs that are
          synthetic but more complex, and that present properties similar to the real ones.
          This is useful if we want to know the impact of a precise network property in the
          dynamical process, such as the existence of hubs or the degree distribution.
              The first kind of these synthetic graphs that we need to define are the equiv-
          alent to the random (null) conditions. This graphs are called Erdös-Rényi graphs,
          and they are defined as:
            Definition 2.16 An Erdös-Rényi-Gilbert graph is a graph G(p) = (V, E) with
            |V | = n such that each pair of nodes is connected by an edge with probability
            p, i.e., the edge set E is distributed as a binomial distribution with parameters
            n(n − 1)/2 and p.


               R     Note that we have called this model the Erdös-Rényi-Gilbert model while
                     it was presented in the previous phrase as just Erdös-Rényi. This is be-
                     cause it is typically called just E-R, but the model proposed by them was
                     slightly different. An original Erdös-Rényi graph is defined as a graph
                     G(n, m) = (V, E) with |V | = n and the set of edges E is chosen uni-
                     formly at random from the set {E ⊂ V × V : |E| = m}, i.e., the set of
2.1 Concepts on Graph Theory                                                            21




Figure 2.6: Example of an Erdös-Rényi graph with n = 20 nodes and p = 0.3.


        all possible edge sets of size exactly m. Obviously, there is an equivalence
        between the two definitions, as choosing p = m        leads to similar graphs
                                                        (n2 )
        in mean. Nonetheless, the first definition is more useful from the point
        of view of generation, as it allows to generate graphs “on the fly”, using a
        random number generator to decide whether to add an edge or not, while
        the second one requires to generate all the possible graphs with m con-
        nections and then choose one of them uniformly at random. Moreover,
        the E-R(-G) model also allows to obtain some probabilistic properties of
        the graph, such as the expected number of edges or the expected degree
        of a node, which are not so easy to obtain in the original model.

     These E-R networks, being uniformly random created, are not very realis-
tic, but they are useful to test models as a null model for structural properties.
This is, as every edge can be present at random, no nodes should present any
special property, and processes typically show their behaviour in heterogeneous
networks without a big influence of the structure.
    Even though the heterogeneity introduced by this networks show some dif-
ferences in the processes with respect to the continuous, regular case, there are
some properties of real world networks that have to be studied in a synthetic way.
One of this properties is the existence of hubs, which are nodes with a very high
degree, much higher than the average degree of the network. This is a common
property in networks such as the internet or social networks. It is not present in
E-R networks as all nodes have a degree close to the average one. To create net-
works with this property, we can use the Barabási-Albert model, which is defined
22                                           Chapter 2. Foundational concepts




Figure 2.7: Example of a Barabási-Albert graph with n = 20 nodes and m = 2.


as follows:
  Definition 2.17 A Barabási-Albert graph is a graph G(n, m) = (V, E) with
  |V | = n generated by a process of preferential attachment defined as follows:
       • Start with a graph with m0 ≤ m nodes, typically an E-R graph with
         mean degree m.
       • At each time step, add a new node v to the graph and connect it to m
         existing nodes, chosen with a probability proportional to their degree,
         i.e., the probability of connecting to node i is given by pi = |Vk|i , where
                                                                       P
                                                                             kj
                                                                       j=1
        ki is the degree of node i.
      • Stop when the graph has n nodes.
    This preferential attachment model generates networks with a power-law de-
gree distribution Π(k) ∼ k −γ , with γ ≈ 3. A power-law degree distribution is
a property that defines the scale-free networks. These can also be seen, grossely
speaking, as networks in which a subgraph will present some resemblance to the
whole graph.
      Finally, there is another property of many real networks, speciallly those
coming from social interactions, that does not necessarily appear in the previous
model of graph generation: the small-world property. This property is defined as
the fact that, in a network, any two nodes are typically connected by a short path,
i.e., the average distance between any two nodes is small. This property is not
2.1 Concepts on Graph Theory                                                    23




Figure 2.8: Example of a Watts-Strogatz graph with n = 20 nodes, k = 4
nearest neighbors, and p = 0.3.




present in E-R networks, as they are typically sparse and the average distance in-
creases as the graph size increases. On the other hand, the Barabási-Albert model
does not guarantee this property, as the existence of hubs may allow for this
short path existence (through the most connected node), but leaf nodes may still
be far apart. To create networks with this property, we can use the Watts-Strogatz
model, which is defined as follows:
  Definition 2.18 A Watts-Strogatz graph is a graph G(n, k, p) = (V, E) with
  |V | = n generated by a process of rewiring defined as follows:
       • Start with a regular ring lattice with n nodes, each connected to k near-
         est neighbors.
       • With probability p, rewire each edge to a randomly chosen node, creat-
         ing shortcuts.


    This model generates networks with a small average distance between nodes,
as the rewiring process creates shortcuts that connect distant nodes. The param-
eter p controls the amount of rewiring, and it can be adjusted to obtain networks
with different properties. For p = 0, the network is a regular ring lattice, while
for p = 1, the network is an E-R random graph.
        24                                             Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

2.2     Generalizations of Graphs
        The definition of graph is very broad, and it can be applied to many different
        systems. However, there are some systems that are not well represented by dis-
        crete nodes that interact in a pairwise and reciprocal fashion. For these different
        systems, we can use different generalizations of the concept of graph.

2.2.1   Directed Graphs
        In a directed graph, the edges have a direction, meaning that they are not recip-
        rocal. This means that the edges are not symmetric, and nodes can affect their
        neighbors without being influenced back. The formal definition of a directed
        graph is:
          Definition 2.19 A directed graph (digraph) is a pair G = (V, E), where V is a
          set of objects, called vertices or nodes, and E ⊂ V × V is a set of connections
          between them, called directed edges, which might not be symmetric.

            In this case, the standard directed incidence matrix is a clear matrix represen-
        tation of a digraph without any necessary change. Nonetheless, the adjacency
        matrix is slightly different even though the definition is the same, as the fact that
        now (i, j) ∈ E does not imply (j, i) ∈ E means that the matrix is not symmetric.
        This has an important consequence on the definition of node degree.
            For directed graphs, we can differenciate between the in-degree and out-
        degree of a node. They are defined as:
          Definition 2.20 Given a directed graph G = (V, E) with adjacency matrix A,
          the in-degree of a node i is defined as:

                                                      |V |
                                                      X
                                          (kin )i =          Aji                       (2.3)
                                                      j=1

          , accounting for the number of edges that point to node i, and the out-degree
          of a node i is defined as:
                                                  |V |
                                                  X
                                       (kout )i =      Aij                         (2.4)
                                                      j=1

          accounting for the number of edges that point from node i to its neighbors.

            This differentiation will be crucial for the definition of the dynamical opera-
        tors we will define later.
        2.2 Generalizations of Graphs                                                        25




        Figure 2.9: Directed graph representing the ethane dehydrogenation network.
        Extracted from [64], where the Figure (a) is the traditional reaction network
        and there are internal nodes in the reactions, while the Figure (b) is the corre-
        sponding network with these nodes explicitly declared, and the directions show
        the reversivility of each of the reactions.


        ■   Example 2.5 Chemical reactions are a common example of directed graphs, as
        the reactions have a direction, from the reactants to the products. In this case, the
        nodes are the chemical species and the edges are the reactions between them. The
        direction of the edges is given by the fact that the reactions go from the reactants
        to the products, and not all reactions are reversible. (Fig. 2.9)
                                                                                             ■



2.2.2   Weighted Graphs
        The next generalization is broadly used as well, due to its direct representation of
        real systems. In a weighted graph, the edges have a weight (positive) associated
        with them. These weights may represent several situations, but usually are seen
        as the strength of the interactions. The formal definition of a weighted graph
        is:
           Definition 2.21 A weighted graph is a triplet G = (V, E, W ), where G =
           (V, E) is a graph and W : E → R+ is a function that assigns a positive
           weight to each edge in the graph.

        As by the definition, weighted graphs could also be directed.

             R   There is a conceptually different type of graph, called multigraph, which
                 allows multiple edges between the same pair of nodes. While the construc-
                 tion and definition of some processes are different in these multigraphs,
                 they can be generally seen as a weighted graph, where the weight of the
26                                           Chapter 2. Foundational concepts




Figure 2.10: Sioux Falls Transportation Network, where the weights of the
edges represent the distance between the intersections.



         edge is the number of edges between the two nodes.


    Regarding the matrix representation of a weighted graph, the extensions are
straightforward. The adjacency and incidence matrices are now a weighted ma-
trix, where the entries now correspond to the edge weights instead of being all
1.

■   Example 2.6 Transportation networks are a common example of weighted graphs,
as the edges represent the connections between the nodes and the weights rep-
resent the distance or cost of the transportation. In this case, the nodes are the
locations and the edges are the connections between them, with weights repre-
senting the distance or cost of the transportation. In the case of the road network
of a city, typically the nodes are the intersections and the edges are the roads,
with weights representing the distance or time it takes to travel between them,
which is a very intuitive way of representing a city. (Fig. 2.10)

                                                                                 ■
        2.2 Generalizations of Graphs                                                                     27

2.2.3   Hypergraphs
        The previous generalizations are based on changing the pairwise interactions.
        However, there are systems where the interactions are not pairwise, but involve
        multiple nodes. This would account for situations in which the interaction ap-
        pears in groups of different sizes, instead of just two-by-two relationships. For
        these systems, we can use hypergraphs, which are defined as:
            Definition 2.22 A hypergraph is a pair G = (V, E), where V is a set of objects,
            called vertices or nodes, and E ⊂ P(V ) is a set of connections between them,
            called hyperedges, which connect groups of nodes of any size.

        Of course, hyperedges can be weighted as well, with implications similar to the
        ones in weighted graphs.
            The matrix representation of hypergraphs is not as straightforward as the pre-
        vious ones, because the adjacency matrix is not well defined. The most common
        representation is the incidence matrix, which is defined as:
            Definition 2.23 The incidence matrix of a hypergraph G = (V, E) is the ma-
            trix B ∈ R|V |×|E| such that:
                                                      (
                                                       1     if i ∈ j
                                              Bij =                                                    (2.5)
                                                         0   otherwise

            where a node i is incident to a hyperedge j = (j1 , j2 , . . . , jk ) if i ∈ (j1 , j2 , . . . , jk ).

             R     Note that the incidence matrix, as in the case of undirected graphs, is not
                   unique, as it depends on the order of the nodes in the hyperedge, but the
                   implications rather differ in this case, as we will see further in this chapter.
        ■   Example 2.7 An example of hypergraphs from the Academia are the collabora-
        tion hypernetworks, where the nodes are the researchers and the hyperedges are
        the articles in which they have collaborated together. In this case, a hyperedge
        connects all the researchers that have co-authored a particular article, but not all
        of them have to have collaborated even pair-wise and they will have articles with
        different sets of co-authors. (Fig. 2.11)
                                                                                                            ■


           There is a special case of hypergraphs which is very used in the literature
        because of their nice properties, called simplicial complexes. These objects come
        from a geometric background, so we will define them first in a geometric way,
        and then we will show how to represent them as hypergraphs.
28                                               Chapter 2. Foundational concepts




Figure 2.11: Hypergraph representing the collaboration network of the authors
in the first 100 articles of the DBLP computer science bibliography [244]. The
nodes are the authors and the hyperedges in colors are the articles in which they
have collaborated together. The hyperedges are represented as circles, with the
nodes inside them being the authors of the article.


  Definition 2.24 A set of points s = (a0 , a1 , . . . , ak ) ∈ Rn (k ≥ n ≥ 2) is
  geometrically independent if the set of vectors (a1 −a0 , a2 −a0 , . . . , ak −a0 ) is
  linearly independent in Rn . The convex hull of s is the set of points that can be
  expressed as a linear combination of the points in s with positive coefficients,
  i.e., the set of points s ∈ Rn such that:

                            k
                            X                               k
                                                            X
                   sk = {         λi ai , with λi ≥ 0 and         λi = 1}.         (2.6)
                            i=0                             i=0

  In this case we say that sk is a k-simplex with vertices a0 , a1 , . . . , ak . Any
  space spanned by a subset of the vertices s is called a face of sk . If K is a
  collection of simplices, we say that it is a (geometric) simplicial complex if it
  satisfies the following conditions:
      • If sk ∈ K, then all its faces sj are in K.
      • If sk , τ ∈ K and sk ∩ τ ̸= ∅, then the intersection is a face of both sk
         and τ .

    Under this definition, which we call geometric simplicial complex, we obtain
a set of vertices which would be the nodes of a network, while the different faces
of the simplices would be the hyperedges. This means that we can represent a
2.2 Generalizations of Graphs                                                     29




Figure 2.12: Example of a simplicial complex representing the coverage of a
terrain by sensors.


simplicial complex as a very specific case of hypergraph, with the difference that it
is no longer an abstract object, but a geometric one. This allows to represent them
using coordinates in a euclidean space, which is very useful for some applications.
■   Example 2.8 One example where naturally emerges simplicial complexes (of
dimension 2) is in the study of sensor coverage among a terrain [200]. Each node
represents a sensor, which cover a certain area around it. If two sensors are close
enough, they are connected by an edge, as they can “see” each other. Then, the
triangles appear if three sensors overlap in their coverage area. This is an easy
representation to look and understand is there are any holes in the area to cover.
(Fig. 2.12)
                                                                                   ■


    On the other hand, the property of inclusion of the simplicial complex is a
key property, even more that the one about the geometric independence. This
allows mathematicians to use tools from the field of algebraic topology, such as
the definition of homology or the Hodge decomposition theorem, rather than
just the geometric ones. This is why the whole concept of (geometric) simplicial
complex can be slightly modified to obtain a more abstract definition, which is
the one that is usually used in the literature.
30                                             Chapter 2. Foundational concepts




Figure 2.13: Example of a simplicial complex representing a group contagion
process from [147]. A susceptible individual can be affected by an infected one
with probability β. If there is a group interaction of three people, meaning that
they form a simplex, then the probability of infection is not two independent
events with probability β, but includes a third independent event with probability
β∆ .


    Definition 2.25 Given a family of sets K, we say that it is an abstract simplicial
    complex if, for every set s ∈ K, every non-trivial subset τ ⊆ s is also in K.

    This definition includes the previous one, as any geometric simplicial com-
plex can be seen as an abstract simplicial complex, but it is not grounded in an
euclidean space. It can be seen this way that (abstract) simplicial complexes are a
very special case of hypergraphs, where the hyperedges are closed under inclu-
sion. This means that if a hyperedge connects 4 nodes, then all the triangles and
pairwise edges that can be formed with them are hyperedges as well.
■  Example 2.9 These abstract simplicial complexes are applied, for example, to
simmulate group contagion processes, in which the nodes are individuals that can
be susceptible or infected. In these cases, a susceptible individual can be infected
by the infected ones, but the probability of infection depends on the number of
infected individuals in the group. The inclusion property comes from the fact that
if an individual can be infected by a group, then it can be infected by any subset
of people in that group too (Fig. 2.13)                                            ■


     In any kind of hypergraph, but more specifically in simplicial complexes due
to its definition, we can define a new object coming from the geometric interpre-
tation: the boundary of a hyperedge. The boundary of a hyperedge is the set of
2.2 Generalizations of Graphs                                                        31

all the faces of the hyperedge, which are all the hyperedges of the hypergraph
that are a subset of the hyperedge. In the case of simplicial complexes it is direct
that any face of a hyperedge is a hyperedge as well, so it can be expressed as: the
boundary of a k-simplex is the union of all the (k − 1)-simplices formed by any
k − 1 combination of the vertices of the original simplex.
    From now on, we will generally refer to these objects as hypergraphs and,
when refering to simplicial complexes, we will refer to the abstract ones, as they
are the ones that are studied in the graph theory literature.
    Regarding the matrix representation of hypergraphs, the adjacency matrix is
not so well defined, as it has to account for different kind of interactions of dif-
ferent sizes. However, the incidence matrix is well defined [26], and it is given by
a block matrix of the incidence matrices for the different hyperedge sizes. Thus,
we have to define how to construct the incidence matrices of different orders, for
which is again necessary to give a different numerical label to each node, between
1 and |V |.
  Definition 2.26 Let G = (V, E) be a hypergraph. Denote by M = max{|e| :
  e ∈ E} the maximum size of the hyperedges in G. Fix 0 ≤ m ≤ M . The
  incidence matrix of order k is a matrix Bk ∈ Rn×m , where n is the number of
  simplices of order k −1 in the hypergraph and m is the number of k-simplices,
  such that the entry corresponding to a k − 1-simplex [a0 , a1 , . . . , ak−1 ] and
  a k-simplex [a0 , a1 , . . . , ak−1 , j] is equal to the sign of the permutation that
  orders the set {a0 , a1 , . . . , ak−1 , j} into an increasing set. By convention,
  B0 = BM +1 = ∅. The complete incidence matrix of the hypergraph is the
  block matrix                                               
                                        B0 0 · · · 0
                                                             
                                      0 B1 · · · 0 
                               B= .            ..  ..     ..  .
                                                             
                                      ..        .     .    . 
                                                             
                                         0     0 · · · BM

   To have a higher variety of matrix representations, we can see the hyper-
graphs as different structures which have a clear matrix representation.
    For example, we can see the hypergraph as a bipartite graph, where one group
of nodes are the vertices of the hypergraph and the other group are the hyper-
edges, and the links between these groups are given by the inclusion of the ver-
tices in the hyperedges.
   This so called facet representation allows to use the adjacency matrix of the
        32                                           Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

        bipartite graph to represent the hypergraph (with size equal to |V | + |E| × |V | +
        |E|), but we need further information as this way we lose the information about
        the size of the hyperedges. This can be solved by using a weighted bipartite graph,
        where the weights of the edges are the sizes of the hyperedges. This way we can
        use the adjacency matrix of the bipartite graph to represent the hypergraph, but
        we need further information as this way we lose the information about the size
        of the hyperedges.
             Other option is to consider it a multigraph, obtained by projecting the bipar-
        tite graph into the node set. In this case, the weight or number of edges connect-
        ing two nodes is the number of hyperedges that include both of them. With it,
        we can obtain an adjacency matrix of size |V | × |V |, and define even the degree
        of a node in the hypergraph as the number of number of hyperedges that include
        it, counting its size.
             Finally, we can consider the multipartite representation via Hasse diagrams.
        It consists in constructing a directed graph from the hypergraph, where the nodes
        are the hyperedges and the edges are the inclusion relations between them. Again,
        this representation needs extra information for general hypergraphs as it may
        lose the information about the size of the hyperedges.

2.2.4   Gain graphs
        The last discrete generalization we will define is the gain graph. These are a very
        abstract case of graphs which, under specific conditions, can be really useful to
        model real-world phenomena.
            To define them correctly, we need to define first the concept of group:
          Definition 2.27 A group is a set Γ with a binary operation · : Γ × Γ → Γ such
          that:
              • The operation is associative: (a · b) · c = a · (b · c) for all a, b, c ∈ Γ.
              • There is an identity element e ∈ Γ such that a · e = e · a = a for all
                a ∈ Γ.
              • For every element a ∈ Γ, there exists an inverse element b ∈ Γ such
                that a · b = b · a = e.

            Then, we can join the concept of graph with the concept of group to define a
        gain graph:
          Definition 2.28 A gain graph is a triplet (G, (Γ, ·), ϕ), where G = (V, E) is an
          undirected graph, Γ is a group and ϕ : E → Γ is a function that assigns a group
2.2 Generalizations of Graphs                                                      33

    element to each edge in the graph with the following condition: For a given
    edge (i, j) ∈ E, the group element ϕ((i, j)) is the inverse of the group element
    ϕ((j, i)), corresponding to the edge trasversed in the opposite direction.
    There is here an important change in the structure of the graph that appears
directly in the gain graph. Although the original graph is undirected, the gain
graph is directed, but in a very special way, as each of the connections is bidi-
rected. Bidirected graphs are a compromise between fully directed and undirected
graphs, as and undirected edge and a bidirected one are equivalent in most cases.
The difference in gain graphs is the inverse relation given by the function ϕ.
    A gain graph has a quite broad definition, seemingly too abstract to be use-
ful. However, there are straightforward situations in which this is the correct
representation of the system.
■   Example 2.10 Consider the Rubik’s cube, a cube with each of its faces divided
into 9 squares, all of them with the same color and one different per face. Each of
the faces of the cube can be rotated independently, modifying the adjacent faces.
We can represent each possible configuration of the cube as a node in a graph,
and two states will be linked if they can be obtained from each other by a single
rotation of one of the faces. Thus, we can assign to each edge the face and rotation
that connects the two configurations, and it is obvious that trasversing the edge
in the opposite direction will correspond to rotate the same face in the opposite
direction. (Fig. 2.14)
                                                                                     ■


    About the representation of gain graphs, we can use the same adjacency ma-
trix as in the case of undirected graphs, but now the entries are not 0 or 1, but
the group elements assigned to the edges.
    There is an important subset of any kind of the gain graphs, that has to do
with the structure of the graph, the group, its operation but specially the function
ϕ. They are called the balanced (gain) graphs [271], which have a set of equivalent
definitions:
    Theorem 2.2 Given a gain graph (G, (Γ, ·), ϕ), the following are equivalent:
       (I) The gain graph is balanced.
      (II) Let A be the adjacency matrix of the undirected graph G. There exists a
           function θ : V → Γ such that, if Θ = diag(θ), then the gain matrix A
           can be expressed as A = Θ · A · Θ−1 .
     (III) The product of the group elements around any cycle is the identity ele-
34                                               Chapter 2. Foundational concepts




Figure 2.14: Gain graph of the 2 × 2 × 2 Rubik’s cube, where the nodes are
the configurations of the cube and the edges are the rotations of the faces (only
allowed 180◦ turns). The green lines represent the rotation of the frontal face,
the blue ones are the upper face rotation while the red lines are the rotations
of the lateral one [15].


       ment.
  (IV) For each u, v ∈ V , the product of the group elements along any walk
       from u to v is independent of the walk taken.


     R   Note that, as we are working with groups, the adjacency matrix has non-
         zero entries equal to the identity element of the group, which might not
         be equal to the number one, and the operation · in the formula is not the
         usual matrix multiplication, but the group operation (which is well defined
         for these matrices as Θ is diagonal). Nonetheless, as in this thesis we will
         focus on gain graphs whose groups are contained in the complex num-
         bers, we will use 1 as the identity element of the group and · as the usual
         multiplication without changing any notation for these general cases.

Proof. Let’s see the equivalence of the different definitions via a circular implica-
tion:
    • (II) =⇒ (III): Writing the adjacency matrix as A = Θ · A · Θ−1 , im-
      plies that, for any u, v ∈ V adjacent, then ϕ((u, v)) = θ(u)−1 · θ(v).
      Thus, if we consider any cicle as a sequence of nodes u1 , u2 , . . . , un with
      u1 = un , we can write the product of the group elements as ϕ((u1 , u2 )) ·
      ϕ((u2 , u3 )) · · · ϕ((un−1 , un )) = θ(u1 )−1 ·θ(u2 )·θ(u2 )−1 ·θ(u3 ) · · · θ(un−1 )−1 ·
      θ(un ) = 1.
2.2 Generalizations of Graphs                                                        35

   • (III) =⇒ (IV): For any u, v ∈ V , consider two walks W1 and W2 from u to
     v, and denote by W2−1 the same walk trasnversed in the opposite direction.
     Then, W1 ∪ W2−1 is a cycle, and we can write the product of the group
     elements as ϕ(W1 ∪ W2−1 ) = ϕ(W1 ) · ϕ(W2−1 ) = 1. Using the fact that
     ϕ(e−1 ) = ϕ(e)−1 , we can write ϕ(W2−1 ) = ϕ(W2 )−1 , which implies that
     ϕ(W1 ) = ϕ(W2 ).
   • (IV) =⇒ (II): Fix a node u ∈ V and define the function θ : V → Γ
     as θ(v) = ϕ(Wu,v ), where Wu,v is any walk from u to v. This is well
     defined as u is fixed and, for a given v, it is independent of the walk taken
     by hypothesis. The, for any two connected nodes in the graph v, w ∈ V ,
     the edge (v, w) between them is a particular walk from v to w, and so it
     is any walk of the form Wv,u ∪ Wu,w . Thus, we can write ϕ((v, w)) =
     ϕ(Wu,v )−1 · ϕ(Wu,w ) = θ(v)−1 · θ(w), which directly implies that θ is the
     function we are looking for.
                                                                             q.e.d.

   R    For any function θ : V → Γ, we can define the switching of a gain graph
        as the function ϕθ ((u, v)) = θ(u)−1 · ϕ((u, v)) · θ(v) for any u, v ∈ V .
        This is a new gain graph equivalent to the original one.
            This means that balanced gain graphs are the subset of gain graphs
        that are equivalent to the undirected graph.
    These subset of gain graphs are the ones with better properties, and will be
long used in this thesis.
    Among the gain graphs, there are important subsets that are further studied
in the literature because of the group structure being naturally included in the
matrix theory. These are the so-called T-graphs, which are gain graphs whose
associated group is contained in the complex unit circle T = {z ∈ C : |z| = 1},
with the multiplication as the group operation [214]. This means that the group
elements are complex numbers of modulus 1 and so, its inverse is the conjugate
and it’s contained in the group as well.
    For these graphs, we can easily define the adjacency matrix as the matrix
A ∈ C|V |×|V | such that Aij = ϕ((i, j)) if (i, j) ∈ E and Aij = 0 otherwise. This
matrix is not symmetric, but have the property that aji = a−1     ij = aij , for any
                                                                          ∗

element in the matrix, where a∗ij is the complex conjugate of aij .
    similarly, we can define the incidence matrix as the incidence matrix in the
underlying graph (V, E) with an arbitrary fixed orientation, but now the entry
corresponding to the edge (i, j) is given by ϕ((i, j)) multiplied by the sign chosen
for such edge. This means that the incidence matrix is not unique, as it depends
36                                           Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

on the orientation chosen, but it is well defined for any orientation and we will
see how this does not really affect for future definitions.
    There are, nonetheless, some differences here with respect to the degrees of
the nodes. On the one hand, we have the absolute degree dabs , which correspond
to the degree vector of the underlying graph and it is only a nonnegative integer.
On the other hand, for any g ∈ Γ, we can define the g-degree of a node i as
dgi = |{j ∈ Ni : ϕ((i, j)) = g}|, where N (i) is the set of neighbors of i. Then,
the net degree is dnet = g∈Γ gdgi , where we have used that the integers are a
                          P
                   i
subset of the complex numbers, so we can multiply and add the elements g and
the g-degrees. This net degree thus is not well defined for a general gain graph.
■   Example 2.11 Let’s consider the most used case of T-graphs, and of gain graphs
in general: the signed graphs. These are a special case of gain graphs, where the
group is the set {1, −1} with the multiplication and the function ϕ assigns to
each edge one of these elements. This means that the edges are either positive or
negative, and they can be seen as weighted graphs allowing negative signs.
     It is natural to use these sigraphs as representation of conflicts or compe-
titions, where the positive edges represent cooperation relationships while the
negative edges represent an antagonistic relationship.
   Moreover, this interpretation allows to see balance in signed graphs as an-
other equivalent property:

    Theorem 2.3 A signed graph is balanced if and only if the set of nodes V can
    be splitted into two disjoint sets V1 and V2 , such that all the edges between
    nodes in V1 and V2 are negative, and all the edges between nodes in the same
    set are positive.



Proof. The proof is straightforward from the definition (II) of the Theorem 2.2.
Given the function θ : V → {1, −1}, we can define the sets V1 = {v ∈ V :
θ(v) = 1} and V2 = {v ∈ V : θ(v) = −1}. Then, for any edge (u, v), we
have that ϕ((u, v)) = θ(u)−1 · θ(v) = (θ(u))2 = 1 if u, v are in the same set,
and ϕ((u, v)) = −1 if they are in different sets. Thus, the edges between nodes
in the same set are positive and the edges between nodes in different sets are
negative.                                                                 q.e.d.


                                                                                     ■
        2.2 Generalizations of Graphs                                                           37

2.2.5   Metaplexes
        This last generalization does not follow the same line as the previous one, as
        it is not a more abstract concept, but a more general view that entangles the
        discrete nature of graphs with the apparent continuity that we seen in the real
        world. This concept uses ideas and toold from both the linear algebra and graph
        theory together with Partial Differential Equations (PDEs) and Functional Anal-
        ysis.
          Definition 2.29 A metaplex is a 5-tuple Υ = (V, E, ω, I, F), where (V, E) is
          a graph, ω = {Ωi | i = 0, . . . , k} is a collection of domains, Ωi ⊂ Rni , where
          ni ∈ N for each i, I : V → ω and F = {Fij | i, j = 0, . . . , k; i ̸= j} are
          bounded analytic maps between these domains. This is, for 0 ≤ i ̸= j ≤ k,
          Fij : Ωi → Ωj , ∥Fij ∥∞ < ∞.

        Here, the underlying graph of the metaplex is the set (V = [0, . . . , k], E =
        {[i, j] : Fij ̸= ∅}). Then, the tuple (ω, I) consists on the set of domains (Ωi )
        without repetitions and I assigns to each node i ∈ [0, . . . , k] the element on the
        set ω corresponding to the original Ωi . We have modified here the previous defi-
        nition of metaplex [90] to remark an important topological characteristic of this
        mathematical object: sinks and sources.
          Definition 2.30 Let Υ = (V, E, ω, I, F) be a metaplex. Then, for each 0 ≤
          i, j ≤ k we call dom(Fij ) ⊆ Ωi a sink of the domain Ωi connected to Im(Fij ) ⊆
          Ωj , which is a source of the domain Ωj .

        These pairwise connected regions relate the different domains, coupling the two
        (continuous) spaces in a similar way as edges relate the discrete nodes in a graph.

           R    We could have defined the metaplex more compactly as just the (ω, F)
                pair, as ω differentiates the nodes in the same way as V , and the collection
                of edges can be seen as E = {(i, j) : Fi,j ̸= ∅}. However, we will keep the
                graph structure as it is useful to define the metaplexes in a more intuitive
                way.

            There are some examples of real world systems that can be represented as
        metaplexes, described in the original article where they were introduced [90]
        (Fig. 2.15). For example, the metaplexes can be used to represent the cell interac-
        tions in a tissue, where the nodes are the cells, with their internal structure and
        dynamics represented by the domains Ωi , and the interactions between them oc-
        cur in their touching boundaries. Another example is in the lexicon, where there
        are several groups of words related between them, but in each group there are
      38                                          Chapter 2. Foundational concepts




            Figure 2.15: Examples of a metaplex representation given in [90].


      more specifici words that can be connected to each other through their mean-
      ings. In ecology, metaplexes can appear in the study of foraging, where animals
      have different areas where they can find food, and they can move from one area
      to another, not stopping in the middle as there is no food there, so the type of
      movement changes from a slow continuous foraging pace to a more directed fast
      movement between areas. Finally, metaplexes can be used to represent clymato-
      logical systems, where the nodes are the different regions of the world with thei
      own local climate, but which may cause global effects that change the climate in
      other regions, such as the El Niño phenomenon.

2.3   From Continuous to Discrete
      University courses and textbooks on dynamical systems typically focus on solv-
      ing systems where the spatial domain is continuous. As a result, most problems
      revolve around spatial advection, the Laplacian operator, and similar differential
      operators, all considered within the continuous regime.
          However, to explain these operators, the fundamental concepts often origi-
      nate from discrete settings. For instance, the derivation of these operators fre-
      quently relies on the differences in concentrations between two points. From this
      foundation, an entire discrete theory is developed, which, when taken to the limit,
      results in the operators commonly used in this field.
          This approach became even more crucial with the advent of computers, which
        2.3 From Continuous to Discrete                                                    39

        inherently operate on discrete representations of mathematical objects. Due to
        their practical importance, it is useful to recall the methods used to approximate
        continuous spaces with discrete structures.
            Here, we will take the opposite approach. Given that continuous operators
        are already well known, we will first describe all the tools necessary to solve the
        diffusion equation in the continuous case to point out the differences with the
        discrete case later. This will allow us to understand the differences between the
        two cases and how to approach them.
            Finally, we will see that the intrinsic topological properties of a graph can sig-
        nificantly influence diffusion operators, such as the Laplacian, giving rise to prop-
        erties not present in the continuous case. This thesis will explore these unique
        properties and their implications for the study of dynamical systems on graphs.

2.3.1   The Heat Equation and Diffusion
        Heat is arguably one of the most crucial elements in human progress throughout
        history. It possesses numerous advantages, making its theoretical study, particu-
        larly through mathematical descriptions, of great interest.
            Fortunately, the equation governing the diffusion of heat is relatively simple,
        which is why it is one of the first partial differential equations (PDEs) introduced
        in any introductory course on the subject. Mathematically, the heat equation is
        given by:

                                            ∂u
                                               = −µ∆u                                    (2.7)
                                            ∂t
             This equation contains several key elements that will soon become familiar,
        and here we will remind some of their most important properties.
             First, on the left-hand side, we have the time derivative, which represents the
        rate of change of the function u over time. On the right-hand side, we have the
        Laplacian operator, which measures the variation of u over space. This variation
        is isotropic, meaning that it is uniform in all directions, regardless of the choice
        of reference system. The constant µ > 0 is known as the diffusion coefficient,
        which quantifies how quickly heat diffuses through the medium. As it is related
        with the time scale at which the diffusion occurs, we will always assume µ = 1
        for simplicity.
             But, to fully understand these operators, and thus the heat equation, we must
        understand what is the function u they act upon. The function u represents a
        concentration of some quantity, such as heat, at each point in space. Given an
40                                              Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

spatial domain Ω ⊆ Rn , then we can define u as u : R+ × Ω → R, so that the
first argument represents time and the second represents space. Although the
properties of the domains, specially of Ω is crucial to the behavior of the heat
equation and it is an entire field of study [70, 97, 124], we will not focus on this
aspect in this thesis, and we assume that Ω is an smooth domain.
     Instead, we will focus now in the properties of u.
     For the left hand side of the equation, we can see that the time derivative of
u, which, for a fixed point x is given by the limit:

                          ∂u       u(t + h, x) − u(t, x)
                             = lim                       .                           (2.8)
                          ∂t   h→0           h
     If such limit exists for every t ∈ R+ , then we say that u is differentiable and,
if the derivative is continuous, we say that u is continuously differentiable or,
equivalently, belongs to the space C 1 (R+ ). We can also define the space C k (R+ )
as the space of functions that are k times continuously differentiable and, spe-
cially important is the space of smooth (infinitely differentiable) functions C ∞ .
Again, in this thesis is not intended to deal with the properties of the temporal
domain, so we will always assume that u is smooth in time.
     On the right hand side of the equation, we have the Laplacian operator, which
measures the variation of u over space. In the spatial domain, beeing it high-
dimensional, needs to fix a reference system to define the derivatives in each
direction. In the standard Cartesian coordinates, the Laplacian is defined as:
                                           n
                                           X ∂2u
                                    ∆u =              .                              (2.9)
                                           i=1
                                               ∂x2i

     This operator involves (second) derivatives, which are defined equally as be-
fore. But the difference here is the image space of both operators. If the spa-
tial domain of the function u is Rn , then the the derivative operator can be ap-
plied in any of the coordinate directions, obtaining the Gradient operator ∇u =
  ∂u ∂u
( ∂x  ,
     1 ∂x2
                      ∂u
           , . . . , ∂x n
                          ) : Rn → Rn , so it is a vector operator. On the other hand, the
Laplacian operator is scalar, meaning that, as well as the original function u, it
maps Rn to R.
     Nonetheless, the Laplacian involving derivatives implies that it must have
some relation with the gradient operator. For seeing this, we must define the
                                    Pn ∂ui
divergence operator divu =              i=1 ∂xi . On the one hand, this operator is de-
fined for vector fields, so it maps Rn to R, and it measures the variation of the
vector field u in each direction. On the other hand, it is the adjoint operator of
2.3 From Continuous to Discrete                                                      41

the gradient operator, meaning that, for any smooth function u and v, we have
  Ω udivv = − Ω ∇u · v. The relation with the Laplacian is given by the identity
R               R

∆u = div(∇u), which allows to see the Laplacian as a measure of the variation of
the gradient of u, so it quantifies the variation of the change of u in each direction.
     You may observe that the Laplacian operator involving the second derivative
of u in each direction, meaning that it is natural to assume, for it to be well defined,
that u must be at least twice differentiable in each direction, i.e., u ∈ C 2 (Ω).
     Nonetheless, this is not a necessary condition. In fact, the Laplacian can be
defined for functions that are not even continuous, as long as the second deriva-
tives exist in a weak sense. This leads to the concept of weak derivatives, which
are defined through integration by parts, and the space of functions that possess
these derivatives, the Sobolev spaces.
     To understand these spaces, it is necessary to introduce first the spaces of
integrable functions.
  Definition 2.31 Given a domain Ω ⊆ Rn , we can define the space Lp (Ω) as
  the space of functions that are p-integrable in Ω, i.e., functions u such that:
                                   Z
                                      |u|p < ∞;                              (2.10)
                                      Ω
  with the special case p = ∞ being the space of bounded almost everywhere
  functions.

   R    We may be even less restrictive and consider the space Lploc (Ω), which is
        the space of functions that are p-integrable in every compact subset of Ω.
    All these spaces have several interesting properties, such as the fact that they
are Banach spaces, which means that they are complete vector spaces. Moreover,
if and only if |Ω| < ∞, then we have the inclusion Lp (Ω) ⊂ Lq (Ω) if p > q.
    In particular, L2 (Ω) is a Hilbert space, which means that it has an associated
inner product, given by:
                                            Z
                                   ⟨u, v⟩ =    uv.                            (2.11)
                                              Ω
    Thanks to this inner product and the well known formula for integration by
parts, we can define the notion of weak derivatives.
  Definition 2.32 Given a function u ∈ L1loc (Ω), we say that v is the weak
  derivative of u if, for every test function ϕ ∈ Cc∞ (Ω), the following equality
  holds:
42                                              Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

                                Z                   Z
                                            ∂ϕ
                                        u      =−       vϕ.                   (2.12)
                                    Ω       ∂x      Ω
    This definition is equivalent to the standard definition of derivatives if u is
continuously differentiable, but it is more general, as it allows for functions that
are not even continuous.
    Now, we define the Sobolev spaces W 1,p (Ω) as the space of functions which
have a weak derivative in Lp (Ω). It can be generalized to the space W k,p (Ω),
which is the space of functions that have a k weak derivatives in Lp (Ω). A special
name is given to W k,2 (Ω) = H k , as it is as well a Hilbert space and it is the one
that is most commonly used in the study of PDEs.
    Thus, we can find weak solutions to the Laplace equation, which will lay in
H (Ω), instead of C 2 (Ω). We can do it for the heat equation as well, and the
  1

solutions will lay in L2 (R+ ) ⊗ H 1 (Ω).
    Now, the question that always arises here is: why do we need this spaces for?
Can’t we just work with smooth functions? The answer comes with the Lions-
Lax-Milgram theorem [171], which states that, for a large class of PDEs, the weak
solutions are unique and can be found by minimizing a functional.

 Theorem 2.4 Let H be a Hilbert space and V a normed space. Let A : H ×V →
 R be a bilinear continuous form. Then, the following are equivalent:
    • There exists a constant c > 0 such that:
                              inf             sup       |A(v, h)| ≥ c.
                          v∈V ∥v∥V =1 h∈H∥h∥H ≤1


     • For each continuous linear functional f ∈ V ∗ , there exist a unique h ∈
       H such that:
                                A(v, h) = f (v) ∀v ∈ V.

     This means that a bilinear continuous form A is coercive if and only if the
 associated linear functional f has a unique weak solution in H.


Proof. The proof of this theorem is long and technical, needing auxiliary results
such as the Riesz representation theorem and the original Babuŝka-Lax-Milgram
theorem. For a complete proof, see [97][Section 6.2] and [171].            q.e.d.

    This functional is normally defined in the Sobolev space, and it is not guar-
anteed to be well defined in the space of smooth functions.
2.3 From Continuous to Discrete                                                       43

    Nonetheless, this may just seem a mathematical workaround to show exis-
tence and uniqueness of the solution, as we can always come back to the smooth
functions to find the solution. This is due to a series of inequalities that show
how some Sobolev spaces are contained in Lebesgue spaces of higher order and,
when this order is high enough, those are embedded in the spaces of continuous
functions. We can summarize this as follows:
 Theorem 2.5 Given n ∈ N, consider p ∈ [1, n) and call p∗ the Sobolev con-
                               np
 jugate of p, defined as p∗ = n−p  ( p1∗ = p1 + n1 ). For γ ∈ [0, 1), we define the
 Hölder space C 0,γ (Ω) as the space of functions that are γ-Hölder continuous
 on the closure of Ω.
     Let Ω ⊆ Rn be a a domain (with Lipschitz boundary if it has one). Then:
     • If p < n, then W 1,p (Ω) ,→ Lp (Ω), so that for any u ∈ W 1,p (Ω), there
                                        ∗


       exists a constant C > 0 such that ∥u∥Lp∗ (Ω) ≤ C∥Du∥Lp (Ω) , for Du the
       weak derivative of u.
     • If p > n, then, for γ = 1 − np , we have that W 1,p (Ω) ,→ C 0,γ (Ω),
       so that for any u ∈ W 1,p (Ω), there exists a constant C > 0 such that
       ∥u∥C γ (Ω) ≤ C∥Du∥Lp (Ω) .


Proof. This theorem groups several important results of Functional Analysis and
theory of PDEs, such as the Sobolev Embedding Theorem, the Rellich-Kondrachov
Theorem or the Gagliardo-Nirenberg inequality. For a complete proof, see [97][Sec-
tion 5.6] for the case of bounded domain and [103] for the case of Rn .    q.e.d.

    The power of this theorem is that, once we have a weak solution to the PDE,
we know through bootstrapping techniques that it is, in fact, smooth, so it is a
classical solution to the PDE. A very complete idea of all these techniques can
be found in [72], although they are focused in a more general case where the
derivatives are fractional.
    The way of proceeding is as follows. Suppose we have a weak solution u ∈
W (Ω) to the heat equation, then we can use the Sobolev Embedding Theorem
   1,p
                      ∗                            p∗
to show that u ∈ Lp (Ω). Specifically, ∂u  ∂t ∈ L (Ω), so, by the same equation,
           ∗
Du ∈ Lp (Ω) too. Thus, we can apply this process iteratively until p∗ > n,
which implies that u ∈ C 0,γ (Ω) for some γ > 0. Again, the function fulfilling
the equation implies that the function is even in C 1 . Again, using the PDE we
can finally arrive that the function is in fact in C ∞ , so it is a classical solution to
the heat equation.
        44                                                Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

             We also have to note a very important property of the heat equation in certain
        situation, that it is the conservation of mass. This means that, if we integrate the
        function u over the spatial domain Ω, then this integral is constant in time. This
        is true if the domain Ω is the full space Rn or if the boundary conditions are such
        that the flux of the function u through the boundary is zero. Nonetheless, it is not
        true in bounded domains for certain boundary conditions. To see a discussion on
        this topic, see [76] and the references therein.
             Now that we know that classical solutions exist to the heat equation, let’s see
        how we can find them.

2.3.2   Classic solutions to the Heat Equation
        Typically, the heat equation has different solutions depending on the boundary
        conditions imposed on the spatial domain Ω.
           Nonetheless, we can explain the general method to find solutions using Kernel
        methods. The idea is to find a function K : R+ × Ω × Ω → R, called the heat
        kernel, such that the solution to the heat equation can be expressed as:
                                           Z
                                u(t, x) =     K(t, x, y)u(0, y)dy,                 (2.13)
                                             Ω

        where u(0, y) is the initial condition of the problem.
            For doing so, we decompose the Laplacian operator ∆ using its eigenfunc-
        tions and eigenvalues. The Laplacian is a self-adjoint operator, so it has a com-
        plete set of orthonormal eigenfunctions {ϕi }∞  i=1 in L (Ω), with corresponding
                                                                2

        eigenvalues {λi }i=1 .
                          ∞

            Thus, we can write the kernel as:
                                                 ∞
                                                 X
                                 K(t, x, y) =          e−λi t ϕi (x)ϕi (y),             (2.14)
                                                 i=1

        where the sum converges in L2 (Ω) for every t > 0. We can write this solution in
        a more compact way, saying K = e−t∆ .
        ■   Example 2.12 One easy situation is to calculate the heat kernel for the case of
        Ω = Rn , as the manifold does not have any boundary conditions.
