            This shows the close relation between the diffusion equation and the expo-
        nential function, as the heat kernel is a Gaussian function that decays over time.
        This fundamental property of the heat equation, shows both that the diffusion
        process is smoothing, as it decays the initial condition over time until arriving to
        0, and that it is instantaneous, as any point in the space gets affected by any kind
        on initial condition, no matter how far it is, as long as the time is positive.

2.3.3   Functions on Graphs
        What does it mean for a function to be continuous on a graph? In this context, a
        function is defined on the vertices of the graph and maps them to a field K, which
        is typically the real numbers R or the complex numbers C, and we will assume
        R for simplicity. Thus, a function on a graph is defined as:

                                                   f :V →R                                            (2.17)

            Analogous to the Hilbert space L2 in the continuous setting, we define an
        l2 (V ) space over graphs.  Here, the function is defined over a countable set of
        vertices, and its squared sum must be convergent:
                                                   X
                                         ∥f ∥2 =         |f (v)|2 < ∞.                                (2.18)
                                                   v∈V

            This allows us to define a norm for such functions, similar to the continuous
        case. Moreover, this can be defined for any p ∈ [1, ∞), leading to the definition
        of the lp (V ) Banach spaces, with a similar norm definition.
            However, a notable difference arises: the inclusion relations between function
        spaces are reversed. Consequently, bootstrapping techniques cannot be applied
        since there are no inherently continuous functions defined at discrete vertices.
            Again, we will keep using the l2 (V ) space, as it has an inner product defined:
                                                     X
                                          ⟨f, g⟩ =         f (v)g(v).                                 (2.19)
                                                     v∈V



             R   For using norms and inner products, it is usually needet to specify in which
                 space are we working, as functions might be in different spaces at the same
                 time and these definitions might not be equivalent. Yet, in this thesis we
                 will always work with the l2 (V ) space, so we will not specify it explicitly
                 and will use the notation ∥·∥= ∥·∥l2 (V ) and ⟨·, ·⟩ = ⟨·, ·⟩l2 (V ) , and equally
2.3 From Continuous to Discrete                                                           47

        if the functions are defined in the edges, so pertain to the space l2 (E).
    Given the lack of a traditional spatial structure, how can we define deriva-
tives on a graph? In continuous space, the derivative of a function represents its
variation along a specific direction.
    In graphs, a natural extension of this idea is to consider the edges emanating
from a given vertex as defining possible “directions”. Therefore, the operator that
quantifies variation at a vertex along a specific direction should be a function
mapping vertices to edges:

                                      D : V → E.                                       (2.20)

    But this is directly the definition of the incidence matrix of the graph, given
by Definition 2.3. Given a function f : V → R, we see that Bf is a function on
the edges defined by:

                (Bf )(e) = f (v2 ) − f (v1 ),     for e = (v1 , v2 ) ∈ E.              (2.21)

    So, we can see the incidence matrix as a discrete version of the derivative
operator, which measures the variation of the function f along the edges of the
graph.
    The question now is how can we define the divergence operator for this case.
Here, the adjoint is a much easier operation, as it is just the transpose of the
incidence matrix. Thus, div = B T is a function defined on the vertices of the
graph, and measures how vertex get affected from their incident edges.
    Finally, let’s define the graph Laplacian operator in undirected graphs.
  Definition 2.33 Given an undirected graph G = (V, E), the Laplacian opera-
  tor is a linear operator L : l2 (V ) → l2 (V ) defined as:
                                         X
                          (Lf )(v) =            (f (v) − f (w)).                     (2.22)
                                       w∈N (v)


  where N (v) is the set of neighbors of v.


   R    This graph Laplacian operator might be called also classical, usual or com-
        binatory Laplacian, depending on the literatute. This is due to the exis-
        tence of different operators which will be defined later, which in other
        works might be called Laplacian as well. Along this thesis, when we refer
        to Laplacian we will be referring to this operator, unless otherwise stated.
   This operator is easy to interpretate, as it compares the value of the function
48                                            Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

at a vertex with the values of its neighbors. This means that it can be seen as a
discrete version of the Laplacian operator, which measures the variation of the
function in all directions or, in this case, in all the edges connected to each vertex.
    Regarding the matrix representation of the Laplacian operator, we can easily
rearange the terms to see that it is given by the following expression:

                                    L = K − A,                                   (2.23)

where K is the diagonal matrix of the degrees of the vertices and A is the adja-
cency matrix of the graph.
   Nonetheless, this is not the only way to represent the Laplacian operator. Al-
though the incidence matrix might have some definition problems for undirected
graphs, as it is needed to impose an orientation to the edges, we can express the
Laplacian operator in terms of it.
  Definition 2.34 Given an undirected graph G = (V, E), the Laplacian opera-
  tor is a linear operator L : l2 (V ) → l2 (V ) defined as:

                                     L = BB T .                                (2.24)

    This way of writing the Laplacian operator is another similarity with the con-
tinuous case, as it is the adjoint of the derivative operator. On the other hand, we
have to see that this operator is the same as the previous one and, most impor-
tant, that it is well defined and does not depend on the orientation chosen for the
edges.

 Theorem 2.6 The Laplacian operator defined as L = BB T is well defined and
 the same operator as the one defined as L = K − A.



Proof. Let’s see that the two definitions are equivalent after rearranging the terms.
    Consider two neighboring vertices v, w ∈ V, v ∼ w, connected through the
edge e = (u, v). Given a function f : V → R, we see that B T f applied to v is a
vector of size |E| with all the entries equal to 0 except for the ones corresponding
to the edges incident to v. In particular, the entry corresponding to the edge e is
equal to σ(e)(f (v) − f (w)), where σ(e) represents the direction of the edge e
(σ(e) equals 1 if the node is chosen to point towards v and −1 if it points away
from it). Now, using the definition of B taking into account the orientation of the
        2.3 From Continuous to Discrete                                                              49

        edges, we can see that BB T f applied to v is given by:
                                               X
                            (BB T f )(v) =              σ(e)2 (f (v) − f (w)) =
                                              w∈N (v)
                   X                         X                                    X
               =         (f (v) − f (w)) =         (f (v) − f (w)) = kv f (v) −         f (w),
                   e∈E                       e∈E                                  e∈E

        where kv is the degree of the vertex v.This is exactly the definition of the Laplacian
        operator given in matrix form by L = K − A. We have used that, independently
        on the choice of σ, σ(e)2 = 1 for all edges e ∈ E, so the result is independent of
        the orientation of the edges.                                                   q.e.d.

           This operator is the one used to model the classical diffusion equation on
        graphs:
                                       ∂u
                                           = −νLu.                              (2.25)
                                        ∂t
            As for the continuous case, ν > 0 is the diffusion coefficient, which controls
        the speed of the diffusion process and we will set ν = 1 for simplicity, as it only
        affects to the time scale of the process.
            Thus, to solve this equation, as well as to understand what are the properties
        of a diffusion process on a graph, we need to understand the properties of the
        Laplacian operator. We will see them separated by the two fields of mathematics
        that converge in this operator: Linear Algebra, to study the matrix properties of
        the operator; and Dynamical Systems, to study the properties of the solutions.

2.3.4   Classical Laplacian operator: Linear Algebra
        In this section, we will focus on the properties of the Laplacian operator from a
        linear algebra perspective, studying its matrix representation and the properties
        of the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the operator.
            First we will see a very basic and easy property: The Laplacian operator is a
        linear operator.

         Theorem 2.7 The Laplacian operator is a linear operator, i.e., for any f, g ∈
         l2 (V ) and α, β ∈ R, we have that:

                                     L(αf + βg) = αLf + βLg.                                     (2.26)


        Proof. This is straightforward from the definition of the Laplacian operator. Let
50                                                Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

f, g ∈ l2 (V ) and α, β ∈ R. Then, we have that:
                               X
      L(αf + βg)(v) =              ((αf (v) + βg(v)) − (αf (w) + βg(w)))
                            w∈N (v)
                                X                            X
                          =α          (f (v) − f (w)) + β         (g(v) − g(w))
                               w∈N (v)                      w∈N (v)

                          = αLf (v) + βLg(v).

                                                                                  q.e.d.

     This theorem is important as it allows us to use the properties of linear op-
erators, which is important as it allows to represent the Laplacian operators as
a matrix even in the case of infinite graphs. Some of this representations, as for
example L = K − A, needs the graph to be locally finite to be used, mean-
ing that each vertex has a finite number of neighbors. However, both L(f ) =
    w∈N (v) (f (v) − f (w)) and L = BB are well defined for any graph, even if it
                                          T
P

is infinite, as long as the function f is in l2 (V ).
     Now, Let’s dive into the central area of linear algebra, the spectral theory.
Eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the Laplacian operator are directly related to
both the structure of the graph and the diffusion process defined on it.
     First, we will see that the Laplacian is positive semi-definite, meaning that it
has non-negative eigenvalues.

 Theorem 2.8 The Laplacian operator is positive semi-definite, i.e., for any f ∈
 l2 (V ), we have that:
                                         ⟨f, Lf ⟩ ≥ 0.                        (2.27)

    This means that all the eigenvalues of the Laplacian operator are non-
 negative.


Proof. This is straightforward from the definition 2.34 of the Laplacian operator.
Let f ∈ l2 (V ). Then, we have that:

             ⟨f, Lf ⟩ = ⟨f, BB T f ⟩ = ⟨B T f, B T f ⟩ = ∥B T f ∥2 ≥ 0,           (2.28)

which is non-negative as it is the square of the norm of a vector.
   This directly implies that the eigenvalues of the Laplacian operator are non-
negative, as the inner product ⟨f, Lf ⟩ is equal to λ∥f ∥2 for any eigenpair (λ, f )
2.3 From Continuous to Discrete                                                   51

of the Laplacian operator. Thus, we have that λ ≥ 0 for all eigenvalues λ of the
Laplacian operator.                                                       q.e.d.

    If we call (λi , vi )ni=1 the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the Laplacian oper-
ator, ordered increasingly so that λ1 ≤ λ2 ≤ . . ., we can see that λi ≥ 0 for all
1 ≤ i ≤ n. Moreover, we can see that the first eigenvalue is always zero and the
implications of this fact.

Proposition 2.1 The first eigenvalue of the Laplacian operator is zero, i.e., λ1 = 0,
and its corresponding eigenvector is the constant function v1 = ⃗1. Moreover, the
multiplicity of this eigenvalue is equal to the number of connected components
of the graph.


Proof. First, suppose a simple graph. Define v = ⃗1 and recall the definition of
degree as ki = j∈N (i) 1 = nj=1 Aij . Then, we have directly that:
                P            P


               Lv = Kv − Av = K⃗1 − A⃗1 = K − K = 0 = 0 · v.,                  (2.29)

which implies by the definition of eigenpair that λ1 = 0 and v1 = ⃗1.
    Now, suppose that the graph is not connected. Then, we can write the graph
as a disjoint union of connected components G1 , G2 , . . . , Gk . For each compo-
nent Gi , we can define the function vi as 1 on the vertices of Gi and zero on the
rest of the vertices. Then, we have that:

                  Lvi = Kvi − Avi = Kvi − 0 = Kvi = 0 · vi .                   (2.30)

It is also easy to see that, for i ̸= j, 1 ≤ i, j ≤ k, the eigenvectors vi and vj are
orthogonal (⟨vi , vj ⟩ = 0), as they are zero on the vertices of the other component.
     This implies that for 1 ≤ i ≤ k, we have that λi = 0 and that the associated
eigenvectors are the constant functions on each connected component.
     Finally, suppose there is another eigenvector w associated to the eigenvalue
0, i.e., Lw = 0. Then, we directly obtain that:
                                      n X
                                      X n
                   0 = ⟨w, Lw⟩ =                Aij wi2 − wi Aij wj =
                                      i=1 j=1
               n                                       n
                                                     X 1
               X       1
           =             Aij (wi2 + wj2 − 2wi wj ) =     Aij (wi − wj )2 ,
                       2                               2
               i,j=1                                 i,j=1
52                                               Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

which implies that wi = wj for all connected nodes i ∼ j, meaning by definition
of connected component that w is constant on each connected component of the
graphs. Thus, w is a linear combination of the eigenvectors vi , so w is not a new
eigenvector. Hence, the multiplicity of the eigenvalue 0 is equal to the number of
connected components of the graph.                                           q.e.d.

     This result is one of the most remarkable results of this classical Laplacian on
graphs, and will play an important role later on. Also, it is another reason apart
from the intuitive simplicity to work on connected graphs. This way, we know
now that the first eigenvalue of the Laplacian is zero and we can talk about λ2 ,
its second always positive eigenvalue, which is called the algebraic connectivity.
     We have to name the Courant-Fisher principle [145][Theorem 4.2.6] to give
a formula for this value:
 Theorem 2.9 — Courant-Fisher min-max theorem for hermitian matrices. Let
 A be a hermitian matrix of size n with eigenvalues λ1 ≤ · · · ≤ λn . Let Sk ⊆ Cn
 denote any subspace of Cn of dimension k. Then:

      λk = maxn        min    ⟨x, Ax⟩ =          min            max       ⟨x, Ax⟩   (2.31)
             Sk ⊆C x⊥Sk ∥x∥=1              Sn−k+1 ⊆Cn x⊥Sk ∥x∥=1



 Corollary 2.1 The algebraic connectivity λ2 of a connected graph G = (V, E)
 with n nodes is given by:
                                                 X
                      λ2 = 2n       min                   (xi − xj )2 .             (2.32)
                                ⟨x,⃗1⟩=0∥x∥=1
                                                (i,j)∈E


   This representation is important as it characterizes the second eigenvector as
well, usually called the Fiedler vector. This formula says that such vector has to
                                                                                 n
have entries of different sign due to the space in which it is taking: ⟨x, ⃗1⟩ =
                                                                                 P
                                                                                   xi
                                                                                     i=1
implies that, either x = ⃗0 or x has entries of opposing sign, and the first option is
excluded by ∥x∥= 1. Moreover, it says that it is the least squares minimum of the
functional (i,j)∈E (xi − xj )2 , so it minimizes the distance between neighboring
           P

nodes. Thus, we can use this vector to divide the network into two subgraphs
(corresponding to the non-negative and negative entries of the vector, respec-
tively), which minimize the distance between them. The proof of the later, as
well as that the two subgraphs are connected, by Fiedler is what gives his name
to the vector.
2.3 From Continuous to Discrete                                                   53

 Theorem 2.10 — Fiedler [102]. Let G = (V, E) be a connected graph and let
 λ2 be its algebraic connectivity. Then, for the second eigenvector v2 of the
 Laplacian operator L, we can define the sets:

        S+ = {v ∈ V : v2 (v) ≥ 0},       S− = {v ∈ V : v2 (v) < 0}.          (2.33)

 Then, the subgraphs induced by these sets, G+ = (S+ , E ∩ (S+ × S+ )) and
 G− = (S− , E ∩ (S− × S− )), are connected.

Proof. The proof of this theorem can be found, apart from the original paper,
in [102], in the lecture notes of [238], where they offer the proof generalized to
the case of k-th eigenvector.                                               q.e.d.

    We can use this theorem to understand better the structure of the graph.
Graph clustering is a rich area that tries to divide a given graph into a set of dis-
joint subgraphs attending to different properties of the network. In this area, we
have seen that the pair (λ2 , v2 ) can be used to divide the graphs into its two most
self-connected subgraphs, and from that idea several new methods have been
developed in what it is known today as Spectral graph clustering. For instance,
along the same lines of these last theorems, it can be proved that the k-th least
eigenvector can be used to decompose the graph in k different subgraphs. Each
subgraph can be seen as a community, depending on the nature of the network
data, which is why these kind of methods have a great applicability.
    Finally, we can establish some bounds on the eigenvalues. Here we will prove
the classical and easier to obtain ones, as well as cite the proofs of some sharper
ones.

2.3.5   Classical Laplacian operator: Dynamical Systems

        We will focus now on solving the diffusion equation on any given graph:

                                  ∂u
                                     = −Lu;       u(0, x) = u0 (x).                     (2.37)
                                  ∂t

           The first difference to notice is that in this case we don’t need to impose any
        boundary conditions, as the graph is discrete and does not have any boundary.
        This means that the solution will be defined for all t ≥ 0 and all x ∈ V .
            Moreover, we can see that this equation is no longer a PDE but a system of
        ODEs, as the Laplacian operator is in this case a matrix operator. Moreover, as the
        coefficients of the system are constant, we only need to remember the definition
        of the matrix exponential to solve it:
          Definition 2.35 The matrix exponential of a matrix M is defined as:

                                                  ∞
                                                  X Mn
                                           eM =              .                        (2.38)
                                                        n!
                                                  n=0


            This definition is well defined for any matrix and it is the unique solution to
        the equation:
                                    d Mt
                                      e = M eM t ; eM 0 = I.                          (2.39)
                                   dt
        Hence, we can directly write the solution to the diffusion equation as:

                                       u(t, x) = e−tL u0 (x).                           (2.40)


            Notice that this solution is similar to the one we found for the heat equation in
        the continuous case, where we found the heat kernel K = e−t∆ and the solution
        was given by u(t, x) = Ω K(t, x, y)u(0, y)dy for the initial condition u(0, y).
                                   R

        Nonetheless, the first difference here is that here we obtain that any solution is
        well defined if u0 ∈ l2 (V ), as the matrix exponential is well defined for any matrix
        and there is no need to check for continuity or differentiability of the solution, so
56                                             Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

it is enough to check:

              ∥u(t)∥2 = ∥e−tL u0 ∥2 = ∥e−tL ∥2 ∥u0 ∥2 = ∥u0 ∥2 < ∞.            (2.41)

Observe that this also implies that the norm of the solution is constant in time, as
the matrix exponential is a norm-preserving operator. This is a very important
property, as it means that the diffusion process does not change the total amount
of the function over the graph. It can be interpreted as the total concrentration of
the substance being diffused over the graph is conserved, which is a very intuitive
property of the diffusion process.
    Even though the specific solutions will change depending not only on u0 , but
generally on the structure of the graph, we can use the property in Proposition 2.1
to see a general property of the solutions.

 Theorem 2.12 Let G = (V, E) be a connected graph with Laplacian operator
 L and let u(t, x) = e−tL u0 (x) be the solution to the diffusion equation on the
 graph with initial condition u0 ∈ l2 (V ). Then, asymptotically we obtain that:

                                          ⃗1
                    lim u(t, x) = P              ,   for all x ∈ V.          (2.42)
                    t→∞                   u0 (y)
                                    y∈V


Proof. Let (λi , vi )ni=1 be the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the Laplacian oper-
ator L, ordered increasingly so that λ1 ≤ λ2 ≤ · · · ≤ λn . Then, we can write the
solution as:
                                             Xn

                                             i=1

   Then, as we have assumed that the graph is connected, we have that λ1 = 0
and v1 = ⃗1 by the Proposition 2.1.
   Also, as the eigenvalues are increasing and non-negative, we obtain that for
long times:
                    lim e−tL u0 = e−tλ1 ⟨u0 , v1 ⟩v1 = ⟨u0 , ⃗1⟩⃗1.      (2.44)
                   t→∞

    Finally, we can see that ⟨u0 , ⃗1⟩ =       x∈V u0 (x), so we obtain the desired
                                            P

result.                                                                        q.e.d.

    This Theorem is very important, as it tells us that the diffusion process on a
connected graph will always converge to a constant function, which is the aver-
age of the initial condition over the whole graph. This is a very intuitive result,
      2.4 Summary on similarities and differences between the continuous and
      discrete Laplacian operators                                                      57
      as it tells us that the diffusion process will always tend to equalize the values of
      the function over the graph, the same way as it happens in the continuous heat
      equation.

         R    Using the same approach as this theorem, we can see that the convergence
              to this constant function is exponentially fast, as the second eigenvalue
              λ2 is always positive. This means that the convergence rate is given by
              e−λ2 t , so the larger the algebraic connectivity of the graph, the faster the
              convergence.
         Another very important property of this diffusion process that it is used in a
      broad range of applications is the fact that the this equation preserves the total
      mass of the function over the graph.

2.4   Summary on similarities and differences between the
      continuous and discrete Laplacian operators
      Along the past section we have seen how to study the diffusion equation both on
      graphs and on continuous spaces, remarking how the properties of the Laplacian
      operator are key to understand the process. In this section, we will summarize the
      main similarities and differences between the continuous and discrete Laplacian
      operators, as well as the diffusion equations defined on them.
58                                          Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

Similarities between continuous and discrete diffusion settings
     • Laplacian definition: Laplacian operators can be defined as the product
       of an operator and its adjoint.
     • Positive semi-definiteness: Both Laplacian operators are positive semi-
       definite, implying that they have non-negative eigenvalues.
     • Self-adjointness: Both operators are self-adjoint.
     • Isotropy: The Laplacian operator is in both cases isotropic, meaning that
       it does not depend on the direction of the edges in the graph or the coordi-
       nates in the continuous case.
     • Spectral solution: In both cases, solutions can be expressed via spectral
       decomposition:                  X
                                u(t) =     e−λi t ⟨u0 , ϕi ⟩ϕi
                                        i

       with eigenfunctions (or eigenvectors) ϕi and associated eigenvalues λi of
       the Laplacian.
     • Exponential form: The evolution operator in both systems is given by
       e−t∆ or e−tL acting on the initial state.
     • Steady state: On connected domains/graphs, the steady state is a constant
       function equal to the average of the initial values.
     • Convergence rate: Solutions converge to an steady state at a speed deter-
       mined by the smallest nonzero eigenvalue λ2 .
2.4 Summary on similarities and differences between the continuous and
discrete Laplacian operators                                                    59



Differences between continuous and discrete diffusion settings

                        Continuous Setting          Graph-Based (Discrete)
     Domain              Continuous domain            Discrete vertex set V

   Gradient /

   Divergence

2.5     Extending the diffusion operator to generalizations of
        graphs
        We have presented the classical Laplacian operator, which is only defined for
        undirected graphs, and compare to its continuous counterpart, as one is based
        on the other. However, in Section 2.2 we extended the definition of graphs to
        include some other representations of real world systems that are not necessarily
        undirected. In this section, we will see how to extend the definition of the Lapla-
        cian operator to these new representations, so that we can use the same diffusion
        equation to model the diffusion process on them.

2.5.1   Directed graphs
        Extending the definition of the Laplacian operator to directed graphs is straight-
        forward if we use the Definition 2.22. The main problem is to choose which we
        will use as degree of the vertex for the diagonal elements. This way, we can define
        the in-Laplacian and the out-Laplacian operators as:
          Definition 2.36 Given a directed graph G = (V, E), the in-Laplacian operator
          is a linear operator Lin : l2 (V ) → l2 (V ) defined as:

                                          Lin = Kin − A,                             (2.46)

          where Kin is the diagonal matrix of the in-degrees of the vertices and A is the
          adjacency matrix of the graph.
              Similarly, the out-Laplacian operator is a linear operator Lout : l2 (V ) →
          l (V ) defined as:
           2

                                        Lout = Kout − A,                             (2.47)

          where Kout is the diagonal matrix of the out-degrees of the vertices.

            This change is not trivial, and induces an important change in the properties
        of the diffusion equation on directed graphs as we will see. Fortunately, we can
        see how they are related [57].

         Theorem 2.14 Let G = (V, E) be a directed graph and let Lin and Lout be the
         in-Laplacian and out-Laplacian operators, respectively.
             Define the reversed graph GR = (V, E R ) as the graph obtained by revers-
         ing the direction of all the edges in G. Then, we have that:
2.5 Extending the diffusion operator to generalizations of graphs                       61


                                  Lin (G) = Lout (GR )T .                           (2.48)

Proof. This is straightforward as reversing the direction of the edges in a directed
graph is equivalent to transposing the adjacency matrix of the graph. Thus, we
have that kin (G) = kout (GR ) and vice versa. So:

      Lout (GR )T = (Kout (GR ) − A(GR ))T = Kout (GR )T − A(GR )T =
                             = Kin (G) − A(G) = Lin (G).

                                                                                     q.e.d.

    One easy property of these operators is that, as their diagonals are given by
the sum of the columns or rows of the adjacency matrix, they have (0, ⃗1) as an
eigenpair, but the in-Laplacian will have it as a right eigenpair while the out-
Laplacian will have it as a left eigenpair. This is the crucial difference between
the properties shown for these operators.
Proposition 2.2 Let G = (V, E) be a directed graph and let Lin and Lout be the
in-Laplacian and out-Laplacian operators, respectively. Consider the diffusion
equations mediated by these operators. Then:
   1. The diffusion equation mediated by the in-Laplacian operator Lin con-
      verges as t → ∞ to a constant function.
   2. The diffusion equation mediated by the out-Laplacian operator Lout fulfills
      that                   u0 (v) for all t ≥ 0.
           P             P
               u(t, v) =
           v∈V              v∈V


Proof. The first point uses that the in-Laplacian operator has (0, ⃗1) as a right
eigenpair, so we can see that:

                                     ⃗1˙ = Lin⃗1 = 0,                                (2.49)

so ⃗1 is an steady state and, as 0 is the first eigenvalue, we can see that the solution
will converge to it as t → ∞ using the spectral decomposition:
                 n
                                         t→∞
                 X
   e−tLin u0 =         e−tλi ⟨wi , u0 ⟩vi −−−→ e−tλ1 ⟨w1 , u0 ⟩v1 = ⟨w1 , u0 ⟩⃗1.    (2.50)
                 i=1


    The second point uses that the out-Laplacian operator has (⃗1, 0) as a left
62                                              Chapter 2. Foundational concepts




Figure 2.16: Difference between the diffusion process on a directed balanced
cycle graph and the underlying undirected cycle graph. Although the initial
state and the steady one are the same, the temporal dynamics are different.


eigenpair, so we can see that:
         X ˙        X            X
          u(t, v) =   u̇(t, v) =   Lout u(t, v) = ⃗1T Lout u(t) = 0,                   (2.51)
         v∈V           v∈V            v∈V


     which implies that the evolution of       v∈V u(t, v) is constant in time.
                                            P
                                                                                   q.e.d.


    This theorem briefly justifies what Airlie Chapman distinguishes in her book [57]
as three different protocols (equations) on directed graphs:
     • The consensus protocol, mediated by the in-Laplacian operator, in which
       all agents (nodes) converge to the same point (state value).
     • The advection protocol, mediated by the out-Laplacian operator, in which
       the total amount of the particles is conserved and moves through the graph
       following the direction of the edges.
     • The diffusion protocol, occuring when both Laplacians are equal and both
       situations occur at the same time, so that the particles move through the
       graph and converge to the same state.

     R    Notice that, for the diffusion protocol to occur and both Laplacians to
          be equal, it is sufficient but not necessary that the graph is undirected.
          There are graphs called balanced directed graphs, which are character-
          ized for having the same in-degree and out-degree for each vertex, so that
          Kin = Kout , which makes both Laplacians equal while producing a dif-
          ferent dynamic from the undirected case.
        2.5 Extending the diffusion operator to generalizations of graphs                            63

2.5.2   Hypergraphs
        There are a few different ways to extend Laplacian operators to hypergraphs,
        using the two different definitions of Laplacians 2.34 and 2.22, which are no longer
        equivalent for hyperedges. The point in common to preserve is that they coincide
        with the graph Laplacian for the hyperedges of size 2.
            We are going to focus on the definition through incidence matrices, as it is
        the most used and usually defined for simplicial complexes.
          Definition 2.37 Let G = (V, E) be a hypergraph. Let Bk be the incidence
          matrix of k-hyperedges into (k + 1)-hyperedges, i.e., Bk ∈ R|Ek |×|Ek+1 | is the
          matrix such that Bk (e, f ) = 1 if e ⊂ f and 0 otherwise. Then, the kth-order
          Laplacian operator of the hypergraph is defined as:

                                                 T
                                      Lk = Bk+1 Bk+1 + BkT Bk .                              (2.52)

             Generally we will refer to the first term as Lup
                                                           k and the second term as
          Llow
           k   .


           R    This definition is usually used for simplicial complexes as, contrary to a
                general hypergraph, it has all the Laplacians from 0 up to the maximum
                order of the simplices, due to the inclusion property of a simplex. Nonethe-
                less, there are hypergraphs which may have hyperedges of size 3 but not
                of size 2.


           R    Notice that, due to the definition of the incidence matrix of different orders,
                the Laplacian operator of order 0, L0 , is the classical Laplacian operator
                of the underlying graph, as it is the incidence matrix of the vertices into
                the edges. This is why this is considered a good extension of the Laplacian
                operator to hypergraphs, but higher order Laplacians do not preserve the
                same properties, such as being always positive semi-definite or having the
                first eigenvalue equal to zero. One of the main problems of this definition
                is that incidence matrix, to be well defined, need to impose an orientation
                to the simplices, and this translates on the Laplacians of order higher than
                0 may vary depending on the chosen orientation.

            These Laplacians are usually called Hodge Laplacians as they are can be used
        to do the Hodge decomposition of a simplicial complex, which is a generalization
        of the classical Hodge decomposition of differential forms on manifolds. This
        means that the incidence matrices fulfill:

                                    Bk Bk−1 = 0,        T
                                                       Bk−1 BkT = 0,                              (2.53)
64                                            Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

which translate to the reltions:

                               Im(Lup
                                   k ) ⊆ Ker(Lk ),
                                              low
                                                                                  (2.54)
                                             up
                              Im(Llow
                                  k ) ⊆ Ker(Lk ).                                 (2.55)

   These set of relations are not just a curiosity, but they allow to study the co-
homology of the simplicial complex, which has to do with the topology of the
underlying space, specifically with the number of holes of different dimensions.
This is a very important area of study in algebraic topology, and can be sum-
marized in the following extension of the Hodge theorem [129] for simplicial
complexes:

 Theorem 2.15 — Hodge theorem for simplicial complexes. Let G = (V, E) be
 a simplicial complex and let Lk be the k-th order Laplacian operator of the
 complex. Then:
                              Hk (G) = Ker(Lk ),                       (2.56)

 where Hk (G) is the k-th homology group of the simplicial complex G.

    This means that studying the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of the Laplacian
operator of a simplicial complex allows to study the topology of the underlying
space. This is a very powerful tool for several applications, one of them being
the hyperedge prediction problem, which is useful in sociology of friendship net-
works [28].
    We have to finish this section by mentioning that this is not the only way
to extend the Laplacian operator to hypergraphs, and there are other definitions
that have been proposed in the literature for specific hypergraphs. For instance,
if we have an s-uniform hypergraph, i.e., a hypergraph in which all hyperedges
have the same size s, we can define the Laplacian operator as: [63]
  Definition 2.38 Let G = (V, E) be an s-uniform hypergraph. Define the
  matrix K of complete graph as:
                      (
                       1, if x = [x1 i2 . . . ik−1 ] = [y1 i2 . . . ik−1 ] = y,
          K(x, y) =
                        0, otherwise.

  for any k-simplices x, y ∈ G.
        2.5 Extending the diffusion operator to generalizations of graphs                        65

               Then, the s-uniform Laplacian operator is defined as:

                                   Ls = D − A + ρ(K + (s − 1)I),                            (2.57)

          where D is the diagonal matrix of the degrees of the vertices, A is the adja-
          cency matrix of the hypergraph, ρ is the average degree of the hypergraph
          and I is the identity matrix.

           R     A simple graph is a 2-uniform hypergraph, and in this case the matrix K
                 is essentially a diagonal matrix of 1s, so we recover the classical Laplacian
                 operator.
            This Laplacian could be more suitable for certain applications related to dif-
        fusion processes, as it preserves the property of ⃗1 being always an eigenvector
        (associated with an eigenvalue ρ|V | instead of 0).

2.5.3   Gain graphs
        Defining Laplacians on gain graphs migh be tricky because of the difficulties we
        have already seen in defining the degree of a vertex in a gain graph. Nonetheless,
        there is some literature on the subject using Representation Theory of Groups [51].
            Luckily, we can restrict ourselves to the case of T-gain graphs, which is more
        common in the literature, and specifically to the signed graphs, for which it has
        been shown that its Laplacians have very useful application in real-world prob-
        lems. In this case, we can define two operators, using the absolute degree or the
        net degree of the vertex.
          Definition 2.39 Let G = (V, E, ϕ) be a T-gain graph. The repelling Laplacian
          operator is defined as:
                                         Lrepelling = Dabs − A,                             (2.58)

          where Dabs is the diagonal matrix of the absolute degrees of the vertices and
          A is the adjacency matrix of the gain graph. The opposing Laplacian operator
          is defined as:
                                     Lopposing = Dnet − A,                        (2.59)

          where Dnet is the diagonal matrix of the net degrees of the vertices.

            These two Laplacian operators show very different dynamics, so they are used
        for different purposes, specially for the specific case of signed graphs.
            For the opposing Laplacian, its main property is its relation with the balance
        of the graph:
66                                                   Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

 Theorem 2.16 Let G = (V, E, ϕ) be a signed graph and let Lopposing be the
 opposing Laplacian operator. Then, the following holds:
    • If the graph G is balanced, calling V1 and V2 the bipartite sets character-
      izing this balance, then the lowest eigenvalue of Lopposing is 0 and the
      corresponding eigenvector is ⃗1V1 − ⃗1V2 , where ⃗1Vi is the vector with 1s
      in the positions of the vertices in Vi and 0s elsewhere.
    • If the graph G is unbalanced, then the lowest eigenvalue of Lopposing is
      positive and the diffusion dynamics will converge to the state ⃗0 as t → ∞
      for any initial state.


Proof. To proof both claims, we have to rewrite the Laplacian as a cuadratic form:
                             n
                             X
          T
        x Lopposing x =             ((|Aik |) δij − Aij ) xi xj =                    (2.60)
                             i,j=1
                                 n
                             1  X
                         =               |Aij |(x2i + x2j − 2sign(Aij )xi xj ) =     (2.61)
                             2
                                 i,j=1
                                    n
                             1    X
                         =               |Aij |(xi − sign(Aij )xj )2 .               (2.62)
                             2
                                 i,j=1

This means that the Laplacian is positive semi-definite, as it is a sum of squares,
and it is zero if and only if xi = sign(Aij )xj for all i, j.
    Thus, for the first claim, consider x as in the statement. If the graph is bal-
anced, we can take xi and xj either to the same subset Vk or one form V1 and
the other from V2 . In the first case, we have that sign(Aij ) = 1, and by construc-
tion xi = xj , so the term is zero. In the second case, we have that sign(Aij ) =
−1,and by construction xi = −xj , so the term is zero. Thus, we have seen that
xi = ⃗1V1 − ⃗1V2 is a valid eigenvector with eigenvalue 0.
    For the second claim, we use that the Laplacian is positive semi-definite, to
just prove that 0 is not an eigenvalue. If it was, then it would exists a vector x such
that xT Lopposing x = 0. Then, as by hypothesis the graph is unbalanced, there
exists at least one cycle with an odd number of negative edges. WLOG, suppose
that the cycle is v1 , v2 , . . . , vk−1 , vk and suppose that all edges are positive except
the edge (vk , v1 ), which is negative. Then, we have that:

                              k−1
                              X
               T
              x Lopposing x =     (xvi − xvi+1 )2 + (xvk + xv1 )2 = 0.

     The last equality forces to all of the terms being zero. Focusing on the terms
in the sumatory, we have that x1 = x2 = . . . = xk−1 = xk , but the last term
being equal to 0 can only happen if xk = 0 = x1 , which contradicts that x is an
eigenvector. Thus, we have that 0 is not an eigenvalue of the Laplacian operator,
so it is positive definite. As the solution to the diffusion equation mediated by the
Laplacian operator is given by:

we have that all the eigenvalues are positive, so the solution will converge to 0
as t → ∞ for any initial condition u0 .                                    q.e.d.


   R     Note that this theorem states not only a sufficient but also a necessary
         condition of balance, meaning that the graph is balanced if and only if the
         lowest eigenvalue of the opposing Laplacian is 0 and the corresponding
         eigenvector is ⃗1V1 − ⃗1V2 .
   This theorem is interesting as it relates the long time dynamics of a diffusion
process on a signed graph with the balance of the graph. This means that, if we
have a signed graph and we want to know if it is balanced or not, we can use the
opposing Laplacian operator to check it.
   On the other hand, the repelling Laplacian has this name because it models a
more repulsive interaction, leading sometimes to non-convergent dynamics:

                             i∈V   i

       • If β < β0 , then the diffusion process converges to a constant function as
         t → ∞.
       • If β > β0 , then the diffusion process diverges and goes to ∞.
The proof of this theorem can be found in [232][Theorem 2].
   This Laplacian has been applied to measure the graphs bipolarization struc-

        ture, which is a trending topic in todays social media research[46].

2.5.4   Metaplexes
        Diffusion processes on metaplexes are a very recent area of study, nonetheless it
        is straightforward to extend any kind of operators to them. The reason is that
        an operator on a metaplex can be defined as a matrix operator with out-diagonal
        elements corresponding to the operator on the underlying graph and diagonal
        elements corresponding to the continuous operators on the metaplex. This can
        be summarized in the following definition:
          Definition 2.40 Let Υ = (V, E, ω, I, F) be a metaplex. A dynamical sys-
          tem on a metaplex is a tuple (H = {Hi : L2 (Ωi ) → L2 (Ωi )}, T = {Tij :
          L2 (dom(Fij )) → L2 (Im(Fij ))}) such that, for any u0 = ((u0 )i )ki=0 ∈ (L2 (Ωi ))ki=0 ,
          the initial value problem ∂t ui (t) = Hi (ui (t)), ui (0) = (u0 )i is well-posed.

            This means that we can define the Laplacian operator in a metaplex by defin-
        ing the operators Hi as the continuous Laplacian operator ∆ on the domain Ωi
        and the operators Tij are the equivalent to the discrete Laplacian operator, taking
        the difference between the values of the function at the sinks and sources of the
        different connected domains.


2.6     Is that all for simple graphs?
        As we have said, the study of the Laplacian operator in the continuous setting is
        a very active are of research. That does not mean that the heat equation in Rn is
        still studied today as we presented it here. The concept of Manifold generalized
        that of space, allowing for the study of heat equations on more complex geome-
        tries through an extension of the Laplacian operator, called the Laplace-Beltrami
        operator. Applied to the space of Rn , it can be also studied the effect of some met-
        ric g on the geometry of the space, which modifies the behaviour of the diffusion
        process.
            Can we do the same for graphs? The answer is yes, there are several exten-
        sions of the properties of the graph that produce new kind of Laplacian operators,
        new types of diffusion processes and new applications.
            Here we have to briefly mention to what we will call a Laplacian operator.
        Studying the literature, one may see different definitions of Laplacian operator
        on graphs. These definitions vary on which properties of the original combina-
        torial Laplacian operator presented in Section 2.3.3 are preserved. This is inter-
2.6 Is that all for simple graphs?                                                 69

esting from the point of view of applications: restricting the definition of which
operators are allowed in a problem allows you to ensure that the important con-
sequences of the Laplacian operator are preserved, so that the problem can be
solved using the same techniques as the original one.
    We will present here some of the more common definitions of Laplacian op-
erator on graphs to then explain to which of them we will refer to when we talk
about Laplacian operators in the rest of the thesis.
    The most common extension is given by the following definition[215]:
  Definition 2.41 Given a graph G = (V, E), an operator L : l2 (V ) → l2 (V ) is
  called a Laplacian operator if it fulfills the following properties:
      1. L is positive semi-definite, i.e., ⟨x, Lx⟩ ≥ 0 for all x ∈ l2 (V ).
      2. The rows in L sum to zero, i.e., v∈V Luv = 0 for all u ∈ V .
                                             P

      3. All the non-diagonal elements are non-positive, i.e., Luv ≤ 0 for all
         u ̸= v ∈ V .

   This definition allows for an operator whose dynamics converge to a constant
function for long times, and that can be decomposed as a difference K −A, where
A can be interpreted ad the adjacency matrix of a (possibly weighted) graph.
    A very similar definition is given in [71]:
  Definition 2.42 Given a graph G = (V, E), an operator L : l2 (V ) → l2 (V ) is
  called a Laplacian operator if it fulfills the following properties:
      1. L is symmetric, i.e., Luv = Lvu for all u, v ∈ V .
      2. L has finite non-positive off-diagonal elements, i.e., Luv ≤ 0 for all
         u ̸= v ∈ V .
      3. The row and column sums of L are zero, i.e., v∈V Luv = v∈V Lvu =
                                                        P              P

         0 for all u ∈ V .
      4. L is irreducible, i.e. there is no permutation of the vertices that makes
         L block diagonal.

    This definition is very similar to the previous one, as the three first condi-
tions are stronger and imply the positive semi-definiteness of the operator. It
adds, nonetheless, the condition of irreducibility, which implies that the associ-
ated graph is connected. This is a very common scenario to assume in order to
simplify the calculations, as if the graph is disconnected, you can decompose the
problem into smaller problems on each connected component, which are inde-
pendent.
    It is also important to remark that, on the definition of positive semi-definiteness,
70                                               Chapter 2. Foundational concepts

it is assumed that the matrix is symmetric. Nonetheless, it can be defined only by
the condition of having a non-negative quadratic form. In this case, the second
definition implies the first one, but not vice versa, as there are non-symmetric
positive semi-definite matrices with zero row sum and non-positive off-diagonal
elements. Moreover, this definition of positive semidefiniteness also distinguishes
the definition for the zero column sum. This would mean that the first definition
allows for Laplacians whose dynamics do not conserve the mass.
    From this second definition, there is a slight change to arrive to the one given
by [19]:
  Definition 2.43 Given a graph G = (V, E), an operator L : l2 (V ) → l2 (V ) is
  called a Laplacian operator if it fulfills the following properties:
      1. L is symmetric, i.e., Luv = Lvu for all u, v ∈ V .
      2. L is positive semi-definite, i.e., ⟨x, Lx⟩ ≥ 0 for all x ∈ l2 (V ).
      3. The row sum of L is zero, i.e., v∈V Luv = 0 for all u ∈ V .
                                           P

      4. L has rank |V | − 1, i.e., the null space of L is one-dimensional.

    The definition in [19] is equivalent to the one given by [71], as the condition of
having rank |V |−1 and the irreducibility condition are equivalent, as there is a bi-
jection between both of them and the connectedness of the associated graph. The
main difference is that the condition of having finite non-positive off-diagonal el-
ements is not required, so it allows for Laplacian operators with non-bounded
steady states.
     Finally, we have a more lax definition given by [89]:
  Definition 2.44 Given a graph G = (V, E), a matrix L ∈ M (C) is said to
  be a Laplacian operator on G if there exists linear operators grad : Σ → V ,
  div : Σ → V , with domain the space of complex-valued functions on vertices
  and image on the functions over Σ, which is a family of subgraph structures
  of G (such edges, paths, cycles, or higher-order motifs), fulfilling:

                       L = divgrad; such that grad(1) = 0.                       (2.65)


     R   The Laplacian operator defined in [89] when X = E, can be written as:
                                       X
                          (Lf )(v) =         w(v,u) (f (v) − f (u)),
                                       u∈V

         for any v ∈ V , any function f : V → R and being w(v,u) the weight of
         the edge (v, u) in the graph.
2.6 Is that all for simple graphs?                                                  71

    This definition is more focused on the definition of the Laplacian given by
formula 2.34, but allowing different types of incidence relations as well as dif-
ferent weights on them, similar as the extension of the Laplacian operator to
hypergraphs [247]. This definition is very general and allows for a lot of different
types of Laplacian operators, but it does not guarantee that the operator is posi-
tive semi-definite or that it has a zero row sum, so it does not allow to ensure any
properties of the diffusion process mediated by the operator.
    It is notable to notice that many of the usual Laplacians used in the gen-
eralizations of graphs, such as the in-Laplacian and out-Laplacian operators on
directed graphs, the Hodge Laplacians on hypergraphs or the opposing and re-
pelling Laplacians on signed graphs, do not fulfill some, or all, of these definitions.
    To end this section, we have to acknowledge that all these definitions of what
is a Laplacian operator are built upon different views from different areas with
specifically applications in mind (whether to real world problems or to further
mathematical studies). For that same reason, we will call Laplacian operator to
any operator that can be extracted from some diffusion problem related to the
graph or object of study. That is why the most useful definition for us is the one
given in [89].
    This definition will be the one prevailing in the operators that we will define
and use in this tesis with the objective of answering the following questions about
diffusion dynamics on graphs:
    • Can we study processes in which the effects go beyond the immediate
       neighbors of a vertex?
    • What are the consequences of altering the mass of the system during a
       diffusion process?
    • Is it possible to define diffusion processes directly affected by the topology
       of the graph, rather than just observing their passive effects?

    Gain graphs, defined in Chapter 2.2.4, are a generalization of graphs that in-
clude the interaction of edges with a group structure. They are specially studied
in two cases: when the group is a subgroup of the unit circle in C, called the T-
gain graphs, and when the group is Z2 , a special subcase called the signed graphs.
In all of these cases, the Laplacian operator associated to the graph reflects inter-
esting properties about the structure of the problem being modeled.
    On the other hand, trying to study more general gain graphs through their
Laplacian is more difficult, as even for its definition is necessary to use auxiliary
structures, mainly the results of the representation theory of groups. But, are
there any other groups for which it is possible to directly define the Laplacian
operator? The answer is yes, we just have to choose any subgroup of C, so we
can define the Laplacian as an usual matrix operator. Specifically, we can choose
the subgroup R+ . Our next step won’t be just to study the properties of the
graph through the Laplacian, but to make them directly influence the evolution
of the system. These two facts are done with the definition of the hubs biased
Laplacians [84, 93, 111], which will be the objects of study in this chapter.
      76      Chapter 3. Hubs biased Laplacians and related advection models

3.1   Hubs biased Laplacians
      Assume G = (V, E) is a simple, undirected graph. Assume also that it is locally
      finite, meaning that any node has a finite number of neighbors. We can define
      the vector of degrees as k = (kv )v∈V , where kv is the number of neighbors, or
      nodes connected to v. Then, define the degree matrix K as the diagonal matrix
      of degrees. Then, the hubs biased Laplacian is defined as:

                                   Lα = Wα − K α AK −α ,                                (3.1)
                                                                                          α
      where Wα is the diagonal matrix of new degrees given by (Wα )v,v =                    kv
                                                                                                 ,
                                                                                P
                                                                                     Av,u   ku
                                                                               u∈V
      and A is the adjacency matrix of the graph. The parameter α is a real number
      that controls the influence of the hubs in the Laplacian operator:
          • If α = 0, then L0 = K − A, which is the usual Laplacian operator.
          • If α > 0, then this Laplacian favors the less connected nodes.
          • If α < 0, then the hubs are favored in the Laplacian.
          What do we mean by favor in this context? Consider the associated prob-
      abilistic transition matrix Pα = Wα−1 K α AK −α . For a fixed vertex v, the Wα
      matrix is a normalization factor, so the probability of a particle
                                                                      hoping from v to
                                                                          α
      a neighbor u is given by Lα (v, u). This entry is nothing but   kv
                                                                      ku       , so the prob-
      ability of hoping from v to u is proportional to ku−α . This means that, if α > 0,
      then the probability of hoping to a neighbor with a low degree is higher than
      hoping to a neighbor with a high degree, and vice versa if α < 0. This is why
      we call to the representative case α = 1 the hubs repelling Laplacian, and to the
      case α = −1 the hubs attracting Laplacian, as they were the names given in the
      orignal papers [84, 111].
          These Laplacians may look like a special case of a weighted and/or directed
      graph Laplacian, but they are not. The main difference is that the weights of the
      edges are not symmetric but rather fulfill the condition wv,u = wu,v−1 . This makes

      these Laplacians be on the space of the quasi-reciprocal matrices, which are the
      ones fulfilling that same condition for any entry different than 0.
          This operators have many simmilar properties to the usual graph Laplacian:

       Theorem 3.1 Let G be a simple, locally finite graph, and let Lα be the hubs
       biased Laplacian defined in (3.1). Then, the following properties hold:
           • Lα has only real eigenvalues.
           • Lα is positive semidefinite.
3.1 Hubs biased Laplacians                                                      77




Figure 3.1: Example of the weights of the edges for the value of α = 1/2.
This (hubs-repelling) Laplacian favors the less connected nodes, as we can see
by the width
         √ of the arrows in the picture. Thus, a particle in the purple node
will have 2 times more probability of hoping to the red node than to the green
ones.


     • The smallest eigenvalue is 0, and the associated eigenvector is the vector
       of all ones.
     • The rank of Lα is |V | − c, where |V | is the number of vertices in the
       graph and c is the number of connected components.

Proof. The main properties of these hubs biased Laplacians come from the fact
that they are similar to a symmetric matrix:

             K −α Lα K α = K −α (Wα − K α AK −α )K α = Wα − A,                (3.2)

where it is known that Wα is a diagonal matrix with positive entries, and A is the
adjacency matrix of the graph, which is symmetric.
    This is due to the fact that, if two matrices M and N are similar, if (λ, v) is
an eigenpair of M , then λv = M v = P −1 N P v, so λP v = N P v and thus λ is
an eigenvalue also of N .
    Now, to prove the positive semidefiniteness of Lα , we prove so for Wα − A.
Let x be a vector in R|V | , then:
                                  X         X  kv α     X
            xT (Wα − A)x =            x2v       Av,u−         xv xu =

                    =                     xv −         xu .
                                     ku         kv
                        (v,u)∈E
78       Chapter 3. Hubs biased Laplacians and related advection models

     As the sum of squares is always non-negative, we conclude that Wα − A is
positive semidefinite. As K is a diagonal matrix with positive entries, then K −α
is also a diagonal matrix with positive entries, so Lα is also positive semidefinite.
     For observing that the smallest eigenvalue is 0, we use both first proven facts
to know that the eigenvalues are real non-negative numbers, so if 0 is an eigen-
value, then it is the smallest one. As the matrix Wα is constructed so that Lα has
row sums equal to 0, then the vector of all ones is an eigenvector associated to
the eigenvalue 0.
     Finally, suppose that G is connected. Then, suppose that the eigenvalue 0 has
some other eigenvector x different from ⃗1. By the similiraty with Wα − A, we
have that there exist an eigenvector y of Wα − A such that y = K −α x and fulfils:
                                                      α                  α        !2
                                      X           kv    2              ku    2
          0 = y T (Wα − A)y =                               xv −                 xu        .   (3.3)
                                                  ku                   kv
                                    (v,u)∈E

                                                          α             α
                                                               2               2
This means that, for every edge (v, u), we have that kkuv        yv = kkuv       yu ,
which implies that xv = xu for every edge (v, u). As the graph is connected,
this means that x is constant, so it is a multiple of the vector of all ones. Thus,
there are no other eigenvectors associated to the eigenvalue 0 than the vector of
all ones, so the rank of Lα is |V | − 1.
     If the graphs has c connected components, then each of the vectors consisting
of all ones in the entries corresponding to the nodes in each component and zero
otherwise is an eigenvector associated to the eigenvalue 0, so the rank of Lα is
at least |V | − c. To conclude, we use the previous argument for a single compo-
nent to show that any other eigenvector associated to the eigenvalue 0 is a linear
combination of these vectors, so the rank of Lα is exactly |V | − c.          q.e.d.


     R   It is noticeable that the hubs biased Laplacian is the Laplacian of a gain
         graph with the group R+ , as the weights are given by the degrees of the
         nodes. Moreover, this gain graph is balanced by the way of constructing
         it, as the associated incidence matrix is K α AK −α , which it is the expla-
         nation as to why it preserves so many properties of the usual Laplacian.
    These properties show that the hubs biased Laplacians behave very much
alike the usual Laplacian, although the transient dynamics are different due to the
deep changes in the probabilities of hoping from one node to another. Nonethe-
less, there are some properties that are not shared and that have to be taken into
account.
3.1 Hubs biased Laplacians                                                                      79

    The bounds on the eigenvalues of these Laplacians are not the same as the
ones of the usual Laplacian, as they depend on the parameter α or |α|. Evenmore,
we can sometimes bound the eigenvalues for a general alpha to the eigenvalues
for α = 0, which is to show its improvements over the classical setting.

3.2   Diffusion under the hubs biased Laplacians
      The big change stepped in the definition of the hubs biased Laplacian is that the
      probabilities of hoping from one node to another are not symmetric, but rather
      depend on the degrees of the nodes. This makes one easily wonder how the
      diffusion process behaves under these Laplacians. The following theorem shows
      that the diffusion process may look like the classical case, but we will see the
      subtleties that make it different and more interesting.

       Theorem 3.3 Let G be a simple, locally finite graph, and let Lα be the hubs
       biased Laplacian defined in (3.1). Consider the initial condition x0 ∈ R|V | , and
       let xt be the solution to the diffusion equation:

                                  d
                                     xt = −Lα xt ,     x0 = x0 .                      (3.20)
                                  dt
       Then, the solution is given by:

                                         xt = e−tLα x0 .                              (3.21)

       Moreover, as t → ∞, the solution converges to a vector proportional to ⃗1,
       which is the steady state of the diffusion process, and the speed of conver-
82        Chapter 3. Hubs biased Laplacians and related advection models

 gence depends on the spectral gap of the operator, namely the second smallest
 eigenvalue λα,2 .


Proof. The proof follows the same steps as the one for the classical solution. As
Lα is a linear operator, we use the properties of the matrix exponential to find
the expression for the solution of the equation. Then, we can use the spectral
decomposition of Lα = V ΛV −1 , where V is the matrix of (right) eigenvectors
and Λ is the diagonal matrix of eigenvalues, to write the solution as:

         e−tLα = e−tλα,1 vα,1 uTα,1 + e−tλα,2 vα,2 uTα,2 + slower terms,               (3.22)

denoting V = (vα,1 , vα,2 , . . . , vα,n ) and U = (uα,1 , uα,2 , . . . , uα,n ), where V −1 =
U = (uα,1 , uα,2 , . . . , uα,n )T .
     As the first and smallest eigenvalue is 0 and the rest of terms decay exponen-
tially to 0 as t → ∞, we have that the solution converges to the vector vα,1 uTα,1 x0 .
As the row sums of Lα are equal to 0, then the vector vα,1 is the vector of all ones,
so the solution converges to a vector proportional to ⃗1.
     The speed of convergence is given by the second smallest eigenvalue λα,2 , as
it is the first term that decays slower to 0 (as it is the second smallest eigenvalue).
Thus, the solution converges to the steady state at a rate proportional to e−tλα,2 .
                                                                                         q.e.d.


    This theorem shows that the diffusion process under any hubs biased Lapla-
cian behaves very much equal, including α = 0, which is the classical case. The
steady state is proportional to the vector of all ones, so it is a consensus protocol
in which all entries converge to the same value. Also, the speed of convergence
is given by the second smallest eigenvalue, which by the inequality 3.4 are com-
parable to the second smallest eigenvalue of the classical Laplacian. Nonetheless,
there are some subtleties that have to be taken into account and that we can see
thanks to numerical simulations.
    The most noticeable is that the transient dynamics are not the same. As the
probabilities of hoping from one node to another are not symmetric, then the
existence of hubs may allow some specific nodes to converge faster than others.
This is related to the next fact, which is a misinterpretation of the bounds in 3.4,
which are not tight and generally do not allow to know if λα,2 < λ0,2 or not. This
means that the speed of convergence may be faster or slower than the classical
case, depending on the value of α and the structure of the graph.
3.2 Diffusion under the hubs biased Laplacians                                      83

    In the original articles studying the specific case of hubs-repelling Laplacian
(α = −1)[84] and hubs-attracting Laplacian (α = 1)[111], the authors address
this issue by providing specific examples and simulating the process on random
graphs to analyze if there is some pattern. Indeed, in [84] its seen that the speed of
convergence is generally slower than the classical case, while in [111] it happens
the contrary for the hubs attracting Laplacian. In those articles, as well as in [93],
the authors provide tighter bounds for both the second smallest and the largest
eigenvalues using bounds for other quantities, such as the first Zagreb or Randić
indices, which are just directly valid for the cases α = 1 and α = −1.
     In figure 3.2 we show an example of the transient dynamics of the diffusion
process under different hubs biased Laplacian for a simple graph. The results are
consistent with the previous articles, as we can see that the speed of convergence
is generally slower for α = 1 and faster for α = −1, and it seems to exhibit
the pattern that hubs attracting Laplacians converge faster than the classical one
and the hubs repelling Laplacians do the opposite. Nonetheless, there are some
cases in which may not happen. In figure 3.3 we show a simulation comparing
the values of the second smallest eigenvalue of the hubs biased Laplacian with
the classical one for different values of α and different random graphs. Again, for
α = 1 and α = −1, the results are simmilar to the previous case and the previous
literature. But the interesting fact is that this is not a pattern maintained for α > 0
and α < 0. In fact, the speed of convergence seems to be slower for values of α in
an interval starting near −1 and ending in 0, while for the rest of values it seems
to be faster than the classical case.
    Nonetheless, this is not the only difference between the diffusion process un-
der the hubs biased Laplacian and the classical one. The mass of these systems
may not be conserved, even though they converge to a similar steady state. This
is due to a fact inherited for directed graphs, as the sum of the columns of the
hubs biased Laplacian is not equal to 0 in general.
    For proving that, we need the following Proposition:

Proposition 3.1 Let G be a simple, locally finite graph. For each node v ∈ V , let
kv be the degree of v and denote Nv as the set of neighbors of v. Let α ∈ R be a
fixed real number. If for every u ∈ V we have that:

                          v∈Nu               v∈Nu

then either α = 0 or the graph is regular.

     If the graph is not regular, there is at least one vertex w ∈ V such that kw < ∆

    It is easy to see that the condition (3.23) is equivalent to the condition that
the sum of the entries in each column of Lα is equal to 0, which is the condition
for the mass to be conserved as we see in the next corollary.

      condition that the sum of the entries in each column of Lα is equal to 0. But,
