          This evolution of the mass can be seen in Figure 3.2, but we plot it again in
      Figure 3.4 by showing explicitly the mass of the system at each time step. As we
      can see, the mass is not conserved, and it remains constant only when the system
      has reaches its steady state.
          How much does it change? On what does it depend? And even more, is
      there a way to define an operator that conserves the mass and still preserve these
      probabilities of transition towards/against the hubs? That was the topic of the
      article [192] and the next section.

3.3   Hubs biased advection Laplacians
      In a directed graph, we have seen that there are two different Laplacians that we
      can define, the one that is defined using the out-degrees and the one that is defined
      using the in-degrees. The first one is the most used one and it is usually called the
      consensus Laplacian, due to the fact that its diffusion dynamics converge to an
      homogeneous steady state. The second one, nonetheless, is called the advection
      Laplacian, as it is used to model the advection process in a directed graph, where
      the particles are moved following the direction of the edges. This Laplacian is the
      one that preserved the mass for directed graphs, so we can use it as an inspiration
88      Chapter 3. Hubs biased Laplacians and related advection models

to define a Laplacian that preserves the mass in the hubs biased case.
    The idea is simple and one can really see it from Corollary 3.1. If we want
to preserve the mass of the system, we need to define a Laplacian L that fulfills
the condition ⃗1T L = 0, which is equivalent to the condition that the sum of the
entries in each column of L is equal to 0. Due to the fact that the hubs biased
Laplacian has row sums equal to 0, it is enough to take its transposed to fulfil the
equation, or, more formally, take its adjoint operator.

 Theorem 3.4 Let G be a simple, locally finite graph, and let Lα be the hubs
 biased Laplacian defined in (3.1). Then, the hubs biased advection Laplacian is
 defined as the adjoint operator of Lα .
     Moreover, it is given by the following expression:

                     ∗
                                X  kv α             α
                                                       ku
               ((Lα ) g) (v) =                g(u) −         g(v),        (3.28)
                                       ku              kv
                                u∈Nv

 for any function g : V → R and any vertex v ∈ V .

Proof. Appliying the operator to the the function g = ew , where ew is the vector
that has a 1 in the entry corresponding to the vertex w and 0 elsewhere, we have
directly that:
                                      P  α
                                     
                                     
                                               kv
                                               ku  , if v = w,
                                     u∈N
                                     
                                           v
                                             α
                  ((Lα )∗ ew ) (v) =                                        (3.29)
                                          
                                     
                                        ku
                                        kv
                                               ,      if v ̸= w,
                                     
                                                      otherwise.
                                     
                                      0,
                                     

This is directly the definition of the transposed matrix of Lα , so the expres-
sion (3.28) is the definition of the adjoint operator of Lα .             q.e.d.


 Corollary 3.2 Let G be a simple, locally finite graph, and consider the diffusion
 equation mediated by the hubs biased advection Laplacian Lα defined in (3.28).
 Let u be the solution to such equation with initial condition u0 ∈ R|V | , then
 the mass of the system is conserved, meaning that:
                             X                X
                                   ut (v) =         u0 (v),                  (3.30)
                             v∈V              v∈V

 for any t > 0.
3.3 Hubs biased advection Laplacians                                            89

Proof. The proof is straightforward when we use that the hubs biased advection
Laplacian is the adjoint operator of the hubs biased Laplacian. The second one
has ⃗1 as a right eigenvector associated to the eigenvalue 0, so it has row sums
equal to 0. Thus, the adjoint operator has column sums equal to 0 and the mass
is conserved.                                                              q.e.d.

    Here we have given a definition of the hubs biased advection Laplacian based
on the adjoint operator of the hubs biased Laplacian and shown that it preserves
the mass of the system. Unfortunately, proving just this would be to assume that
the properties of directed graphs are directly inherited by this operators. For
really proving that this is an advection operator, we need to show that it is the
divergence of a velocity field, and for that we need to define an incidence matrix
that considers this bidirectionality of the edges.
  Definition 3.1 Let G = (V, E) be a simple undirected graph. A double-
  orientation of G is the graph G↔ = (V, E ′ ) such that if (i, j) ∈ E then
  i → j ∈ E ′ and j → i ∈ E ′ .

  Definition 3.2 Let G↔ be a double-orientation of graph G = (V, E) with
  |V | = n nodes and |E| = m edges. Then, the gradient operator or oriented
  incidence matrix of G is the operator D ∈ R2m×n whose entries are defined
  by

              
              1
              
                    if vertex v is the head of oriented edge e = (v, j),
     Dve :=    −1    if vertex v is the tail of edge e = (j, v),            (3.31)
              
                     otherwise.
              
              0


  Definition 3.3 Let G↔ be a double-orientation of G = (V, E) with |V | = n
  nodes and |E| = m edges. Then, the divergence operator of G is the operator
  D∗ ∈ Rn×2m mapping from the edges to the nodes.

    The necessity for using the double orientation of the graph emerges from
the following. Here, we are considering undirected graphs which are then trans-
formed such that every existing edge is replaced by weighted arcs pointing in
both directions. Those weights are not necessarily the same in both direction,
which oblige us to consider the double-orientation explicitly. In this context, the
incidence matrix indicates how much of a quantity departs from a node to its
90       Chapter 3. Hubs biased Laplacians and related advection models

nearest neighbor, as well as how much arrives at it. This obviously creates a gra-
dient between every pair of connected nodes of the original graph. Note that this
notation maintains the main property of the classical Laplacian with respect to
the incidence matrix, that is L0 = D∗ · D, which reflects that each directed edge
has a reciprocal with the same weight.

    Finally, we can define explicitly the velocity field that will underly to the ad-
vection operator.
  Definition 3.4 Let G↔ be a double-orientation of G. Then, the time-invariant
  velocity field V ∈ Rm×n on the domain of u (t) is defined as

              α
            − kv
            
                            if vertex v is the head of oriented edge e = (v, j),
     Vve :=    kj
                            otherwise.
            
            0
                                                                              (3.32)

    We prove now that this new operator on graphs corresponds to a degree-
biased advection operator on undirected graphs.

 Theorem 3.5 The Adjoint operator of the degree-biased Laplacian, (Lα )∗ , is
 an advection operator where the time-invariant velocity field is biased by the
 degree of the nodes which create a flow in the direction of high or low degree
 nodes according to the respective value of α.


Proof. It is enough to show that

                            (Lα )∗ u (t) = D∗ (Vu (t)) .                       (3.33)

     For that, recall that the product matrix can be written entry by entry as:

                                        |E | ′

                            (D · V)ij =      (D∗ )il Vlj .                     (3.34)

    Suppose i ̸= j, then the product (D∗ )il Vlj ̸= 0 if and only if the edge l goes
from i to j, as the first term is non-zero if and only if l is connected to i and the
second one is different from zero if l goes towards j.
     Hence, there is only only term in (D∗ · V)ij that is different from 0 on the

edge l described before, leading to the calculation:

    On the other hand, for i = j, D∗ will be non-zero for each edge going towards

    Comparing this terms with those produced in (3.28) for g = ei and v = j

    Before following with the properties of this new operator, we need to high-
light a crucial difference between the advection operators defined in the literature
and the one we have just defined. In previous works the only attempts to study
advection processes on graphs have been on directed graphs. This was an obvious
necessity due to the fact that a gradient was needed over the edges of the graph.
However, here this gradient is created by the heterogeneities in the degrees of the
nodes, and it is done for undirected, simple graphs.
    As we have said, the conservation of mass property is inherited by the fact
that the new operator can be seen as an operator on a (bi)directed graph. Nonethe-
less, the existence of non-symetric weights do not ensure many of the properties
of these new operators to be derived directly from this classical theory, and we
need to look at them separately.
    The properties we are going to prove here are those that are related to the
diffusion process, and some of them will be directly inherited from the hubs biased
Laplacian, while others will differ heavily from it.
Proposition 3.2 The spectrum of (Lα )∗ is real non-negative and 0 ∈ σ((Lα )∗ ).

Proof. As it is the transpose of the degree-biased Laplacian, we can use its prop-
erties to write it as:
                                       ⊤
(Lα )∗ = (Lα )⊤ = Wα − K α AK −α            = Wα −K −α AK α = K −α (Wα −A)K α

Now, using the definition of Wα and A, we can say that this Adjoint operator is
similar to a symmetric matrix, thus, have real spectrum.
92       Chapter 3. Hubs biased Laplacians and related advection models

   Moreover, it has the same spectrum as its adjoint. As 0 ∈ σ(Lα ) because its
rows sum up to 0 and σ(Lα ) = σ((Lα )∗ ), we get that 0 ∈ σ((Lα )∗ ).    q.e.d.

                                                               ∗
Proposition 3.3 The 0-eigenvector of the operator (Lα ) is the vector v = k −2α
where k is the vector of degrees of each node.

Proof. Without loss of generality we consider connected graphs. In case of dis-
connected ones we can consider the following results on a component-wise way,
which is standard in network theory.
    Let v be the zero-eigenvector. To find its expression, we must solve the sys-
tem:
                              (Lα )∗ v = 0 · v = 0.                        (3.37)

    Using the previous decomposition, we are going to find u := K α v so the
system is now related to the matrix Wα − A. Hence, we have to solve the system
of equations:
                  X  ki α X
              ui              −      uj = 0; for i = 1, . . . , n.       (3.38)
                        kj
                  j∈Ni             j∈Ni


     This can be written as:
                          X
                                ui (kj )−α − uj (ki )−α = 0,                 (3.39)
                         j∈Ni


which has uj = (kj )−α as solution.
   Thus, the 0-eigenvector is v = (kj−2α )nj=1 .                             q.e.d.

    These propositions show that the diffusion process associated to the advec-
tion hubs biased Laplacian not only preserves mass, but that it is well defined for
any initial condition with non-negative entries and that it converges to a steady
state proportional to the vector of degrees of the graph. This means that, for any
non-zero initial condition, after some time, the system will distribute the concen-
trations so that the amount of mass arriving a each node through the advection
process is the same as the amount of mass leaving it, leaving the concentrations
fixed in its steady state. Moreover, the speed of convergence is given again by
the second smallest eigenvalue of the operator, so the speed of convergence is
exactly the same as its hubs biased Laplacian counterpart. We can observe all
this in Fig. 3.5
3.3 Hubs biased advection Laplacians                                        93




             (a) α = m2                              (b) α = m1




            (c) α = m0.5                              (d) α = 0.5




              (e) α = 1                               (f) α = 2

Figure 3.5: Transient dynamics of the diffusion process under different advec-
tion hubs biased Laplacian for a simple graph. The solid lines represent the
evolution of the concentrations at each node, while the dashed lines represent
the theoretical steady state of the system, which is proportional to the vector
of degrees of the graph. The colors are according to the degree of the nodes.
      94       Chapter 3. Hubs biased Laplacians and related advection models

3.4   Generally-biased Laplacians
      In this chapter we have seen how to use the degree centrality of a network to
      define two different Laplacian operators that change the probabilities of transition
      between nodes depending on the rate between their degrees. These operators,
      defined and studied in [84, 93, 111, 192], open the door to a new way of modelling
      diffusion and advection processes in undirected graphs, as we can extend them
      to any kind of centrality measure in the graph.
        Definition 3.5 Let G be a simple graph, and let c : V → R+ be a centrality
        measure defined on the nodes of the graph. Call C = diag(c) the diagonal
        matrix of centralities. Then, the c-biased Laplacian is defined as:

                                   Lc,α = Wc,α − C α AC −α ,                       (3.40)

        where Wc,α is the diagonal matrix that makes the row sums of Lc,α equal to
        0.
            On the other hand, the c-biased advection Laplacian L∗c,α is defined as the
        adjoint operator of Lc,α , whose formula is simmilar to the one in (3.40) but the
        diagonal matrix Wc,α is constructed to make the column sums of Lc,α equal
        to 0.

          This definition is an straightforward generalization of the hubs biased Lapla-
      cian and the hubs biased advection Laplacian, as we can see that the hubs bi-
      ased Laplacians are just the c-biased Laplacians for the degree centrality measure
      c(v) = kv , where kv is the degree of the node v. The properties of these operators
      are also similar to the ones of the hubs biased Laplacians, namely:
           • The spectrum of Lc,α is real and non-negative, and 0 is an eigenvalue.
           • The 0-eigenvector of Lc,α is the vector ⃗1.
           • The 0-eigenvector of L∗c,α is the vector c−2α , where c is the vector of cen-
             tralities of the nodes.
           • The  speed of convergence is given by the second smallest eigenvalue of the
             operator, which is comparable to that of the classical Laplacian.
           • The diffusion process under Lc,α∗ preserves the mass of the system, mean-
             ing that the sum of the entries in each column of L∗c,α is equal to 0.
           • The diffusion process under Lc,α does not preserve the mass of the system
             unless α = 0 or the centrality measure is constant for all nodes.
         Unfortunately, these operators have not been studied in detail yet, much less
      applied to model any real phenomena. Still, the interpretability of these operators,
      3.5 Conclusion                                                                  95

      together with the nice properties they have, make them a promising tool to model
      diffusion and advection processes in undirected graphs. In Fig. 3.6 and Fig. 3.7 it
      is plotted some examples of the transient dynamics of the diffusion process under
      the c-biased Laplacian for different choices of c and α.

3.5   Conclusion
      Hubs biased Laplacians are a generalization of the classical Laplacian operator on
      undirected graphs that introduces an inhomogeneity in the transition probabili-
      ties between nodes, depending on their degrees. This allows to model diffusion
      processes in which going through the more connected nodes is more likely, or less
      likely, depending on the value of the parameter α. Nonetheless, this change in the
      transition probabilities does not affect the convergence of the diffusion process
      to a steady state proportional to the vector of all ones, which is the consensus
      protocol. The speed of convergence is given by the second smallest eigenvalue of
      the operator, which is comparable to the classical case. However, is not only the
      transient dynamics that change, but they show a change over time on the mass
      of the system, which is not conserved in general. This is an interesting fact, as
      typically the diffusion processes on undirected graphs are defined to preserve the
      mass of the system, but not all the processes that happen in real life do. On the
      other hand, an operator the shows this unbalance in the transition probabilities,
      but preserves the mass of the system, has also interest as it can be used to model
      advection processes in undirected graphs. This is the case of the hubs biased
      advection Laplacian, which is defined as the adjoint operator of the hubs biased
      Laplacian and preserves the mass of the system. The diffusion process under this
      operator shows properties very simmilar to the ones of the hubs biased Laplacian,
      but it converges to a non-homogeneous steady state, which is proportional to the
      vector of degrees of the graph.
          These two kind of different but closely related operators show how it is pos-
      sible to model diffusion and advection processes in undirected graphs, which is a
      topic that has not been studied as much as the directed case. The results obtained
      in this chapter show both the similarities and the differences between these two
      kind of processes and the classical diffusion one, and give us an idea on its use-
      fulness in modeling real life processes.

                                                  All along there was some
                                                  Invisible string

                                                                             Swift


    Typically, the Laplacian operator is defined as a local operator, which means
that it only considers interactions between nearest neighbors. This might seem
as a necessary condition, as it would mean that the topology of the network is
direclty present in the definition of the Laplacian. However, we can think of
diffusive processes that hop further than just to nearest neighbors, for which
the topology of the network is taking into account in the way of measuring this
hopping distance. This new point of view for the graph Laplacian operator is
heavily influenced by the recent resurge in the research of the fractional Laplacian
for the continuous case, which is the equivalent non-local operator.
    In this chapter, we will present the definition of the long range Laplacian
operator, which is a non-local operator that generalizes the classical Laplacian
operator. We will also discuss its properties and differences with respect to the
diffusion process defined by the classical Laplacian operator. These properties
will show its usefulness in the context of complex networks, where long range
interactions are also present.
      100            Chapter 4. Long-range diffusion processes in simple graphs

4.1   The continuous fractional Laplacian

      Before entering into the definition of a long range operator for networks, let us
      briefly review the definition of the fractional Laplacian operator in the continuous
      case.
        Definition 4.1 The fractional Laplacian operator of order s ∈ (0, 1) is defined
        as
                                             f (x + y) + f (x − y) − 2f (x)
                                        Z
                                 1
               (−∆)s f (x) = − Cd,s                                          dy,      (4.1)
                                 2       Rd             |y|d+2s

        where Cd,s is a normalization constant that depends on the dimension d and
        the order s of the operator.


          The non-local nature of the fractional Laplacian relies on the fact that the
      integral in (4.1) is taken over the whole space Rd , which means that the operator
      considers interactions between points that are not necessarily close to each other.
      Nonetheless, the choice of the kernel |y|−(d+2s) ensures that the contributions
      from distant points decay rapidly, meaning that further points have a smaller
      influence on the value of the operator at a given point. This is explicitly present
      in the limit of the fractional Laplacian as s → 1, which recovers the classical
      Laplacian operator. Thus, is this penalization of the contributions from distant
      points that really changes the nature of the diffusion process from the classical to
      the fractional Laplacian. It is also worth noting the other limit, as s → 0, which
      recovers the identity operator.

           There are several equivalent definitions of the fractional Laplacian operator,
      depending on the domain and boundary conditions (see [164, 172]), but this def-
      inition is the most common one in the literature.

          There is a large body of literature on the fractional Laplacian operator [44,
      221], but we recommend the reader to [72] for a first construction of the operator
      and some of its properties. In this same article, as well as in other important
      works such as [188], the construction of the fractional Laplacian operator comes
      from its interpretation as the limit of Lévy flights, which is a stochastic process
      that models random walks with long jumps. This interpretation is also the basis
      for the definition of the long range Laplacian operator in networks that we will
      present in this chapter.
      4.2 The d-path Laplacian                                                                 101

4.2   The d-path Laplacian
      To define long-range Laplacians in a network, the first step we have to do is to de-
      fine what it is a distance between two nodes in a graph. There are a lot of networks
      that are constructed in base of a real scenario in which we can assign a geometric
      distance between nodes, such as the case of geographical networks. However, in
      a general case, the network is not constructed in this way, and we need to define
      a distance that exists for any kind of abstract graph. For this purpose, the most
      common distance used is the shortest path distance, which is defined as the min-
      imum length of all paths connecting the two nodes. Recall that the length of a
      path in a simple graph is the number of edges it transverses. We will denote this
      distance by dij for nodes i and j, and we can construct then the distance matrix
      D = (dij )i,j∈V , where V is the set of nodes in the graph.
          The way to translate this topological feature to a Laplacian is then easy.
        Definition 4.2 Let G be a simple graph. Let dij be the shortest path distance
        between nodes i and j in G and fix d ∈ N. Then, the d-path Adjacency matrix
        is defined as:
                                                        (
                                                         1 if dij = d
                       Ad = ((ad )ij )i,j∈V , (ad )ij =                       (4.2)
                                                         0 otherwise

             Thus, we can define the d-degree as
                                                   X
                                        (kd )i =         (ad )ij ,                           (4.3)
                                                   j∈V

        which counts the number of nodes in G at a distance d from node i.

         R    Another way of defining the d-path adjacency matrix, perhaps more com-
              putational, is given by using the distance matrix D and boolean logic. This
              way we have
                                              Ad = 1D=d ,                            (4.4)
              where 1D=d is the indicator matrix that takes the value 1 if the condition
              is satisfied and 0 otherwise.
                   This will be helpful not only for computational purposes, but also for
              when we need to join several long-distance operators.

         R    Although we have fixed d ∈ N, it is obvious that for any d ≥ ρ, where ρ
              is the diameter of the graph or the maximum distance between any two
              nodes in the graph, we have that Ad = 0.
102            Chapter 4. Long-range diffusion processes in simple graphs




Figure 4.1: Example of a graph superposing the connections given by the 1-
path Laplacian (in black) and the 2-path Laplacian (in blue). The black edges
represent the direct connections between nodes given by the usual adjacency
matrix, while the blue edges are long range connections that jump over the
purple node, and are represented thinner as they typically are chosen to have
less influence than the neighboring nodes.


  Definition 4.3 Let G be a simple graph. Let dij be the shortest path distance
  between nodes i and j in G and fix d ∈ N. Call Ad the d-path adjacency matrix
  and define Kd as the diagonal matrix of d-degrees. Then, the d-path Laplacian
  matrix is defined as:
                                Ld = Kd − Ad .                             (4.5)


   R    The d-path Laplacian matrix is a generalization of the classical Laplacian
        matrix, which corresponds to the case d = 1. In this case, we have that
        A1 is the adjacency matrix of the graph and K1 is the degree matrix.

    These Laplacians thus, would represent diffusion processes of particles that
jump to nodes at fixed distances d from the current node instead of just to nearest
neighbors.
    This is an intepretation given a fixed graph G, but each of these Ld operators
can be seen just as classical diffusion operators in different graphs, whose adja-
cency matrix is given by Ad . This is why, in the article [83] where these operators
were first introduced, they also present subsequent propositions that show that
each of them have the very same properties as the classical Laplacian operator.
For instance, the number of connected components of these graphs is still given
by the number of zero eigenvalues of their Laplacian, thus obtaining if a graph
can be traversed through d-hoppings.
      4.3 Path Laplacian matrices                                                       103

4.3   Path Laplacian matrices
      We know now how to mathematically model long range interactions in a graph
      through the use of d-path Laplacian matrices, but we still need to define how to
      combine these matrices to obtain a single operator that captures the whole of
      the long range interactions of the graph. The most natural way to do this is to
      consider some kind of combination of these d-path Laplacians.
        Definition 4.4 Let G be a simple graph and let Ld be the d-path Laplacian
        matrix of G for 1 ≤ d < ρ. Then, the Path Laplacian matrix is defined as:
                                              ρ
                                              X
                                         L=         wd Ld ,                           (4.6)
                                              d=1

        where wd ≥ 0 is a weighting factor of the interaction at distance d.

          Nonetheless, the choice of this combination is not trivial, as different combi-
      nations lead to very different results both in the mathematical properties of the
      resulting operator and in the interpretation of the diffusion process it models.
          For that reason, we will present here the two most common ways of combin-
      ing the d-path Laplacian matrices, based on a modelization premise: the influence
      of further nodes must decay as the distance increases. This means that the con-
      tributions of the d-path Laplacian matrices must be weighted by a factor that
      decreases with d.
          Furthermore, there is another fact to consider. If the graph is finite, the choice
      of weights can be more flexible as the number of terms it is finite too. But for the
      case of infinite graphs, the decay of the weights with the distance must be so that
      the series converges, otherwise the resulting operator would not be well defined.
        Definition 4.5 Let G = (V, E) be a simple graph and consider the d-path
        Laplacian matrices Ld for 1 ≤ d < ρ.
        Growth condition. We say that the graph G has polynomial growth if there
        exist constants C ≥ 0 and τ ≥ 0 such that

                                 kd ≤ C dτ    for all 1 ≤ d ≤ ρ.                      (4.7)
104           Chapter 4. Long-range diffusion processes in simple graphs

  (i) Laplace-transformed d-path Laplacian. For λ ∈ C define
                                        ρ
                                        X
                               Lλ :=          e−λd Ld .                     (4.8)
                                        d=1

  Then Lλ is a bounded operator on ℓ2 (V ) whenever

                                  Re(λ) > ln k.                             (4.9)

  Moreover, for real λ with
                                    λ > ln k,                              (4.10)

  the operator Lλ is positive semidefinite.
      If (4.7) holds, then Lλ is bounded for every λ ∈ C with

                                   Re(λ) > 0.                              (4.11)

  (ii) Mellin-transformed d-path Laplacian. For s ∈ C define
                                        ρ
                                        X
                                Ls :=         d−s Ld .                     (4.12)
                                        d=1

  If (4.7) holds, then Ls is a bounded operator on ℓ2 (V ) whenever

                                 Re(s) > τ + 1.                            (4.13)
    The main advantage when working with these Path Laplacians is that they
flexibly allow to model these long range interactions by adjusting the weights
assigned to each d-path Laplacian. This adaptability has its counterpart in the
mathematical properties of the resulting operator, as working with sums of ma-
trices modifies the spectral properties and, thus, the behavior of the diffusion
process it describes.
    Nonetheless, because of the individual properties of each of the d-path Lapla-
cian and the way of constructing its transformed counterparts, some of the most
important ones are preserved:

Proposition 4.1 Consider the Path Laplacian matrix L defined in Definition 4.4.
Then, the following properties hold:
   (i) L is a self-adjoint operator on ℓ2 (V ).
  (ii) 0 is an eigenvalue of L with associated eigenvector ⃗1 (the constant func-
4.3 Path Laplacian matrices                                                             105

       tion).
 (iii) Consider the diffusion process associated with L. Then, the mass is pre-
       served, i.e., for any initial distribution of mass, the total mass remains con-
       stant over time.
 (iv) Consider the diffusion process associated with L. The speed of convergence
       to the steady state is determined by the spectral gap of L, which is the
       smallest non-zero eigenvalue of L and it is always positive. If the graph is
       connected, this eigenvalue is given by the second eigenvalue λ2 of L and
       the rate of convergence is given by e−λ2 .

Proof. The first claim is straightforward, as each d-path Laplacian Ld is self-
adjoint (symmetric) and the sum of self-adjoint operators is also self-adjoint.
    For the second and third claim, we need to prove that the constant function ⃗1
is an eigenvector of L, both from left and from right, associated with the eigen-
value 0.
    For this, just recall that each of the d-path Laplacians is constructed to have
row sum equal to 0, so we have:
                                   ρ
                                   X                 ρ
                                                     X
                           L⃗1 =         wd Ld⃗1 =         wd 0 = 0,                  (4.14)
                                   d=1               d=1


so ⃗1 is a right eigenvector with eigenvalue 0.
     The left eigenvector comes then from the symmetry of the operator. It implies
the mass conservation as, if u is the solution to the equation u̇ = −Lu, we have:

                   d X
                       ui (t) = ⟨⃗1, u̇(t)⟩ = −⟨⃗1, Lu(t)⟩ = 0.                       (4.15)
                   dt
                       i

                                                                                  q.e.d.


   R   One common mistake when first approaching this Path Laplacian operator
       is to think that, as the formulation is equivalent, we are just working with
       the Laplacian of a weighted graph, so the topology of the underlying graph
       is not relevant anymore. However, this is not the case, as the weights all
       follow the structure imposed by the paths in the graph, thus preserving
       the effect of the topology of the initial graph in the operator itself. In
       other words, the distribution of the weights in the Path Laplacian is not
       arbitrary, but rather follows the structure of the graph, and observing them
       just as one more weighted graph instead of a much less connected graph
      106            Chapter 4. Long-range diffusion processes in simple graphs

               with long range connections may lead to a wrong interpretation of the
               modelled phenomena.


4.4   Diffusion processes under the Path Laplacian
      In Proposition 4.1 we already proved several properties that, as well as they did for
      the classical Laplacian, shape generally the behavior of the diffusion processes as-
      sociated with the Path Laplacian. This behavior can be summarized in the phrase
      “the diffusion process under the Path Laplacian operator is a consensus protocol that
      preserves the mass”. This may give the idea that the process does not change much,
      but the following example will show how much this indirect influence benefits
      the process in graphs with badly communicated communities.




                                   (a) Two-community graph.




                              (b) Classical diffusion (L = K − A).

      Figure 4.2: Example on how the existence of very defined communities in a
      graph can lead to a slow diffusion process under the classical Laplacian.

      ■   Example 4.1 Construct a graph with two communities and a few interconnec-
      tions between them, just as the one in Figure 4.2a. Consider the diffusion process
4.4 Diffusion processes under the Path Laplacian                                 107

starting from a uniform initial distribution over the nodes in the left community.
In the classical case, the diffusion is primarily confined within each community,
leading to a slow mixing between them. This is evident in the concentration pro-
files shown in Figure 4.2b, where the lines for nodes in different communities
remain largely separated over time.
     In contrast, when using the Path Laplacian, the diffusion process benefits from
the long-range connections introduced by the d-path Laplacians. This allows for
a more efficient mixing of the concentrations across the two communities, as
illustrated in Figures 4.3, 4.5, and 4.4.




          (a) Laplace, λ = 0.5                          (b) Mellin, s = 1




           (c) Laplace, λ = 2                           (d) Mellin, s = 3

Figure 4.3: Diffusion under the Laplace d-path transform for several λ values
(left) and Mellin d-path transform for several s values (right). The lines repre-
sent the concentration profiles of the nodes, with initial condition concentrated
in the blue community.

    As we can see in Fig. 4.3, the diffusion process ruled by the Path Laplacians
converge to the steady state faster than the classical diffusion process. This is be-
cause the long range connections, although weaker, still generate more bridges
between the two communities, avoiding the bottleneck effect in the nodes in the
boundary of the communities. Nonetheless, observing the profiles, we can ob-
serve that the consensus is still reached first inside each community, where the
108           Chapter 4. Long-range diffusion processes in simple graphs




         (a) Laplace, λ = 0.5                       (b) Mellin, s = 1




          (c) Laplace, λ = 2                        (d) Mellin, s = 3

Figure 4.4: Concentration profiles among nodes under the Laplace d-path
transform (left) and the Mellin d-path transform (right), both normalized so
that the interactions with direct neighbors weight 1. The lines represent the
concentration profiles of the nodes, with initial condition concentrated in the
blue community
4.4 Diffusion processes under the Path Laplacian                           109




         (a) Laplace, λ = 0.5                       (b) Mellin, s = 1




          (c) Laplace, λ = 2                        (d) Mellin, s = 3

Figure 4.5: Concentration profiles among nodes under the Laplace d-path
transform (left) and the Mellin d-path transform (right), both normalized so
that the weights sum up to 1. The lines represent the concentration profiles of
the nodes, with initial condition concentrated in the blue community
      110             Chapter 4. Long-range diffusion processes in simple graphs

      strong connections are located, meaning that the first order topology still leads
      the process.
          One may observe that the Subfigure 4.3c seems to be an exception to this
      rule, as the diffusion process does not reach the steady state and seems equal or
      slower to the classical diffusion process. But this is due to the weights used in
      this transformation. Contrary to the Mellin transform, that always asigns weight
      equal to 1 to the direct neighbors, the Laplace transform assigns them the weight
      e−λ which is much smaller than 1 for bigger values of λ. To normalize these
      operators and compare them, we can choose two ways of interpreting them.
          The first way consist in multiplying the weights so that the direct neighbors
      have weight 1, and its shown in Fig. 4.5. There we see that all cases are faster than
      the classical diffusion process as, on top of that diffusion, we still have the long
      range connections that help to bridge the two communities, that we can interpret
      as neglected by the classical approach.
          The second way, shown in Fig. 4.4, is to normalize the weights so that the
      sum of the weights is equal to 1. This way, we are assuming that the diffusion
      coefficient is equal but distributed among the direct and indirect (long-range)
      connections. In this Figure we still observe that any of the Path Laplacian dif-
      fusion processes are faster than the classical one, as the existence of long-range
      connections continues to facilitate the diffusion process between communities. ■
      ■   Example 4.2 We have observed that the diffusion process under the Path Lapla-
      cian operator is faster than the classical diffusion process through the concentra-
      tion profiles, but we can quantify this through several other quantities. One very
      common in the study of diffusion processes on networks is through the spectral
      gap of the operator. This quantity is related to the speed of convergence to the
      steady state as seen in Proposition 4.1, as a larger spectral gap implies a faster
      convergence.
          We can use this quantity to compare several values of the parameters λ and s
      for the Laplace and Mellin transforms, respectively, and explore several graphs.
                                                                                         ■




4.5   Superdiffusive processes
      We have already seen that the diffusion process under the Path Laplacian oper-
      ator is faster than the classical diffusion process, but we can go further and ask
      whether these operators are in fact superdiffusive, as happens for the fractional
      Laplacian in the continuous case. This question was studied in [91, 92] and we
4.5 Superdiffusive processes                                             111




(a) SBM two communities (35, 35; pin =           (b) Cycle (n = 30)
0.2, pout = 0.01)




 (c) Barabási–Albert (n = 40, m = 2)     (d) Erdős–Rényi (n = 40, p = 0.10)




          (e) Star (n = 31)                      (f) Path (n = 30)

Figure 4.6: Algebraic connectivity λ2 under d-Path Laplacians across graphs.

112             Chapter 4. Long-range diffusion processes in simple graphs

summarize their results here.
    To answer it, we first clarify what we mean by a superdiffusive process on
a network. These processes are not only quantitatively faster than normal dif-
fusion; they are qualitatively different, with probability distributions that have
heavier tails than the Gaussian distribution characterizing normal diffusion. This
general definition entails different scaling laws for several observables, some of
which we summarize below.
    A diffusive process can be characterized by the mean squared displacement
(MSD), which measures how far the particles have moved from their initial posi-
tion over time. On a network, we can write
                                         1 X 2
                           MSD(t) =          di,i0 xi (t),                        (4.16)
                                        |V |
                                            i∈V

where di,i0 is the shortest-path distance between node i and the initial node i0 ,
and xi (t) is the (probability) mass at node i at time t. In a normal diffusive process,
MSD(t) ∼ t. In a superdiffusive process, MSD(t) ∼ tβ with β > 1, indicating
faster-than-linear spread and a change in the shape of the distribution.
    Nonetheless, probability distributions that underlie the generalized d-path
dynamics may produce jumps so long that the second moment diverges, in which
case the MSD is not well defined. It is therefore convenient to track observables
that remain meaningful in the heavy-tailed regime. Two such quantities are:
      • Full width at half maximum (FWHM) of the distribution, which measures
        how the width between the two points where the profile takes half of its
        maximum value grows with time; and
      • Decay of the maximum value of the distribution with time.
For normal diffusion one has FWHM(t) ∼ t1/2 and max xi (t) ∼ t−1/2 . In a
                                                              i
superdiffusive process the typical scaling is FWHM(t) ∼ t1/α and max xi (t) ∼
                                                                            i
t−n/α with α < 2 and n being the dimension of the space. In particular, α = 1
yields ballistic scaling.
    In [91, 92] the authors investigate these quantities for several transformed d-
path Laplacians on the infinite path graph and on the infinite square lattice, where
closed-form Fourier representations permit explicit asymptotics. They prove that
the Laplace and factorial-transformed d-path Laplacians always produce normal
diffusion with Gaussian distributions. By contrast, the Mellin transformed oper-
ator produces superdiffusion for small enough values of s. Concretely:

      law of the form

   R    If we calculate the growth parameter τ from the condition 4.7 for the in-
        finite path and the infinite square lattice, we obtain τ = 0 and τ = 1,
        respectively. Thus, as the Mellin transform requires Re(s) > τ + 1 to
        be well defined, we see that the superdiffusive regime is attained in both
        cases for the lowest possible values of s that ensure the convergence of the
        series defining the operator. This gives the intuition that the superdiffu-
        sion happens when the hopping probability does not decay too fast with
        the distance.




          (a) Profiles at t = 1.                        (b) Profiles at t = 16.

Figure 4.7: Classical vs Mellin profiles (solid = diffusive, dashed = superdif-
fusive).

    In Fig. 4.7 we illustrate these results with numerical simulations of the dif-
fusion process under the Mellin-transformed d-path Laplacian on finite approxi-
mations of the infinite path and the infinite square lattice. We observe for small
times that the shape of the distributions are very different between the superdif-
fusive and the normal diffusive regimes, with heavier tails and a sharper peak in
the first case. For large times, one can observe the Gaussian shape of the normal
diffusive operators, whereas the superdiffusive ones are almost a plateau, and the
center of the distribution resembles a Normal distribution as the boundary effects
4.5 Superdiffusive processes                                                     115

of the finite graph start to be relevant on the simulation.
    Apart from the shape of the distributions, we can also observe the scaling
properties of the FWHM and the peak height in Fig. 4.8. Using the theoretical
previous results, one can prove that the scaling properties of these superdiffusive
processes are given by

      FWHM(t) ∼ t1/(s−1) ,       fmax (t) ∼ t−1/(s−1)    for 1 < s < 3,        (4.23)

in the case of Z, and by

      FWHM(t) ∼ t1/(s−2) ,       fmax (t) ∼ t−2/(s−2)    for 2 < s < 4,        (4.24)

in the case of Z2 . On the other hand, either for the Mellin transform with s greater
than the previous values or for the Laplace transform for any λ > 0, we have the
normal diffusive scaling.




                (a)                               (b) FWHM.
                fmax .

Figure 4.8: Scaling properties on Z for the Mellin-transformed d-path Lapla-
cian.

    In Fig. 4.8 we can see how the numerical simulations fit these theoretical
results for the infinite path with high accuracy. The case s = 1.5 is the only
one not fitting the theoretical scaling. This is due to the fact that for such small
values of s, the movement is almost ballistic, and the finite size of the graph used
in the simulation starts to be relevant, leading to a saturation of the FWHM and
the peak height.
    This effect is non-trivial, and fitting the data one can observe that the scaling
      116           Chapter 4. Long-range diffusion processes in simple graphs

      exponent may resemble a normal diffusive process, even when we know from
      the theoretical results that the process is superdiffusive. This lead to the study
      of truncated Lévy flights in [92], where the authors prove that, when the graph
      is finite, the superdiffusive regime is not preserved for all times. This is, when
      the probability of long jumps is too high, the particles reach the boundary of the
      graph too fast and the process ends up being normal diffusive. Thus, we can only
      observe the superdiffusive regime for very small times, and this short window is
      determined by an interplay between the size of the graph and the parameter s,
      specifically how far it is the parameter from the ballistic value.


4.6   Other non-local Laplacians on graphs
      Along this Chapter, we have presented the d-path Laplacian matrices as a way
      of generalizing the classical Laplacian matrix to include long-range interactions
      in a graph, preserving properties simmilar to those of the fractional Laplacian in
      the continuous case. Nonetheless, this is not the only way of defining such non-
      local operators on graphs, following the same premise of resemble some of the
      properties of the fractional Laplacian.
          The alternative to the d-path Laplacian matrices are the so-called fractional
      Laplacian matrices. These operators, defined in [215], are based directly on pow-
      ering the classical Laplacian matrix, instead of constructing new operators based
      on the topology of the graph.
        Definition 4.6 Let G be a simple graph and let L be its Laplacian matrix. Then,
        the Fractional Laplacian matrix of order γ, with 0 < γ ≤ 1, is defined as:

                                        Lγ = QΛγ QT ,                              (4.25)

        where Q is the matrix of eigenvectors of L and Λ is the diagonal matrix of
        eigenvalues of L.

           This operator is well defined as the Laplacian matrix is symmetric and positive
      semidefinite, so it has a spectral decomposition with non-negative eigenvalues.
      Moreover, its eigenvalues are direct to compute, as they are just the eigenvalues
      of L powered to γ. This is a property of the fractional Laplacian in Rn too, as
      its eigenvalues are the eigenvalues of the classical Laplacian powered to γ. In
      general, it preserves all of the basic properties of the classical Laplacian such as
      the positive semidefiniteness, the existence of a zero eigenvalue with constant
      eigenvector, or the mass conservation in the associated diffusion process.
4.6 Other non-local Laplacians on graphs                                         117

     If we restrict ourselves to the ring graph, one can prove that the probabilities
of particles following these operators are Lévy flights, as shown in [215]. This
is, the probability of jumping from node i to node j decays as a power law of
the distance between them, resembling the behavior of the Mellin-transformed
d-path Laplacian and being the base of construction of the fractional Laplacian
in Rn as done in [72].
     Thus, one might think that these two operators are equivalent, as they both
resemble the fractional Laplacian in Rn and have similar properties. However,
this is not the case, as we can observe in the following example.




                (a)                                 (b) FWHM.
                fmax .

Figure 4.9: Scaling properties on Z for the Fractional Laplacian and the Mellin-
transformed d-path Laplacian.

    In Fig. 4.9 we can see the FWHM and the peak height for the diffusion process
under the Mellin-transformed d-path Laplacian and the fractional Laplacian on
a ring graph with 4096 nodes. These quantities show similarities for long times,
showing that both processes are superdiffusive, at least in the infinite path graph
Z. Nonetheless, for short times both processes show a very different behviour,
when the fractional Laplacians diffuses much slower. This effect can be further
observed in the concentration profiles in Fig. 4.10, where we can see that the
fractional Laplacian produces a very sharp peak and almost no diffusion for short
times, slower even than the usual diffusion (case γ = 1), whereas the Mellin-
transformed d-path Laplacian produces a more spread distribution. Nonetheless,
for longer times this difference dissapears and the Fractional Laplacian profile
spreads faster.
    The explanation for this is further from trivial and was studied in [85]. Through
studying the differences of the operators in a triangle and a 3-path graph, one can
118                                                             Chapter 4. Long-range diffusion processes in simple graphs




                                 (a) Profiles at t = 0.5.                                                                                                                           (b) Profiles at t = 2.

                                 Figure 4.10: Fractional vs Mellin profiles for different times.


observe that the fractional Laplacian backtracks the diffusion process, allowing
particles to stay in the initial node for long times, whereas the Mellin-transformed
d-path Laplacian does not allow this. This is due to the fact that the fractional
Laplacian is constructed with the eigenvectors of the classical Laplacian, so it still
gives a higher importance to the local structure of the graph, whereas the d-path
Laplacian is constructed to directly model long-range interactions.

Figure 4.11: Diffusion processes on the Karate club graph for the classical
Laplacian, the Mellin-transformed d-path Laplacian with s = 2 and the frac-
tional Laplacian with γ = 0.5.

     Moreover, while the d-path Laplacian is always constructed to produce long
range jumps, the fractional Laplacian only produces Lévy flights in specific graphs,
such as the ring graph. In general, the probability of jumping from node i to node
j is not a function of the distance between them, but rather a more complicated
function of the topology of the graph. This leads to the counterintuitive result
that, in some graphs, the fractional Laplacian may produce a diffusion process
      4.7 Conclusions                                                                   119

      slower than the classical one, as shown in [85]. This happens specifically for
      graphs whose algebraic connectivity λ2 (L) is greater than 1, as the fractional
      power of the eigenvalues then produces a smaller algebraic connectivity λ2 (Lγ ),
      leading to a slower process. This can be seen in Fig. 4.11, where it is used the well-
      known Karate club graph [270], whose algebraic connectivity is λ2 (L) ≈ 1.1871.
      In this case, we can observe that the diffusion process approaches the consensus
      state first for the Mellin-transformed d-path Laplacian, then the classical diffusion
      process, and finally the fractional Laplacian diffusion process.

4.7   Conclusions
      The Fractional Laplacian in Rn is a non-local operator that has been widely stud-
      ied in the last decades due to its applications in several fields. Its definition
      through the Fourier transform makes it difficult to generalize it to discrete struc-
      tures such as graphs, but the d-path Laplacian matrices provide a way of defin-
      ing non-local operators on general graphs that resemble many of the properties
      of the fractional Laplacian in Rn . In particular, the Mellin-transformed d-path
      Laplacian produces superdiffusive processes on some infinite graphs, such as the
      infinite path and the infinite square lattice, resembling the behavior of the frac-
      tional Laplacian in Rn . Moreover, they are based in the same principle of Lévy
      flights, where the probability of jumping from one node to another decays as a
      power law of the distance between them, and so, they share several properties.
          Thus, these d-path Laplacian matrices provide a way of defining non-local
      operators on graphs that can be used as a theoretical framework to support appli-
      cations in which long-range interactions are relevant, such as epidemic spreading
      or transportation networks.
    5. Non-conservative processes in
    graphs: Lerman-Ghosh operators



                                                 'Cause you are the piece of me,
                                                 I wish I didn't need
                                                 Chasin' relentlessly, still ght
                                                 and I don't know why
                                                      Clarity - Zedd ft. Foxes



    Historically, the processes of diffusion and reaction have been modeled as
limit of random walks of particles in a defined spaced. Thus, a property preserved
by the operators that model these processes is the preservation of the number of
particles in the system, usually called the mass of the system.

    Nonetheless, in many real-world systems, the number of particles is not pre-
served, as they can be created, destroyed, included into the system from an exter-
nal source, or removed from the system through a sink. All these considerations
are not naturally included in the classical diffusion and reaction operators, and
so, new operators have been proposed to model these processes.

    We will present the Lerman-Ghosh operators in this chapter, a modification
in the construction of the classical Laplacian matrix that modifies the number of
particles that are in each node of the graph, allowing to model processes where
the mass is not preserved.
              Chapter 5. Non-conservative processes in graphs: Lerman-Ghosh
      122                                                          operators




      (a) Example of non-conservative process (b) Example of non-conservative process
      in which mass is added to the system.   in which mass is removed from the system.

      Figure 5.1: Examples of non-conservative processes in which the mass is not
      constant over time as particles are added or removed from the system.


5.1   Constructing a non-conservative operator
      To construct the non-conservative operators, let’s start from the classical Lapla-
      cian matrix L = K − A, where K is the degree matrix and A is the adjacency
      matrix of the graph.
           The Laplacian matrix can be seen as a combination of two operators with
      different purposes. First it is the adjacency operator A, that copies particles from
      one node to its neighbors. Thus, for a given vector of particles u, the operator Au
      produces a new vector where each node has the number of particles that were in
      its neighbors in the previous time step. This operator is the base, for example, of
      the SI model of epidemic spreading, where a susceptible node becomes infected
      if it has an infected neighbor without changing the state of the original infected
      node.
          The second operator is the degree operator K, that removes particles from
      each node. This is done to compensate the addition of particles done by the ad-
                                                                       n
      jacency operator, as the operator K is the diagonal of the sum     Aij , and so,
                                                                      P
                                                                         j=1
      removing the particles that have been copied, the total number of particles in the
      system is preserved.
          Thus, an intuitive way to construct a non-conservative operator is to modify
      the degree operator, to change the number of particles that are removed from
      each node. This way, if we remove less particles than the ones that are added by
      the adjacency operator, the system will increase its number of particles, and, if
      we remove more particles, the system will decrease its number of particles.
5.1 Constructing a non-conservative operator                                  123

   This is done by the operator defined as:

                               Lχ = K + χI − A,                              (5.1)

where I is the identity matrix and χ ∈ R is a parameter that controls the number
of particles that are added or removed from each node. If χ < 0, then the system
is adding |χ| particles to each node, if χ = 0 the system is preserving the number
of particles, and if χ > 0 the system is removing χ particles from each node.
    Thus, we can try to predict the behaviour of the system heuristically based
on the value of χ:
   • If χ < 0, then the system is adding particles to each node, and so, the
     system will increase its number of particles until it explodes.
   • If χ = 0, then the system is preserving the number of particles, and the
     result is the classical diffusion equation.
   • If χ > 0, then the system is removing particles from each node, and so, the
     system will decrease its number of particles until all particles are removed
     from the system.
   This is provided by the following Proposition:

Proposition 5.1 Let G be a graph with adjacency matrix A and degree matrix K.
Let χ ∈ R and consider the operator Lχ = K + χI − A. Then, the diffusion
equation ruled by this operator:

                           du
                              = −Lχ u;     u(0) = u0 ,                       (5.2)
                           dt
has the following behaviour:
   • If χ < 0, then the system diverges as t → ∞ for any non-zero initial
     condition.
   • χ = 0, then the system preserves the number of particles, and converges
     If
     to the consensus state ⃗1 for any non-zero initial condition.
   • If χ > 0, then the system converges to 0 as t → ∞ for any initial condition.

Proof. As the system is a linear ODE, the solution of the equation is given by
u(t) = e−Lχ t u(0).
   Note that Lχ = L + χI, where L = K − A is the classical Laplacian matrix.
Thus, we can diagonalize L as L = QM QT , where Q is an orthogonal matrix
whose columns are the eigenvectors of L, and M is the diagonal matrix with the
              Chapter 5. Non-conservative processes in graphs: Lerman-Ghosh
      124                                                          operators
      eigenvalues of L. Then, as I = QQT , we have that:

                   Lχ = L + χI = QM QT + χQQT = Q(M + χI)QT .                          (5.3)

      Thus, the eigenvalues of Lχ are given by µi + χ, where µi are the eigenvalues of
      L.
         We can join these two results to see the behaviour of the system depending
      on χ:
         • If χ > 0, then all eigenvalues of Lχ are positive, and so, all eigenvalues
            of −Lχ are negative. Thus, all the terms in the matrix exponential e−Lχ t
            converge to 0 as t → ∞, and so, the system converges to the zero vector
            for any initial condition.
         • If χ = 0, then Lχ = L, and so, the system is the classical diffusion equation.
            In this case, the system preserves the number of particles and converges to a
            state proportional to the eigenvector associated to the eigenvalue 0, which
            is the vector ⃗1.
         • If χ < 0, then at least the smallest eigenvalue of Lχ is negative, as it is
            equal to χ. Thus, at least one eigenvalue of −Lχ is positive, and the corre-
            sponding term to this eigenvalue in the exponential diverges as t → ∞ for
            any non-zero initial condition.
                                                                                   q.e.d.


5.2   Lerman-Ghosh Operators
      Eventhough we have defined a non-conservative operator with a direct inter-
      pretation, there is another way to proceed which has been more studied in the
      literature.
           To follow the construction of this different operator, we will start by restrict-
      ing ourselves to the case of regular graphs 2.12, where all nodes have the same
      degree k. In this case, the degree operator is K = kI. Hence, modifying could be
      seen as changing the value of k to a different value χ, and the difference k −χ will
      be the number of particles that are added or removed from each node, depending
      on the sign of the difference.
           This way, we can use the operator χI instead of the degree operator K to
      define the Lerman-Ghosh operator:
5.2 Lerman-Ghosh Operators                                                      125




(a) χ = −1: node concentrations vs. time. (b) χ = 0: node concentrations vs. time.




(c) χ = 1: node concentrations vs. time.          (d) Total mass vs. time.

Figure 5.2: Dynamics on the grasshopper graph for u̇ = −(K − A + χI)u
with random u0 summing up to 1. Subfigures show node-wise concentrations
for χ ∈ {−1, 0, 1} and the total mass for all three cases.


  Definition 5.1 Let G be a graph with adjacency matrix A. Let χ ∈ R. The
  Lerman-Ghosh operator Lχ is defined as:

                                  Lχ = χI − A.                                (5.4)

   This operator was firstly defined in [114], where it was defined through a
change in the random walk process to make it non-conservative, arriving to the
operator Lχ as the infinitesimal generator of the process.
    Note that, in the original definition of [114], the operator is defined for χ <
λ1 (A), where λ1 (A) is the largest eigenvalue of the adjacency matrix A. This
is done due to the objective of the article, where the steady state of the diffusive
process related to Lχ is related to the centrality studied therein. However, we can
define these operators for any real value of χ, and we will study the differences
of the operators in these regimes:

Proposition 5.2 Let G be a graph with adjacency matrix A, and let χ ∈ R. Let
λ1 ≥ λ2 ≥ · · · ≥ λn be the eigenvalues of A and call I to the identity matrix of
         Chapter 5. Non-conservative processes in graphs: Lerman-Ghosh
126                                                                          operators
the same size as A. Let Lχ be the Lerman-Ghosh operator defined in 5.1. Then,
the eigenvalues of Lχ are µi = χ − λi , for i = 1, . . . , n. In particular:
    • If χ > λ1 , then all eigenvalues of Lχ are positive.
    • If χ = λ1 , then Lχ is positive semi-definite, with a single eigenvalue equal
      to 0.
    • χ < λ1 , then Lχ has at least one negative eigenvalue.
      If

Proof. Using the eigendecomposition of A, we have that A = QΛQT , where Q
is an orthogonal matrix whose columns are the eigenvectors of A, and Λ is a
diagonal matrix with the eigenvalues of A. Then, as I = QQT , we have that:

              Lχ = χI − A = χQQT − QΛQT = Q(χI − Λ)QT .                          (5.5)

Thus, the eigenvalues of Lχ are given by the diagonal elements of the matrix
χI − Λ, which are µi = χ − λi , for i = 1, . . . , n. The rest of the statements
follow directly from this expression.                                      q.e.d.

    This eigendecomposition allows to compute easily the exponential of the op-
erator, that will be useful to define diffusive processes based on this operator:

 Theorem 5.1 Let G be a graph with adjacency matrix A, and let χ ∈ R. Let
 λ1 be the greatest eigenvalue of A with v1 its corresponding eigenvector and
 call I to the identity matrix of the same size as A. Let Lχ be the Lerman-
 Ghosh operator defined in 5.1 and consider the diffusion equation ruled by this
 operator:
                          du
                              = −Lχ u; u(0) = u0 .                         (5.6)
                           dt
 Then, the solution of this equation is given by u(t) = e−Lχ t u(0) and the steady
 state of the system is:
     • If χ > λ1 , then the steady state is u(t) → 0 as t → ∞ for any initial
        condition.
     • If χ = λ1 , then the steady state u(t) is proportional to the eigencentrality
        vector v1 for any initial condition different than zero.
     • If χ < λ1 , then the system diverges as t → ∞ for all non-zero initial
        conditions.

Proof. Using the properties of the matrix exponential it is direct to see that the
solution of the diffusion equation is given by u(t) = e−Lχ t u(0).
5.2 Lerman-Ghosh Operators                                                       127

    Using the Proposition 5.2, we have that the eigenvalues of −Lχ are given by
λi − χ, for i = 1, . . . , n. Thus, the eigenvalues of the matrix exponential e−Lχ t
are given by e(λi −χ)t , for i = 1, . . . , n. Thus, the behaviour of the system as
t → ∞ is determined by the sign of λ1 − χ:
    • If χ > λ1 , then all eigenvalues of −Lχ are negative, and so, all eigenvalues
      of e−Lχ t converge to 0 as t → ∞. Thus, the system converges to the zero
      vector for any initial condition.
    • If χ = λ1 , then λ1 − χ = 0 is the smallest eigenvalue of −Lχ , and so,
      for long times, the system is dominated by the eigenvector v1 associated to
      this eigenvalue. Thus, the system converges to a vector proportional to v1
      for any initial condition different than zero.
    • If χ < λ1 , then the term λ1 − χ is positive, and so, e(χ−λ1 )t diverges for
      any non-zero initial condition.
                                                                              q.e.d.

     If we go back to the case of G a regular graph, it is easy to see what is the
effect of the parameter χ in the dynamics of the system, as it will be the same
as for the operator Lχ . The first thing to note is that, if G is k-regular, then the
largest eigenvalue of A is λ1 = k, with associated eigenvector v1 = 1, the vector
of all ones. Thus, the three regimes of the system are given by:
     • If χ < k, then the system is copying particles from each node to its neigh-
        bors, but just removing χ particles from each node. Thus, the system is
        increasing the number of particles in n(k − χ) each time step, and so, it
        explodes as it is always growing.
     • If χ = k, then Lχ = kI − A is the classical Laplacian operator, and so, the
        system preserves the number of particles. In this case, the system converges
        to a state where all nodes have the same number of particles, proportional
        to the initial number of particles in the system.
     • If χ > k, then the system is removing more particles from each node than
        the ones that are being normally diffused to its neighbors. Thus, the sys-
        tem loses particles each time step until all particles are removed from the
        system, and so, it converges to the zero vector.
     If we consider now a non-regular graph, the interpretation of the parameter
χ is not as direct as in the regular case, as the degree of each node is different.
It is still valid that, for a node with degree ki , if χ < ki the node is increasing
its number of particles, if χ = ki the node is preserving its number of particles,
and if χ > ki the node is losing particles. However, as the degree of each node
        Chapter 5. Non-conservative processes in graphs: Lerman-Ghosh
128                                                          operators




(a) χ = λ1 − 1 = 1.343: node concentra- (b) χ = λ1 = 2.343: node concentrations
tions vs. time.                         vs. time.




(c) χ = λ1 + 1 = 3.343: node concentra-
tions vs. time.                                     (d) Total mass vs. time.

Figure 5.3: Dynamics on the grasshopper graph for u̇ = −Lχ u with random
u0 summing up to 1. Subfigures show node-wise concentrations for χ ∈ {λ1 −
1, λ1 , λ1 + 1} and the total mass for all three cases.


is different, it would not be possible to know the behaviour of the system doing
a reasoning as for the regular case. Nonetheless, the results of Theorem 5.1 still
hold, and so, the three regimes of the system are still determined by the sign of
χ − λ1 . If we remember that λ1 is bounded by the maximum degree of the graph,
we see that this means that, if λ1 < χ < kmax , where kmax is the maximum
degree of the graph, then there are nodes that are increasing their number of
particles, but the global behaviour of the system is still to lose particles until all
particles are removed from the system. The same can be seen from the other side,
if kmin < χ < λ1 , where kmin is the minimum degree of the graph, then there
are nodes that are losing particles, but the global behaviour of the system is still
to increase the number of particles until it explodes.
   Comparing Lχ and Lχ , both produce the same three qualitative regimes but
with different thresholds: Lχ transitions at χ = λ1 (A), which depends on the
graph, whereas Lχ transitions at χ = 0, independently of the graph. The operator
      5.3 Conclusions                                                                 129

      Lχ is more directly interpretable because the same amount is removed or injected
      at every node, while Lχ is more inhomogeneous and the system can grow or
      decay even if some nodes locally lose or gain mass. At the critical values the
      asymptotics differ: for Lχ with χ = 0 the total mass is conserved and the state
      converges to consensus; for Lχ with χ = λ1 the state aligns with the leading
      eigenvector of A and, although mass is not conserved along the trajectory, it
      stabilizes to a finite, nonzero limit that depends on u0 and the eigencentrality
      vector.
          Thus, even if both operators are even the same for regular graphs and have
      some very important similarities, they are different operators with different prop-
      erties and interpretations, and so, the choice of one or the other will depend on
      the application at hand.

5.3   Conclusions
      In this chapter we have presented two families of non-conservative operators,
      that can be used to model diffusion processes where the number of particles is not
      preserved. These operators are based on a modification of the classical Laplacian
      matrix, changing the degree operator by a different operator that removes or adds
      a fixed number of particles to each node.
           We have studied the properties of these operators, and we have seen that both
      operators have three different regimes depending on the value of the parameter
      χ. In one regime, the system explodes, in another regime the system stabilizes in
      a constant mass and in the last regime the system loses all its mass.
           Between the two operators, the Lerman-Ghosh operator Lχ is the one that
      has been more studied in the literature, as it shows a more complex behaviour.
      First, it shows these three different regimes in cases in which some nodes are
      locally losing mass while others are locally gaining mass. Second, in the critical
      regime, the system usually does not conserve the total mass, but it still stabilizes
      and the steady state is related to the leading eigenvector of the adjacency matrix,
      which is a very interesting property for applications.
