     In many engineering systems a group of agents, in contrast with a single one,
are organized to execute a mission or perform a task with better operational capa-
bility and efficiency. These multi-agent systems (MAS) perform tasks like forma-
tion flight of unmanned air vehicles, clusters of satellites, self-organization, au-
tomated highway systems, and congestion control in communication networks,
among others. In performing these tasks the agents are coordinated through the
so-called linear or nonlinear consensus protocols [4, 132, 169, 187, 225]. The MAS
is usually represented by a graph G = (V, E) where the set of vertices V repre-
sents the agents and the set of edges E represents their interactions, which are
encoded in the adjacency matrix A = [Aij ]n×n , where n = #V is the number of
vertices in the graph. Then, the state of the agent i at the time t is described by
134                 Chapter 6. The non-conservative logistic growth model

                                     n
                                     X
                         u̇i (t) =         Aij (uj (t) − ui (t)) ,               (6.1)
                                     j=1

in the linear protocol, where u̇i (t) = dui (t) /dt, or by

                                   n
                                   X
                       u̇i (t) =         Aij ϕij {uj (t) − ui (t)} ,             (6.2)
                                   j=1

in the nonlinear one, where ϕij {· · · } is an action function obeying a given set of
conditions [225]. The models of synchronization on networks, which are widely
and extensively studied in the literature [12, 263], belong to this class of nonlinear
consensus protocols.
    When the linear consensus protocol is expressed in matrix-vector form (see
Preliminaries), the model is easily recognizable as the diffusion equation on the
graph. Diffusion is a very ubiquitous phenomenon in many real-world complex
systems ranging from molecular to social and ecological ones [182]. Then, it is not
unexpected that many MAS were originally inspired by the diffusive processes
occurring in biological systems in nature, such as the labour between ant colonies,
parade of fish schools, formation of bird groups and cooperative hunting of the
herd, foraging and migration patterns, etc. [209].
    Traditionally, diffusion processes on networks have been modeled by means
of conservative models, meaning that the total number of particles in the sys-
tem remains constant over time [86]. The cases of linear and nonlinear diffusive
models expressed before belongs to the class of conservative processes. However,
there are many processes on real-world complex systems which are nonconserva-
tive on the graphs representing the skeleton of the systems. They include traffic
in urban environments due to the emergence and sinking of cars from/to park-
ing spaces which make their number on the streets of a city is not constant over
time. Other examples include the diffusive neurotransmission in neuronal sys-
tems, where the so-called volume transmission is clearly nonconservative in the
neuronal network, the communication in social media like X or Facebook where
an user can post a message which can be read by her followers, but also (if not
constrained by the user) by non-followers, as well as the diffusive processes in
ecology which include births and death of species, the diffusion of microplastics
and other materials in the ocean, among others (see [86] and references therein).
     Another common characteristic of the (linear and nonlinear) consensus mod-
els is the obvious fact that the final steady state of the system is characterized by
      6.1 Preliminaries                                                               135

      a global consensus in the state of the agents of the system. That is, when t → ∞,
      uj (t) − ui (t) = 0 for every pair of agents in the system. This consensus state is
      not always observed in real-world systems, as different groups of people can have
      different opinions, animals can split into diverse groups to perform a task, etc. In
      the case of engineering systems it is also possible that states in which there is a
      heterogeneous consensus, i.e., groups of agents performing actions different from
      other groups, is desirable. Here, we conduct research towards the development of
      a model which accounts for both, nonconservativeness and heterogeneity in the
      steady state. We develop our approach on the basis of the Lerman-Ghosh Lapla-
      cian operators defined in Chapter 5.2, which generates nonconservative diffusion
      when plugged into the graph diffusion equation. However, it possesses a finite
      non-zero steady state for only a single value of the parameter χ, limiting its ap-
      plicability in many contexts [86]. Therefore, we propose to develop a logistic-like
      model incorporating this operator, ensuring that the system’s solutions remain
      bounded, specifically by the value of 1, are nonconservative and not necessarily
      homogeneous. We start by deriving the new model through various approaches,
      continue with several applications of this model to the study of heterogeneous
      rendezvous of agents in the unit square, studying the diffusion of innovations in
      a social system adapting the Bass model for it, and by analyzing the connections
      between the new model and the Lotka-Volterra equations for studying interac-
      tions between plant pollinators in an ecosystem.

6.1   Preliminaries

      for some specific value of the parameter χ for regular graphs. Nonetheless, they
      generate very much different results when plugged into the diffusion equation on
      graphs. In particular, using the spectral decomposition of the operator, we obtain
      that the long term behavior of the diffusion process is given by:

    Before proceeding let us clarify what we will understand here by the stabil-
ity of the solutions of the model studied. An equilibrium is said to be stable in
the sense of Lyapunov if for every ε > 0, there exists a δ > 0 such that any tra-
jectory starting within a δ-neighborhood of the equilibrium remains within the
ε-neighborhood for all future times [177], meaning that moving slightly away
from the solution still leaves the system close to the solution. While Lyapunov
stability guarantees that trajectories remain close to an equilibrium, asymptotic
stability requires that these trajectories actually converge to the equilibrium as
time goes to infinity [158]. More formally, an equilibrium point is asymptotically
stable if it is Lyapunov stable and there exists a δ > 0 such that every solution
starting in the δ-neighborhood converges to the equilibrium as t → ∞.
                                                                              ∂f
    As usual we will consider the eigenvalues of the Jacobian, J =            ∂x x=x ,
                                                                                      e
where u̇ = f (u) as the criteria of stability. In particular, if none of the eigenvalues
of the Jacobian is zero, the fixed point is said to be hyperbolic and nonlinear dy-
namics resemble those of their linearization–according to the Hartman–Grobman
theorem [138]. Therefore, fixed points with all the eigenvalues of their Jacobian
negative will be asymptotically stable while fixed points with at least one positive
eigenvalue of their Jacobian will be unstable. In cases where some eigenvalues
have zero real parts, the linear analysis is inconclusive, and higher-order terms
must be considered. In a neighborhood of a hyperbolic fixed point, the phase
space can be decomposed into invariant subspaces known as the stable and un-
stable manifolds. The stable manifold consists of all trajectories that converge to
the fixed point as t → ∞, while the unstable manifold comprises trajectories that
diverge from the fixed point in forward time [128]. This can be summarised as,
for such points, some directions are stable while the others are not. Here we refer
to “direction” in the following way. In a similar way as ∂f     ∂x is the change of f in
the direction of x, here we refer to du̇i /duj as the change of the vertex i in the
direction of j.
    Let us designate ui (∞) := lim ui (t). Then, the following are general ob-
                                   t→∞
servations of the behavior of the mean final state of the nonconservative logistic
model in P4 , C4 , and S1,3 :
   1. For χ < δ, ui (∞) = 1 for all i ∈ V ;
   2. For χ > λ1 , ui (∞) = 0 for all i ∈ V . However, there are also several
      unstable branches appearing for , due to the fact that u0i = 1 for some
140                 Chapter 6. The non-conservative logistic growth model




            (a)                         (b)                         (c)

Figure 6.2: Steady states numerically obtained for different graphs with 4
nodes, respectively the cycle C4 , the path P4 and the star S1,3 .


      vertex i, then ui (t) = 1 for any t > 0, so the system falls in an steady state
      that is only unstable in the direction of that specific vertex;
   3. For (δ, λ1 ) there is a stable steady state in (0, 1);
   4. For χ = λ1 there is not a discontinuous transition to ui (∞) = 0 for all
      i ∈ V , but several values in the interval between them are reachable, de-
      pending on the initial conditions. There is a sigmoidal shape around this
      point, which is a computational artifact emerging by the fact that the sys-
      tem enters in a transient (quasistable) zone, caused by the proximity to a
      bifurcation as explained by [139].
    Our main goal here is to study the stable solutions in the interval (0, 1). How-
ever, to gain insights into them we will start by exploring ruling out the trivial
solutions which may appear in this interval. It is straighforward to realize that
the solutions u (t) = 1 and u (t) = 0 are steady states of the equation 6.5. To
study their stability, we linearize the system around these solutions and calculate
the eigenvalues of the corresponding Jacobian. First, we calculate the derivative
of each component with respect to every direction:
                           
                                                            if i = j,
                                           P
                           
                           
                           −χ + 2χui −         uj
                    ∂ u̇i              (i,j)∈E
          (J)ij =         = 1−u                             if (j, i) ∈ E,      (6.8)
                    ∂uj    
                              i

                                                            otherwise.
                           
                            0
                           


   Thus, when substituting for u (t) = 0, we obtain that the Jacobian is exactly
J = −χI + A = −Lχ . The solution is stable if and only if all the eigenvalues
      6.3 Searching for stable solutions in (0, 1)                                        141

      of the Jacobian are negative. Then, because the eigenvalues of Lχ are λi − χ, we
      conclude that u (t) = 0 is stable if χ > λ1 . On the other hand, if u (t) = 1 then
      the Jacobian is: J = K − χI, whose eigenvalues are: ki − χ. Hence, if χ < δ, all
      of eigenvalues are negative, obtaining the range for which 1 is an stable solution.
      This solution obviously includes the case χ = 0 that was analyzed previously by
      Estrada [86]. Finally, considering the stability of the two solutions given before
      and using the fact that: δ ≤ k̄ ≤ λ1 , with equality if and only if the graph is
      degree-regular, we conclude that for the interval (δ, λ1 ) there is a stable steady
      state in the interval (0, 1).
           Apart from u (t) = 0 and u (t) = 1, another trivial steady state that should
      be ruled out is the one corresponding to the eigenvector corresponding to the
      0-eigenvalue of Lχ , if it exists. We have seen that, using the first two solutions
      (u (t) = 0 and u (t) = 1) and assuming that the initial condition 0 ⪯ u0 ⪯ 1,
      all values of u (t) must lie in the interval (0, 1). On the other hand, if λi are the
      eigenvalues of the adjacency matrix of the graph, then the eigenvalues of Lχ are
      λi − χ. Thus, if we consider all values χ < λ1 it is true that there is a value
      of χ = λj̸=1 , which makes λj − χ = 0. In other words, by considering all
      possible values of χ < λ1 , for any graph there is always an eigenvalue equal to
      zero. Therefore, any of these zero-eigenvalues are steady states of the equation.
      On the other hand, they are unacceptable, in the sense that they must have at
      least one negative entry and then do not obey the condition that 0 ⪯ u (t) ⪯ 1.
      Consequently, the only acceptable solution of this kind is for χ = λ1 , and the
      steady state is given by any multiple of ψ1 (the corresponding eigenvector), whose
      entries are known as the eigenvector centrality, such that 0 ⪯ cψ1 ⪯ 1 is ensured.


6.3   Searching for stable solutions in (0, 1)
      As we have seen, the stable solutions for χ ∈ (δ, λ1 ] usually are neither 0 nor 1.
      Thus, we have to further study the stable solutions of the equation 6.5. In other
      words, we will investigate here how to locate those critical points in the diagrams
      of “mean final state” vs. “χ”. To be clear, we call here critical points to those values
      of of χ where there is a change in the solutions of the model. In the first section
      we ruled out all the trivial steady states, which include the ones dealing with the
      whole Lerman-Ghosh operator. The rest of these steady states are given by the
      fact that the term (1 − u) is local, which means that ui = 1 is an steady state for
      a vertex i independently of the values of the rest of the vertices.
          The conditions under which ui ≡ 1 can happen dare the following ones.
142                  Chapter 6. The non-conservative logistic growth model

      • The initial condition is already u0i = 1, which doesn’t change with time.
      • The only stable steady state for such a vertex and the given value of χ is 1.
        As we have seen this is equivalent to set χ to be smaller than the degree of
        i.
We then propose an algorithm that allow us to find solutions for model under
study, available at [191]. For that purpose, let us start by defining the vector
di to be the number of neighbors of node i for which ui (∞) = 1. Let G′ de-
note the graph obtained by removing from the original graph G all vertices for
which ui (∞) = 1. Let us denote by Lχ (G′ ) the Lerman-Ghosh Laplacian of the
modified graph. Then, it can be easily seen that finding the steady states for the
remaining nodes is equivalent to solving:

                    Lχ (G)ui (∞) = 0 ←→ Lχ (G′ )ui (∞) = d.                     (6.9)

    It has been previously demonstrated that non-trivial steady states emerge for
χ values within the range between δ and λ1 . We have observed by computational
experiments the following result which we have not been able to prove:
Conjecture 6.1 Let u and v be two steady states of the equation and let χu < χv
be the critical values of χ for each solution. That is, solution u only exists if
χ ≥ χu . Let i and j be two vertices on the graph such that ki < kj . Then, if
ui (∞) = 1 (vi < 1) it implies that vi (∞) = 1 (ui (∞) < 1), and ui (∞) = 1
implies uj (∞) = 1.
    This conjecture shows that stable solutions for higher-degree nodes emerge
at later times than those obtained for lower-degree nodes. Thus, based on this
we develop the following algorithm for any given graph, which allows to find
solutions of the model in an iterative way:
   1. Start by assigning the value ui (∞) = 1 to nodes with the highest degree,
      which is similar to forcing them to obey the first condition in 6.3.
   2. Subsequently, remove these nodes to obtain one or more subgraphs.
   3. For each subgraph, write its Lerman-Ghosh Laplacian and apply (6.9) to
      determine the solution for u.
The value of χ at which this solution is valid is determined by ensuring that
u (∞) ⪯ 1. However, it is sufficient to verify this condition only for the vertex
with the highest degree in each subgraph, as it is the last vertex for which the
solution satisfies this constraint. This process is then repeated recursively for
each subgraph, setting the highest degree nodes to 1 in each step. If the conjecture
results to be false then the algorithm continues to be a valid method for finding
6.3 Searching for stable solutions in (0, 1)                                    143

by the vertices {2, 5} ,{3, 6} and {4, 7}. (c) The result of removing the vertex
2 in the subgraph {2, 5}. The subgraphs enclosed by dotted line are the one
for which the equations need to be solved.


the solutions of the model but they can also include steady states which are not
the stable ones. To understand how the algorithm works we give here an example.

    The primary advantage of this approach is that the graphs are divided into
smaller subgraphs as nodes are eliminated, reducing the size of the matrices in-
volved and thus accelerating computations. While this assumption is necessary
for implementing an ordered recursive process, it also simplifies the determina-
tion of χ values by limiting the analysis to the highest-degree nodes at each step
instead of evaluating all nodes. Also, based on numerical experiments we have
        6.3 Searching for stable solutions in (0, 1)                                     145




        Figure 6.4: Illustration of the change of the final state of the dynamics with

        the following conjecture.
        Conjecture 6.2 Let i ∈ V with degree ki . Then, a non-trivial solution to the
        model 6.5 emerges for min{ki − 1, λ1 } < χ ≤ ki , where the first inequality is
        strict if the minimum value is not λ1 .
            The main challenge in deriving a closed-form expression for any graph lies
        in the dependency of the steady states on both the topology of the graph and on
        the initial conditions of the dynamical system. Consequently, obtaining a closed
        formula for χ values would require an explicit characterization of (Lχ (G′ ))−1 for
        every subgraph G′ of the original graph G. This is inherently unachievable for
        arbitrary graphs, due to the impossibility of describing explicitly the determinant
        of the adjacency matrix for any arbitrary graph. We find however some results
        that help in task of finding explicit solutions for some particular cases.

6.3.1   Explicit solutions for low-degree nodes.
        Following a contrary approach to the previous iterative procedure, we can start
        from the bottom and analytically obtain the solutions of special structures inside
        the graph that obey again the conditions 6.3. This is specially useful for the lowest
        degree vertices in the graph, which will be the first ones at which the solution
        ui = 1 loses its stability. The problem with this approach comes from the multiple
        ways in which the lowest degree vertices can be connected between them when
        there are many of such vertices in the graph.
            For that purpose, we define the boundary of a set of nodes ∂S as the set of

6.4   Why using the Lerman-Ghosh operator?

      In the previous sections we have studied the theoretical properties of the logistic
      equation based on the Lerman-Ghosh operator. However, one may wonder why
      this operator is used instead of the other non-conservative operator described
      in 5.1.

          Such operator represents the classical Laplacian operator for χ = 0, which is
      widely used in the literature for the Fisher-KPP equation, plus offers a more direct
      interpretation, as the amount of particles entering or leaving each node is equal,
      and not depends on the degree of the node itself. However, if we perform the
      numerical simulations of the logistic equation based on this operator, we observe
      that the results are not as interesting as the ones obtained with the Lerman-Ghosh
      operator.

          In Fig. 6.6 we illustrate the results of the numerical simulations for the same
      graphs studied in Figs. 6.5a, 6.5b and 6.5c. It can be observed that in all cases,
      the results are the same as for the regular graph in the case of the Lerman-Ghosh
      operator: two discontinuous phases corresponding to the cases u ≡ 0 and u ≡ 1,
      joined by a continuous vertical line at χ = 0, which corresponds to the classical
      consensus equation that yields different values depending on the initial condition.
      Independently of the value of χ, the final state is always homogeneous, i.e., all
      nodes converge to the same value. This is due to the fact that the only stable
      steady states for this operator are either u ≡ 0 or u ≡ 1, depending on whether
      χ is smaller or larger than λ1 . Therefore, no heterogeneous consensus states can
      be obtained with this operator.

          On the other hand, the Lerman-Ghosh operator allows to obtain heteroge-
      neous consensus states, which are more interesting from the point of view of
      applications in MAS and complex systems. Not only that, but it can also be inter-
      preted as a generalization of the Fisher-KPP equation that results in the SI model
      for the diffusion of epidemics in networks. Therefore, the Lerman-Ghosh opera-
      tor offers a richer dynamics that can be used to model more complex scenarios.
      6.5 Heterogeneous consensus in the general context of MAS and complex
      systems                                                                         151




                  (a)                         (b)                        (c)

      Figure 6.6: Stable steady states for the 4-cycle a, 4-star b and 4-path c graphs.
      Red vertical line corresponds to χ = 0.




6.5   Heterogeneous consensus in the general context of MAS
      and complex systems

      Here we consider some examples of the potential applications of the heteroge-
      neous consensus dynamical system. We start by considering a case of MAS ren-
      dezvous in which a group of agents with initial positions on a unit square are
      directed towards given final positions. In the “classical” rendezvous task, based
      on the linear consensus protocol, the MAS must direct towards a single point in
      the square. Here we consider the case in which the positions of groups of agents is
      different from each other, i.e., we want to separate the MAS into subgroups. The
      second example is based on the connections existing between the current model
      and the Bass model for the diffusion of innovations in a social group. Our main
      goal is to show how the selective use of the parameter of the current model can
      emulate different realistic scenarios in the adoption of an innovation. Finally we
      study the relations between the current model and the Lotka-Volterra equations
      to study the interactions between plant-pollinators in a real-world ecosystem,
      where again we are interested in showing the possibilities of the model to sim-
      ulate several realistic scenarios. We consider these examples as toy models used
      to illustrate how to proceed on the study of realistic scenarios based on real data.
      Therefore, our goal here is to show some situations that may arise in the analysis
      of real-world systems and explain their connections with the parameters of the
      current model.
        152                 Chapter 6. The non-conservative logistic growth model

6.5.1   Heterogeneous rendezvous
        As we have seen in the introduction of this chapter, the consensus protocol is
        widely used in several types of engineering tasks. In the standard consensus
        dynamics the steady state of the system consists on the equilibrium point in which
        all the vertices have the same state independently on the initial condition. Let us
        consider here a rendezvous consensus in the plane, in which we start from initial
        position of the vertices of a graph and describe their trajectories as t → ∞. Here
        we will consider the graph illustrated in Fig. 6.3(a) as an illustration. For all the
        experiments we will consider the same initial conditions to allow a comparative
        analysis between the different cases. Thus, we study the models

                                       u̇i (t) =       −Lij uj (t),                       (6.20)
                                                   j

        with L being the standard Laplacian, and

                                                       X
                              u̇i (t) = (1 − ui (t))        (−Lχ )ij uj (t),              (6.21)
                                                        j


        with ui ∈ R2 in both cases, which represent the x, y coordinates of the vertex i in
        the plane. The initial condition u0i ∈ R2 represents here random initial positions
        of the vertices in the plane.
            The results of the standard rendezvous dynamics are illustrated in Fig. 6.7
        where it is observed that the position of all vertices converge to the position which
        corresponds to the average of the initial positions in x and y coordinates.

■ Example 6.3 We now consider a simple manipulation that allow us to direct
the vertices of a graph to different positions in the unit plane in spite of their
degrees or other topological features. For instance, let us consider again the graph
in Fig. 6.3(a), but in which we want to distribute the vertices in the unit square
into 6 groups: {1} , {2, 5} , {3} , {4} , {6} , {7}. For that we simply add an anchor
vertex va which is connected to every other vertex in the graph through weighted
edges. This node will have fixed state va = 1, so it acts like a control of the system.
The weights of these edges will change the effective degree of the corresponding
vertices. For instance, let us use a weight of 6 for the edge (a, 1), the effective
degree of vertex 1 is now k̃1 = 9. We continue by adding the following weights:
5 to (a, 7) , 3 (a, 6), 2 (a, 4) and 1 (a, 3) as well as to (a, 5), respectively. The pair

while vertex 6 is only connected to vertex 3 which has effective degree of 3. Let

be seen in Fig. 6.9 the steady state of the process indicates the existence of the
six groups of vertices separated at different positions of the unit square. This
example illustrates how we can manipulate the graph to obtain different final
states in an heterogeneous rendezvous process with many potential applications
in the sciences and engineering. For instance, the coordination of robotic swarms
has been a field of study for several years, due to the proliferation of autonomous
systems [223]. Recent efforts have been directed to controlling the final shape of
a robotic formation using different kinds of Laplacians [180, 276], which is similar
to the results shown in this example.
                                                                                    ■



    It must be noticed that the complexity of both models (the standard ren-
dezvous and the model presented here) are similar, as model 6.7 only involves
performing two simple arithmetic operations more per agent. Moreover, both
can be used even in situations where a great number of agents have to be coor-
dinated, which is a common scenario in nowadays study of complex systems. As
each agent only uses the position of its nearest neighbors, so there is no need for
them to store the information about the whole network topology. A more difficult
value to obtain when the number of nodes is large is λ1 , which would be feasible
to approximate through simulations of the model, as it would be the minimum
value of the parameter χ for which all the agents converge to 0. However, excel-
lent numerical linear algebra methods exist to obtain good approximations of the
spectral radius of a matrix [224], which can be exploited for this purpose here.
    On the other hand, compared to the standard rendezvous, the current model
allows for a wider range of final positions. While the first one guides all nodes to
        156                Chapter 6. The non-conservative logistic growth model




        Figure 6.9: Illustration of the trajectories of vertices of the graph illustrated in
        Fig. 6.3 using an anchor vertex which is connected to the vertices of the graph
        using edges with weights (6, 0, 1, 2, 1, 3, 5), respectively.


        the mean initial state (see Fig. 6.7, the heterogeneous consensus dynamics guides
        nodes in the network to different positions, both in the plane and between them
        (see Fig. 6.8), like in the leader-follower consensus model [170] but without the
        necessity of introducing leaders in the system, or like in a recent model [180],
        which needs to manipulate a complex Laplacian. Nonetheless, the current model
        is simpler, both conceptually and computationally, needing only the adjacency
        relations among the agents and the external parameter χ, and operating always
        with real values.

6.5.2   Connections with the Bass model
        Bass model [25] was firstly proposed in 1969 to account for the number of the
        adopters F of a certain innovation in a population along time. The differential
        equation of this model reads as:


                           Ḟ (t) = p (1 − F (t)) + qF (t) (1 − F (t))
                                                                                      (6.22)
                                 = p − (p − q) F (t) − qF 2 (t) ,

        where p is a parameter called innovation term or innovation coefficient, by which
        new people adopt the innovation independently of time, and q is the imitation
        term, which stands for the rate at which people adopts the innovation when en-
6.5 Heterogeneous consensus in the general context of MAS and complex
systems                                                                        157
countering an already adopter person.
Proposition 6.5 The heterogeneous consensus dynamical model 6.5 can be inter-
preted as a Bass model 6.22 with parameters p =   uj and q = −χ
                                                P
                                                             j∈Ni

Proof. From our previous logistic equation, we can rewrite the equation for each
node i as:
                                       X
                   u̇i (t) = (1 − ui )   uj − χ(1 − ui )ui
                                        j∈Ni

                                                                             (6.23)
                              X               X
                          =          uj − (             uj + χ)ui + χu2i
                              j∈Ni            j∈Ni

                          = p − (p − q)ui − qu2i .

                                                                             q.e.d.

    Although this equation would mean a reinterpretation of such model, this
variables may not mean exactly the same as in the original model.
    First, the equation would model not the number of adopters in a single pop-
ulation but in many of them, whose inter relations are given by the graph edges.
Let us then interpret the Bass model as a special case of epidemic spreading where
a susceptible population (S) can get “infected” by a given innovation. Then, the
values of u represent the probability of infection (adopting the innovation) of
different individuals in a social network, following the rules:
                                             P
                                       p=          uj
                                            j∈Ni
                                     S −−−−−−→ I;                            (6.24)
                                              q=χ
                                     S + I −−→ 2S,

and preserving the quantity S + I = 1.
    Second, the term p is not a fixed parameter but depends on the probability of
adoption of its neighbors uj . Hence, the innovation parameter would be in fact
the amount of peers’ influence received by node i. Third, the term q could take
negative values. These negative values are the most interesting ones as they mean
that the probability of adopting an innovation can be self-inhibitory. Indeed, neg-
ative values of q represent a negative word-of-mouth (WOM) [130], so, instead
of new people adopting the innovation because some already have adopted it, it
means that adopters tend to reject the already used innovation when they en-
counter someone not using that same novelty.
158                 Chapter 6. The non-conservative logistic growth model

    Thus, our results would show that, for χ ≤ kmin , the WOM effect, even
if negative, would not prevent the innovation to spread fully in all populations
(nodes). Therefore, when kmin < χ < λ1 , the least connected populations will
start showing groups of people not adopting the innovation. The critical point
χ = λ1 will be the “amount” of negative WOM at which a variety of different
scenarios may appear, but the ranking of population according to their percent-
age of adopters would be given by the eigenvector centrality. Finally, for χ > λ1 ,
innovations die out if not fully established in any of the populations. Analyzing
the evolution over time for these values of χ we can see that some percentages
of adopters may start increasing and then die out, which would reflect this effect
of negative WOM, as adopters start increasing by the effect of neighboring pop-
ulations but we can interpret that innovation is so unacceptable (χ large enough)
that the same adopters start refusing it.


■   Example 6.4 Let us consider an example based on the results of an experiment
known as the Columbia University Drug Study [186]. In this study researchers
collected data on the first subscription of the drug “gammanym” by 31 physi-
cians in several medical establishments. Then we use here the network formed 31
vertices representing doctors which are interconnected by 67 edges representing
face-to-face friendship relations, which were obtained by asking them to name
three doctors whom they considered to be personal friends. The graph in question
displays a spectral radius of λ1 ≈ 5.7751, ∆ = 10 and δ = 1. In the real-world
experiment 17 doctors adopted the innovation after a period of 18 months (with
the first ones adopting it in 3 months), and 14 physicians never adopted the in-
novation. Obviously, the standard consensus dynamics will assume the adoption
of the innovation of all the members of the network as it has been confirmed in
previous studies [96]. Recently, we have performed social experiments showing
that not always the totality of individuals adopt an innovation, although advan-
tageous for them, due to the existence of stubborn individuals [196]. Therefore,
to emulate a situation in which part of the individuals adopt the innovation and
others do not, we consider the parameter χ = 5.37, which is smaller that λ1 and
larger than δ. As can be seen in Fig. Fig. 6.10 (a) there are several individuals that
adopted the innovation or which has a high probability of adopting it after a rela-
tively large time. However, others drop continuously their probability of adoption
which will finally ends up with no adoption. The current model is able to simu-
late several types of scenarios and we emulate now the hypothetical situation in
which a negative WOM is present in the system. For that purpose we use here
        6.5 Heterogeneous consensus in the general context of MAS and complex
        systems                                                                          159
        the heterogeneous consensus dynamical model with parameter 6.43 = χ > λ1 ,
        which represents a situation in which an innovation is spread across the network
        with strong negative WOM. For relatively small t, e.g., t ≤ 100 in Fig. 6.10 (b) we
        observe that the majority of individuals start to get discouraged of adopting the
        innovation, and so their probability of adopting the new drug drops continuously
        since the beginning of the process. However, some individuals start to increase
        the probability of adopting the innovation, reaching a peak, after in which it starts
        to drop. However, as can be seen in Fig. 6.10 (b) the probability of adoption of all
        individuals in the network finally dies out as a consequence of the strong negative
        WOM impossed to the system.




                         (a)                                   (b)

        Figure 6.10: (a) Evolution of the adoption of an innovation in the network
        of physicians studied here. In this case we selected a parameter δ = 1 < χ =
        λ1 ≈ 5.7751 which allows that some individuals adopt the innovation while
        others do not. (b) Time evolution of the diffusion of an innovation with large
        negative WOM (χ = 6.28). In this case the innovation is finally not adopted
        by the population. In both cases, the x-axis represents time, the y-axis the is
        the percentage of adoption and the colors represent different individuals.

6.5.3   Connections with the Lotka-Volterra model
        Lotka-Volterra model [175, 255] accounts for a variety of differential equations
        that model the evolution of groups of species that interact in different ways (mu-
        tualism, competition, predator/prey, etc). The generalized Lotka-Volterra model
160                 Chapter 6. The non-conservative logistic growth model

in vectorial form is given by:

                             ẋ(t) = x (t) ⊙ (r + M x (t)) ;                          (6.25)

where each entry of x represents the percentage of an species present in an
ecosystem with respect to the full capacity of that species there. The vector r
stands for the growth of each of the species on its own, while the matrix M en-
closes the relations between different species (including the internal competitions
between themselves).
    Recently, Mooij et al. [198] use this model by fixing r = 1 and M = − (I + τ A),
to give the analytical points at which ecosystems with a large number of species
may survive. This approach gives precise values to the connectance (the measure
of how much the species interact in the ecosystem) that was proposed by May as
early as in 1972 [183] to solve this same question.
    We now prove that the heterogeneous consensus dynamical model can de-
scribe the same kind of information as the model of Mooij et al. [198] after some
transformations.

Proposition 6.6 Consider the generalized Lotka-Volterra 6.25 equation with r =
χ k−1 and M = χ A−I. Then, the heterogeneous consensus dynamical model 6.5

can be rearranged to that same equation.



Proof. In the heterogeneous consensus dynamical model instead of accounting
for the saturation of the species, i.e., the percentage of living animal with respect
to the maximum value that the ecosystem can hold, we account for the emptyness
of that species. Let us then rearrange the model 6.5 by changing variables vi =
1 − ui , hence obtaining for each node the equation:

■   Example 6.5 In this case we consider a small random network formed by 10
species having 38 connections among them. The network has λ1 ≈ 7.67, ∆ = 9
and δ = 6. We then consider that in this ecosystem species collaborate with a
rate χ1 with respect to a self-competitive interaction, which is set to a value of
1, and a growth rate of 1 − kχi . In this example we will consider the value of
χ = 8.34. In Fig. 6.11 (a) it is plotted the mean percentage of species survival,
where the red line corresponds to λ1 , so all the non-zero values to the left of
it represents “isolated” species surviving only due to their positive growth rate,
while the green line represents the value of ∆. Thus, the mean percentage of
survival observed between the green and red lines is equal to one, and represents
the survival and saturation of species with negative growth rate. Note that, while
the mutualism decreases as χ increases, the intrinsic growth increases. Moreover,
this growth is negative if χ < ki , which explains why all species extinguish if χ <
kmin . There are two interesting conclusions that we can observe from applying
our results to this model interpretation. First is that, as all species survive and
saturate their limits when χ > λ1 , this mutualist interactions allow for species
with negative grow (which alone tend to die) to survive in the ecosystem (see
Fig.6.11(b)) . This always occurs in the range χ ∈ (λ1 , kmax ), which has positive
measure for any network apart from the degree-regular ones. Second, if some
specie is not present in the ecosystem (initial condition vi (0) = 0), then the whole
ecosystem gets affected and it’s needed a very high value of χ (big self-grow and
small interactions) for the rest of species to reach their saturation point. This is,
when one piece is missing in the ecosystem, it is usually better for the individuals
to focus on self-grow rather than in their inter-relationships even though they are
positive and while there is a term of self-competition hindering the increase of
each specie.
162                 Chapter 6. The non-conservative logistic growth model




                     (a)                                  (b)

Figure 6.11: (a) Mean percentage of survival in the random network of mutu-
alistic interacting species for different values of χ. (b) Percentage of saturation
over time of different species mutualistically interacting in the random graph
studied in this example. Each color represents one specie, where the solid lines
represent species with positive growth rate while the dashed ones represent
those with negative growth rate.

■   Example 6.6 We now consider a real-world example consisting of plant–pollinator
interactions in native forest sites with and without domestic cattle from which [253]
constructed 8 plant–pollinator interaction networks in four grazed and four un-
grazed sites in native Nothofagus dombeyi forest in and around Nahuel Huapi
National Park, Argentina. Here we use the aggregated data set grouping the in-
teractions among 144 species in the 8 site locations, which is available in the
database [210]. The original data represents a bipartite network G = (V1 , V2 , E)
in which V1 is formed by plants and V2 is formed by pollinators, such that edges
connect pollinators to the plants they pollinate. Therefore, we consider the V2 , V2
projection of the original network. That is, the network contains 144 pollinators
and two pollinators are connected if and only if they pollinate at least one plant
in common. The largest connected component of this network has 90 vertices
and 1828 edges, indicating it is a very dense network. For the selection of the pa-
rameters of the model we consider that the interactions between pollinators are
mutualistic, meaning that if two species pollinate the same plant this increases
the chances of that plant of getting reproduced and increasing the chances of
      6.6 Conclusions                                                                    163

      both species to find food. We do not consider the case in which the food is not
      abundant and the interaction between the pollinators get competitive. Here we
      consider the following three scenarios: (i) a random initial saturation including all
      species in the network, (ii) the same random initial saturation as in (i) but consid-
      ering that the two most connected species are extinct, i.e., uk (0) = 0 for k being
      the two most connected vertices, and (iii) the same random initial saturation as in
      (i) but considering that the two least connected pollinators are extinct. We start
      the simulations with very low population of all the species, i.e., ui (0) ≪ 1 for
      all i ∈ V . In Fig. 6.12 we illustrate the results corresponding to the three cases
      studied: (i) continuous line, (ii) broken line, and (iii) dotted line. When all species
      are interacting in the ecosystem (case (i)) we observe the typical discontinuous
      transition at the value ofχ = λ1 , where all the species reach their maximum
      population size, ui (∞) = 1, for all i ∈ V . However, when the most connected
      species of the ecosystem is extinct (case (ii)), we observe a continuous transition
      beyond the point χ = λ1 , instead of the abrupt one previously observed. This
      means that the system needs to increase the value of the parameter χ to reach
      the maximum saturation of the species population, in spite of the fact that it has
      not changed neither λ1 nor ∆. More interesting is the case (iii), where not only
      the smooth transition to saturation is observed as in the case (ii), but here the
      system needs even larger values of χ to reach its species saturation. The reason
      is that the reproduction rate of each species is given by 1 − ki /χ. Then, because
      this network has λ1 ≈ 51.6838 we observe that the reproduction rate of the most
      connected species at χ = λ1 is 1 − 83/51.6338 ≈ −0.6059, while for the least
      connected one it is 1 − 2/51.6338 ≈ 0.9613. That is, while the population of
      the most connected species is reproducing with a negative rate at this value of χ,
      the one of the least connected species is reproducing significantly. That is, they
      are the ones that grow the most, independently of their interactions with the rest
      of species in the network. Therefore, the effect of the least connected species is
      very large when they are extinct due to the fact that they are the ones that mainly
      push the system towards saturation.
                                                                                           ■




6.6   Conclusions
      Here we have developed and studied an heterogeneous consensus dynamical
      model which, despite its simplicity, gives rise to nonconservative, heterogeneous
      but bounded steady states, depending only on the network topology and one con-
164                Chapter 6. The non-conservative logistic growth model




Figure 6.12: Stable states of the example ecosystem: with random initial con-
ditions (continuous blue line), when the maximally connected specie is extinct
since the beginning (broken red line), and when the two less connected species
are not present (dotted green line). The vertical red line corresponds to the
spectral radius of the adjacency matrix and the continuous vertical green line
to the largest degree.


trol parameter. We have shown also the applicability of such model in different
real life MAS: the heterogeneous spatial consensus of agents, the split of social
groups into different opinion groups when an innovation is being adopted and
the evolution of interactions between plant-pollinators in an ecosystem.
    The results presented here are only valid for simple graphs, although they
might be easily extended to weighted graphs. Further study would be needed for
the case of directed graphs, for which the results should be similar but may need
a more careful approach. This is due to the difference between considering in or
out-degrees and the way these values affect our model. An alternative approach
could be the use of Hermitian Laplacian matrices, sometime called the magnetic
Laplacian (see [88] and references therein). Nonetheless, advances in the study
of this model would possibly result in a better applicability to areas like:: News
diffusing through social media under an external influence, which is modeled by
χ; in the nonconervative volume transmission [242] in the brain, where neuro-
transmitters are expelled to the intracellular space and absorbed at presynaptic
spaces; etc.
    7. Advection in ecological networks




                                                 Quién más quién menos buscó,
                                                 alguna vez
                                                 Bien en su niñez, bien en su
                                                 vejez
                                                 Bien por timidez o miedo al
                                                 veneno
                                                 A la rigidez del juicio ajeno
                                                 Un corazón bueno, un hombro
                                                 imparcial
                                                     Sereno - ZOO ft. SFDK



     Advection/diffusion processes appear very frequently in many geological, bi-
ological and man-made systems [140]. This kind of processes are characterized by
having a quantity that tends to expand evenly in all directions (diffusion) while
it is affected by an external force, that drifts the expansion of that quantity in
some preferred directions (advection). In the case of ecological systems, for in-
stance, such advective phenomenon describes motion of species towards good
resources like nutrients or against noxious stimuli like predators. This combina-
tion of advective and diffusive motions could ends up with species moving across
the whole system with higher concentrations around the nutrients and/or lower
concentrations near the predators.
166                            Chapter 7. Advection in ecological networks

    In geology, for instance, when there is a layered permeable system with flow
predominately parallel to the bedding, the transport of solute through the more
permeable layers is dominated by advection, while in less permeable layers molec-
ular diffusion controls the transport [115]. Similar processes governs also other
geological and man-made systems such as transport in rivers and drainage net-
works [20].
    In biological systems, including fungi, organs, vascular networks and plants,
the transport of resources for metabolism and growth is frequently carried by ad-
vective and diffusive processes through different kinds of transport networks [27,
45, 117, 118, 149, 184, 226, 266]. Advection-diffusion is also fundamental for un-
derstanding the growing processes of tumours where nutrients and oxygen in the
microenvironment are transported by these mechanisms to support tumour cells
and stimulates their proliferation and migration [160, 227, 233].
    Another scenario in which advection-diffusion plays a fundamental role is in
animal foraging and dispersal [65]. Many animals use combined local diffusion
with longer range advective motions to change from unfavorable parts of their
environment to more suitable areas [127]. The advective motion can emerge ei-
ther from the behavior of individuals or from physical transport processes, such
as winds, currents in rivers, and so on. The combined advection-diffusion strat-
egy of foraging has been shown to be advantageous for species [99]. That is,
foraging strategy is improved when animals change their behaviour from diffu-
sion to advection-diffusion and back depending on their surrounding space [48].
Moreover, it has been shown that populations using strategies with no advection
or high advection can be invaded by other populations [47]. Therefore, according
to these results strategies with no advection or high advection would be predicted
to be unstable relative to evolution.
    A common characteristic of many of the complex systems where advection-
diffusion is ubiquitous is that they have a networked nature. That is, in many
of these systems there are discrete entities which are interconnected to each
other forming a complex network/graph. They range from molecular and cel-
lular networks to landscape networks (see [81] for many examples). In the case
of networks the studies about advection-diffusion have mainly focused on di-
rected graphs. In these studies the advection is created by the directionality of
the edges in the graph, which is accounted for a divergence operator giving rise
to an advection Laplacian operator [54, 55, 56, 57, 146]. The problem is that many
complex networks are undirected. For instance, in a landscape network formed
by regions of a landscape connected by corridors, there is no sign indicating pro-
      7.1 Advection-diffusion equation                                                      167

      hibited directions. However, in many real-life situations the own characteristics
      of the nodes may represent a preference of movement towards these points. Thus,
      the advection operators defined in Chapter 3.3 are a very suitable tool to model
      these cases. In this chapter we will show how to combine these operators with
      diffusion operators to create advection-diffusion processes on undirected graphs,
      following the same ideas in the article [192], to provide a new tool for the study
      of advection-diffusion processes in undirected networks. Moreover, we will show
      how this advection affects different networks in both a quantitative and qualita-
      tive way, focusing finally on the application of these processes to the study of
      animal foraging in landscape networks.

7.1   Advection-diffusion equation
      For application to real-world problems, advection appears as a current leading a
      particle which is diffusing at the same time. For this reason we couple both phe-
      nomena into a degree-biased advection-diffusion equation on undirected graphs.

        Definition 7.1 We define the advection-diffusion equation as the differential
        equation:
                        u′ (t) = −(γadv L∗α + γdif L)u(t);        u(0) = u0 ,             (7.1)

        where u0 is an initial condition, L a diffusion operator, which we will take as

7.1 Advection-diffusion equation                                                       171

      The main goal of obtaining this result is not for the calculation of the corre-
sponding eigenvector vi , for which many excellent numerical methods exist. Our
main goal is to use it in terms its structural interpretation in order to establish
structure-dynamics relations.
      For that purpose, let us further explain what is the meaning of this result. Let
us consider a node i in the bidirected graph G↔ created from a tree graph G as
described before. A directed walk on G↔ is a sequence of edges (e1 , e2 , . . . , eh−1 )
for which there is a sequence of nodes (v1 , v2 , . . . , vh ) such that if ek and ek+1 are
consecutive in the sequence, then ek = (vr , vs ) and ek+1 = (vs , vl ). A directed
path is such a directed walk where all edges and vertices are distinct. A directed
path departing from the node i is a directed path in which v1 = vi . Let el be
an edge in a directed path departing from the node i, and let wl be the weight
of this edge created as described before. Hence, equation (7.5) indicates that the
ith component of the eigenvector corresponding to µ1 = 0 is the product of the
weights of all edges in the tree that are at least in one directed path departing
from the node i. Let us consider VSPi to be the sum of the weights of all edges
in at least on directed path departing from the node i, i.e., the volume of the cor-
responding directed tree. Let pl = wl /VSPi be the probability that the quantity
moving across the network via a diffusive-advective process is located at the edge
el . Let us consider that the probabilities pi and pj are independent. Then, the to-
tal probability Pi of finding a quantity that departed from the node i to any other
node via advection-diffusion is given by the product of all the edge probabilities:
Pi = pl , such that vi ∝ Pi . In plain words, the ith component of the eigen-
        Q

vector corresponding to µ1 = 0 is proportional to the probability that a quantity
leaves the node i.
    We are now in position to prove the general expression for this eigenvector
of the degree-biased advection-diffusion operator based on the formula stated
in [54] which is given in the following result.

   R    This result could be actually obtained as an application of the Markov
        chain tree theorem [5]. This Theorem states that the stationary distribu-
        tion of an irreducible Markov chain is given by the sum of the weights
        of all spanning trees directed towards each node. This would give us the
        same result applied to the transition matrix associated to the advection-
        diffusion operator. However, we preferred to give a direct proof of the
        result in order to better understand the structure-dynamics relation that
        this eigenvector represents.

    Knowing this expression for the first eigenvector of the degree-biased advection-
diffusion equation, we can proceed as in Theorem 3.3 to get the dynamics of the
advection-diffusion equation for long times.

 Theorem 7.2 Let G be any connected graph with n nodes, none of them iso-
 lated. Define v as in (7.7). Then, the dynamical system ruled by the advection-
 diffusion equation (7.1) converges towards v for any initial condition u0 as
 t → ∞.

    The convergence of the solution towards the stationary state depends di-
rectly on its second eigenvalue, and we can use simple estimates to show that
this advection-diffusion case converges faster than the pure diffusion or the pure
advection cases separately.

Proposition 7.3 Let (Lα )∗ be the Adjoint biased Laplacian and L be a diffusion
operator. Then, we have the bounds:

                    γadv µ2 ((Lα )∗ ) ≤ µ2 (γadv (Lα )∗ + γdif L);

            As the second eigenvalue rules the convergence of solutions to the stationary
        state for the advection-diffusion, pure advection and pure diffusion operators,
        this result shows that solutions of an advection-diffusion system will converge
        towards its stationary state faster than in the other two cases.

7.2     Computational results
        In this section we perform computational studies that put in practice the concepts
        developed in the previous part of this work. For that purpose, we start by studying
        some artificial graphs which allow us to visualize the main features of the flows
        of items under the new advection-diffusion dynamical system developed here.
        After this warming up, we develop a “proof-of-concept” example of this model in
        a real-life scenario. For this example we selected a landscape network, consisting
        of patches and corridors which allow the movement of a species through a vast
        geographic area. The selection of this example is twofold. On one hand, we
        have empirical data that allow us a “reverse engineering” exercises to estimate the
        parameters of the model. On the other hand, the foraging movement of species
        in a landscape is a classical example in which diffusive and advective motions are
        combined, giving us a realistic scenario to test our model.

7.2.1   System evolution on artificial networks
        We can numerically show that the Theorem 7.1 correctly represent the steady
        state of advective and diffusive-advective systems, respectively. For that purpose,
        we generated an small Erdős-Rényi graph with 8 nodes and 15 vertices [79].
            In Fig. 7.1 we illustrate the structure of this network, where it can be seen
        that although it is very small it contains enough structure–it is far from tree-
        like–as to illustrate the effects of advection and diffusion on a realistic networked
        scenario. On this graph, we solved numerically the dynamical system u̇ = −L̄u
        7.2 Computational results                                                         175


                                       6

                            7
                                                  0
                                                                        4

                                1          3


                                                           2



                                           5



        Figure 7.1: Representation of the structure of the Erdős-Rényi graph that we
        used with 8 nodes and 15 edges, where the size of each node is proportional to
        its degree.


        with random initial conditions for L̄ = 0.5L + 0.5(Lα )∗ and L̄ = (Lα )∗ . In
        Fig.7.2 we plotted the evolution of the solution along time (dashed lines) where
        each color represents a group of nodes with the same degree. At the same time,
        we computed the expressions of the 0 eigenvector v with the formulas given in
        Theorem 7.1. We plotted the values of v in the same figure as horizontal dashed
        lines, whose color represents the degree of the nodes that should approach this
        steady state.
             As we can see in Fig. 7.2, the analytical results perfectly fit the steady state
        of the system in all the cases. Moreover, it shows that the convergence of the
        advection-diffusion system (panels (B) and (D)) is faster than the convergence of
        the fully advective system (panels (A) and (C)), which is a consequence of Propo-
        sition 7.3.

7.2.2   In- and out-flows on networks
        An important problem for the analysis of real-world networks is that, in general,
        we are able to “observe” them at a given fixed time t, or at best at a few fixed times
        t1 , . . . , th . In the case of a dynamics taken place on such networks we are then
        capable to “observe” the internode flow which is taking place only between near-
        est neighbors. Let us think for instance in the case of animals of a given species
176                             Chapter 7. Advection in ecological networks




                  (a)                                    (b)




                  (c)                                    (d)

Figure 7.2: Illustration of the results of simulations in an Erdős-Rényi random
graph G (8, 15) (solid lines) and the analytically calculated steady states (dashed
lines). For the choice α = 1, panel (A) shows the evolution and steady state of
the advection equation while panel (B) represents the same for the advection-
diffusion equation. Panel (C) represents the advection equation when α = −1
and panel (D) shows the results of the advection-diffusion equation for the same
value of α.


moving in a landscape network, which will be analyzed in the next subsection. If
we take a photograph of the landscape at a give time t we will observe the ani-
mals located at every node and those moving between pairs of nearest neighbor
patches. However, for those already in a given patch (node) we can say noth-
ing about their previous states. That is, we cannot say whether they have come
from a nearest neighbor, from a distant node, or even if they have remained at
that patch for their entire life. On the contrary, for those animals at the edges of
the graph we can infer that at a time τ > t they will almost surely arrive at the
nearest neighbor nodes toward which they were moving at time t.
      When we apply our mathematical models to a dynamical process taken place
7.2 Computational results                                                        177

on a graph, supposing that we have a good estimation of the initial conditions, we
are able to obtain a global picture of the movement of “items” across the whole
network. For instance, if our network is p−q−r we can see that up,r (t > 0) > 0
for certain time t. This indicates that some “items” initially located at node p
have moved to node r at time t. But in a network such movement can only be
realized through the edges of the graph (notice there is no edge between p and r),
such that this “item” has had first to go from p to q and then from q to r. In our
static photograph of the system at time t we possibly will observe such “item”
in its transfer from q to r, together with those that has started their travel at the
node q. Thus, what we really observe is the in- and out-flows of items to/from
the nodes coming/going from/to its nearest neighbors.

    The previously described situation prompts us to consider how can we com-
pare the results of our computational experiments with those that can be ob-
served at a given time in a network. Here, of course we consider a process con-
trolled by the advection-diffusion Laplacian L̃ (γadv ) := γadv L∗α + γdif L when
γadv = 1 − γdif . At a giventimet the flow between
                                                      a pair of nodes v and w is
given by (Γ (t, γadv ))vw =    exp −tL̃ (γadv )        . Therefore, the snapshot of
                                                      vw              
the network flow at the time t is obtained by the matrix exp −tL̃ ⊙ A, where
⊙ denotes the entrywise (aka Schur or Hadamard) product of the two matrices.
In the previous example of the network p−q−r, this will always give values only
for the pairs p − q and q − r, and not for p − r, where an edge does not exist.
Consequently, we will focus here on the in- and out-flows of “items” to/from the
nodes of the network. It is important to note here that we are using the standard
graph terminology for the adjacency matrix of directed graphs [265], such that
the (i, j) entry of A indicates that there is an arrow from i to j. Then, the in- and
out flows of our operators are defined, respectively, as


                      f˜in (t, γadv ) := ⃗1T (Γ (t, γadv ) ⊙ A) ,               (7.8)

where ⃗1 is the all-ones column vector. These parameters can be compared with

            1
added to in the leaves (see Eq. (7.12)). Therefore f˜in,i (γadv < 0.5) is similar
            n
to f˜in,i (γadv = 0) as can be seen by the values of the cosine similarity, and more
importantly they are linearly correlated because the hub still concentrates most
of the in-flow, while the leaves have very little in-flow. In this regime, although
γadv > 0, we are still in a scenario mainly dominated by diffusion. However,
when γadv = 0.5 we have: f˜in,hub (γadv = 1/2) = f˜in,leaf (γadv = 1/2) = 1/2,
which means that at this point the in-flow to the hub and to the leaves is ex-
actly the same. From this point on we have an inversion in the in-flow arriving
at the leaves in comparison with the hub. That is, when γadv > 0.5 the in-flow
arriving at the leaves in bigger than that arriving at the hub. Therefore, the simi-
larity between the vectors f˜in (γadv = 0) and f˜in (γadv > 0.5) drops significantly
as observed in Fig. 7.3, and more importantly there is an anticorrelation between
the the two in-flow vectors. This means that we are in a new regime where ad-
vection dominates, pushing the “items” toward the leaves of the star in spite of
the counter-effect of diffusion which tries to push them to the hub.
        180                              Chapter 7. Advection in ecological networks

            The same analysis can be made for the choice α = −1, but the results are
        less interesting to study due to the fact advection pushes the “items” towards the
        hub, thus increasing the effect of diffusion in the in-flow and resulting in a high
        correlation between the f˜in (γadv ) for all values of γadv .

7.2.4   Influence of assortativity
        The example for the star graph that we analyzed in the previous subsection asks
        the question about which structural characteristic of a network is responsible
        for the inversion of the Pearson correlation coefficient of f˜in from mainly dif-
        fusive to mainly advective processes. The star graph is characterized by a high
        degree heterogeneity–one node of degree n − 1 and n − 1 nodes of degree one–
        as well as by a large degree disassortativity–the degree-degree correlation coeffi-
        cient is negative. Intuitively, the degree assortativity seems more relevant for the
        advection-diffusion inversion. The reason is that f˜in takes into account the in-
        flow to a given node from its nearest neighbors. Thus, the kind of degree-degree
        connectivity pattern should make an important impact on the values of f˜in .
            Here we consider Erdős-Rényi random graphs which have low degree het-
        erogeneity, i.e., they have a normal-like Poisson degree distribution. We then
        rewire the edges of these graphs maintaining the node degrees, such that we can
        tune their degree assortativity. The degree assortativity is measured by the assor-
        tativity coefficient, i.e., the Pearson coefficient of the degree-degree correlation,
        see [80, 205]. For every type of graph with a given assortativity we calculate
        the correlation coefficient between the vectors f˜in (γadv = 0) and f˜in (γadv > 0).
        The results, after the average of 10 random realizations per assortativity rewiring,
        are illustrated in Fig. 7.4. As can be seen when γadv is zero or close to zero, i.e., in
        the mainly-diffusive regime, the nodes with the largest degree concentrate most
        of the in-flow, while the low-degree nodes have low values of it. This produces
        high positive correlation coefficients between f˜in (γadv = 0) and f˜in (γadv > 0)
        in this region as observed in Fig. 7.4. Note that, as advection when α = 1 tends to
        concentrate more flow on nodes with lower degree and diffusion tends to concen-
        trate more flow on high degree nodes (as they are the more connected ones), the
        correlation between this in-flows monotonically decreases as γadv . On the other
        hand, the most interesting thing is that this high correlation is irrespective of the
        degree assortativity of the network. By contrast, when γadv > 0.5 we observe
        a large range of correlations between f˜in (γadv = 0) and f˜in (γadv > 0), which
        range from values close to 1 for highly assortative networks to values close to −1
        for highly disassortative networks. That is, when there is a large contribution of
        7.2 Computational results                                                                                              181

        advection in the diffusive-advective process, the degree-degree correlation of the
        network can change the in-flow of items from hubs (in the assortative network) to
        low-degree nodes (in the disassortative network). This transition does not behave
        in the same way as when we are changing the advection parameter. For instance,
        if we fix a large value γadv , we can observe that the correlation is not monotone
        with respect to the assortativity index, which may indicate that other features of
        the network also affect the in-flows. Indeed, what we are reporting here is the ex-
        istence of two completely different kinds of processes represented by exactly the
        same dynamical equation (including its parameters) for both classes of networks,
        i.e., assortative and disassortative ones.

7.2.5   Foraging in a patched landscape
        As described in the introduction of this chapter, advection-diffusion processes
        are ubiquitous in biological, geophysical and technological systems. Here we
        perform the analysis of diffusive-advective dynamics on a landscape network. As
        described below this landscape network represents a good scenario for testing
        advective-diffusive movement of a species through the patches and corridors of
        a geographic region. Therefore, our goal here is to use this landscape network
        as a proof-of-concept for the new operators and dynamical systems developed
182                             Chapter 7. Advection in ecological networks

in the current work on a realistic scenario. Consequently, we are not interested
here in a microscopic analysis of the movement of a species across the landscape.
Instead, we are interested in showing quantitatively how the newly developed
operators capture some important ecological aspects of species movement which
escape to the use of the standard Laplacian operator to model this kind of scenar-
ios. This landscape network is a graph G = (V, E) where the nodes represent
161 small forest patches (with areas between l and 95 ha) that are scattered in
an agricultural landscape in the Androy region, southern Madagascar [39] (see
Fig. 7.5). This landscape network is used by the ring-tailed lemur (Lemur catta)
to move across the environment. We consider here that this species forages at
the patches of the landscape which contain fruits forming the basis of L. catta
feeding. In studying foraging movement of species it is common to consider
the existence of intensive and extensive search modes. Intensive search mode
is typically modeled by diffusion, while movement in the extensive search mode
is modeled by advection (see for instance [99, 127, 237, 250]). Here we assume
that the degree of a patch, which is the number of corridors that connects it to
the rest of patches, is related to the amount of resources that the species needs
for feeding. In general, it is expected that a patch with large area is connected to
many other patches, while a small one does not. It is also plausible that a large
patch is more abundant in food than a small one. A similar strategy was used
in modeling advection-diffusion movement of species by Grünbaum [126] who
considered that the behavior of predators was mediated by its gut fullness, a.k.a.
satiety, which was a function of prey availability. Similarly, Fagan et al. [98] con-
structed a continuum diffusion-advection model in which consumers movement
in a landscape is driven by local advection governed by intermediate-scale re-
source detection functions. Therefore, we consider the study of the movement of
L. catta between patches of this fragmented landscape as an appropriate platform
for applying the advection-diffusion model on networks developed here.
    In the current work, the edges of the landscape network represent the cor-
ridors existing between pairs of patches. These edges were determined in the
following way. First, it was determined that the dispersal flux rate (i.e., rate of
movements) between any two habitat patches depends on: (1) the geographical
distance between the habitat patches and (2) the area of the habitat patches. It
was also determined that the geographical distance has a much stronger impact
than the area on the dispersal flux rate. Therefore, a measure Sij , which is pro-
portional to the dispersal flux rate, was calculated using a negative exponential
dispersal kernel [137]
7.2 Computational results                                                        183




Figure 7.5: Representation of the foraging landscape studied in the article [39]



                                       √
                                Sij = b aj e−βDij                              (7.13)

where aj is the area of the patch j (in m2 ), Dij is the distance between patch i
and patch j (in m), and β and b are constants (in 1/m) determined by [39], such
that the model partly captured the underlying assumptions behind the Incident
Function Model [137]. The distances between all pair of patches were measured
using the Landsat image. The minimum hospitable patch area for Lemur catta
was estimated to be 1 ha [112]. The parameters were set such that only 5% of the
maximum Sij remained between patches of 1 ha, if separated by a distance Dij
equal to the chosen level of vagility. This level of Sij was also set as the cut-off,
i.e., an infinitesimal increase in distance or an infinitesimal decrease in patch j’s
area would disconnect these patches. Thus, pairs of patches with a Sij below the
cut-off were not considered as connected, i.e. no edge connected such pairs of
nodes.
    We can then consider that the weighted adjacency matrix W of the landscape
network is the Hadamard (entrywise) product of the matrix S where the values
of Sij are given for every pair of patches in the landscape and the adjacency
matrix A of a directed weighted graph: W := S ⊙ A. The adjacency matrix
A contains information only about the topology of the system, that is about the
corridors which are used by the ring-tailed lemur (Lemur catta) to move across
the environment. This species is very important for the ecosystem as a local seed
184                              Chapter 7. Advection in ecological networks

disperser since it forages on fruits of many different plant species in the area.
    In the previous section we have shown the relevance of the degree-degree
assortativity on the evolution of the diffusion-advection processes taking place on
a network. Because the landscape network analyzed here is a weighted directed
graph, we consider the assortativity coefficient defined specifically for this class
of networks according to Yuan et al. [268]:

    Here we are interested in how the in-flow of species at a given node is cor-
related with the in-flow at another. Therefore, from the several combinations of
in- and out-assortativity coefficients we are interested in the in-in-assortativity
one. In this case the landscape network is in-in-disassortativity with coefficient
ρ ≈ −0.118 (the rest of assortativity coefficients are all positive ones). This value
shows that in the network there is a certain tendency to nodes with high in-
degree to be connected to nodes with low in-degree. This may give rise to a high
concentration of the flow in low-degree nodes in the case that the hubs-biased
advection with α = 1 is dominant, as shown in Section 7.2.4.
    The in-flow of L. catta into each patch of the landscape can be measured by
fin := ⃗1T W = ⃗1T (S ⊙ A), which is equivalent to the in-strength–the term
strength is used instead of degree in the case of weighted networks–of the weighted
directed landscape network. In Fig. 7.6 we present a scatterplot of the values of
the fin vs. those of kin as well as its best fit. It can be seen that there is an expo-
nential relation between the two variables: fin ≈ 0.6603e0.2224kin . However, it
is evident that kin cannot be used directly to estimate the values of the in-flow.
The in-degree is able to explain only 45% of the variance of fin using the previ-
ous exponential model, i.e., r2 ≈ 0.45. Consequently, we can observe that for
single values of the in-degree there are wide ranges of values of the in-flow. For
7.2 Computational results                                                         185

instance, for kin = 9 there are values of fin ranging from 2 to 25. Thence, the
values of the in-flow cannot be derived directly from information coming purely
from the adjacency matrix of the graph, i.e., by the in-degree.
